↓
New transmission

Ultimate memory:
## Source → Transmission → Entry → Replication → Movement → Symptoms →
TRANSMISSION   
And the most important management principle:
## Prevention + Resistance + Vector Management + Healthy Planting Materia
L   
Virus = prevention is generally more important than cure.

## Module 16 — Plant Disease
## Management
## 7. Nematodes
## 1. Introduction
Nematodes are microscopic, elongated, unsegmented, worm-like animals. Many nematodes are
harmless or beneficial, but some are plant-parasitic nematodes (PPNs) that feed on plant tissues and
cause economically important crop diseases.
Plant-parasitic nematodes can attack:
- Roots
- Root hairs
- Stems
- Bulbs
- Tubers
- Seeds
- Leaves
- Other plant tissues
They are particularly important because root damage can interfere with:
- Water absorption
- Nutrient uptake
- Root growth
- Plant anchorage
- Transport
- Overall crop development
The central concept is:

Plant-parasitic nematode+Susceptible host+Favorable conditions→Nematode damage/
disease parasitic\ nematode + Susceptible\ host + Favorable\ conditions
 Nematode\ damage/disease  

2. Definition of Nematodes
Simple definition
Nematodes are microscopic, elongated, unsegmented, worm-like animals, some of
which parasitize plants and cause crop damage.

Plant pathology definition
Plant-parasitic nematodes are nematode species that feed on living plant tissues using
specialized mouthparts, particularly a stylet, and cause physiological, structural and
economic damage to crops.

3. What Is a Plant-Parasitic Nematode?
Not every nematode is a plant pest.
Nematodes include:
- Free-living nematodes
- Predatory nematodes
- Bacterivorous nematodes
- Fungivorous nematodes
- Omnivorous nematodes
- Plant-parasitic nematodes
- Animal-parasitic nematodes
Therefore:
Nematode≠Plant pathogen automatically 
Plant-parasitic nematodes are the group important in crop disease management.

4. Important Characteristics
Nematodes generally have:
- Bilateral symmetry

- Elongated cylindrical bodies
- No body segmentation
- A complete digestive tract
- Muscular pharynx
- Cuticle
- Nervous system
- Reproductive system
- Specialized structures in plant parasites
Most plant-parasitic nematodes are microscopic and difficult to see individually with the naked eye.

5. Nematode Body Shape
A typical plant-parasitic nematode is:
- Long
- Cylindrical
- Worm-like
- Tapered at one or both ends
Nematode body≈microscopic, unsegmented, cylindrical 

6. Nematodes Are Animals
This is an important classification point.
Nematodes=Animals 
They are not:
- Fungi
- Bacteria
- Viruses
- Insects
This makes nematode diseases biologically different from fungal, bacterial and viral diseases.

7. Nematode Body Structure
A generalized nematode has:
- Cuticle
- Hypodermis
- Longitudinal muscles
- Pseudocoel/body cavity
- Digestive tract
- Nervous system
- Reproductive system
Plant-parasitic species additionally possess specialized feeding structures.

## 8. Cuticle
The outer body covering is called the cuticle.
Functions include:
- Protection
- Maintaining body shape
- Protection from environmental conditions
- Interaction with the host environment
Nematodes periodically shed their cuticle during development.
This process is called:
Molting 

9. Stylet — Most Important Structure
A stylet is a specialized, needle-like feeding structure found in plant-parasitic nematodes.
It is used to:
- Pierce plant cells
- Inject secretions
- Withdraw cell contents

Conceptually:
Stylet→Plant cell penetration→Feeding 
The stylet is one of the most important characteristics distinguishing plant-parasitic nematodes from
many free-living nematodes.

10. How Nematodes Feed
A simplified feeding sequence is:
Locate root→Stylet penetration→Secretion→Cell disruption/modification→Nutrient uptake 
Some nematodes feed on individual cells, while others establish specialized feeding sites within the
plant.

11. Nematode Feeding Types
Plant-parasitic nematodes can be classified based on where they feed.

Ectoparasites
Remain outside the plant tissue while feeding.
Nematode outside root→Stylet penetrates cellsNematode\ outside\ root  Stylet\ penetrates\
cells

Endoparasites
Enter plant tissues and feed internally.
Nematode→Plant tissue→Internal feedingNematode  Plant\ tissue  Internal\
feeding

Migratory endoparasites
Enter plant tissues and move from cell to cell while feeding.

Sedentary endoparasites
Enter the root and establish a relatively fixed feeding site.

Important examples include:
- Root-knot nematodes
- Cyst nematodes

12. Root-Knot Nematodes
The genus Meloidogyne contains economically important root-knot nematodes.
They are among the most important plant-parasitic nematodes.
Their characteristic symptom is:
Root galls 
General process:
Meloidogyne→Root penetration→Feeding site formation→Cell enlargement/division→Root gallMeloi
dogyne  Root\ penetration  Feeding\ site\ formation  Cell\
enlargement/division  Root\ gall

13. Root Galls
Root galls are abnormal swellings caused by nematode-induced changes in root tissues.
They can interfere with:
- Root development
- Water uptake
- Nutrient uptake
- Root transport
Above-ground symptoms may include:
- Stunting
- Yellowing
- Wilting
- Nutrient-deficiency-like symptoms
- Uneven growth

14. Cyst Nematodes
Important cyst nematodes belong to genera such as:
- Heterodera
- Globodera
Females of these nematodes can form resistant cysts containing eggs.
General cycle:
Eggs→Juvenile→Root penetration→Feeding→Female→CystEggs  Juvenile  Root\ penetration  Feeding  Female  Cyst
The cyst can protect eggs in soil for extended periods.

15. Lesion Nematodes
Important lesion nematodes belong to Pratylenchus.
They are migratory endoparasites that can cause:
- Root lesions
- Root necrosis
- Root decay
- Reduced root function
General relationship:
Pratylenchus→Root penetration→Migration→Feeding→LesionsPratylenchus  Root\
penetration  Migration  Feeding  Lesions

16. Burrowing Nematodes
Radopholus species include important burrowing nematodes.
They can invade roots and cause:
- Lesions
- Root decay
- Root weakening
- Reduced plant growth
They can be particularly important in some perennial and plantation crops.

17. Reniform Nematodes
Rotylenchulus includes reniform nematodes.
The mature female develops a characteristic kidney/reniform shape.
They can cause:
- Root damage
- Stunting
- Chlorosis
- Yield reduction

18. Dagger Nematodes
Xiphinema species are important ectoparasitic nematodes.
Some species are also important because they can transmit certain plant viruses.
Thus:
Nematode→Direct root damage+Possible virus transmission 

19. Sting Nematodes
Belonolaimus species are known as sting nematodes.
They can cause serious root damage in susceptible crops.

20. Important Plant-Parasitic Nematode Genera
Nematode group
Important genus
Characteristic association
Root-knot
Meloidogyne
Root galls
Cyst
Heterodera, Globodera Cysts
Lesion
Pratylenchus
Root lesions
Burrowing
Radopholus
Root lesions/decay
Reniform
Rotylenchulus
Reniform female
Dagger
Xiphinema
Root feeding; some transmit viruses
Sting
Belonolaimus
Severe root damage

21. Nematode Life Cycle
A generalized nematode life cycle is:
Egg→Juvenile→Adult→Egg 
Nematodes undergo several juvenile stages.

## 22. Egg
The egg contains the developing embryo.
Depending on the species, eggs may be:
- Laid in soil
- Deposited in egg masses
- Retained in a female/cyst
- Associated with plant tissues
Eggs can serve as important survival stages.

## 23. Juveniles
Nematodes pass through several juvenile stages.
A simplified sequence is:
Egg→J1→J2→J3→J4→AdultEgg  J 1  J 2  J 3  J 4
 Adult
The first juvenile stage often develops within the egg.
The second-stage juvenile (J2) is particularly important in several plant-parasitic nematodes,
especially root-knot and cyst nematodes.

## 24. Adult
Adults reproduce and generate new generations.
Adult nematodes may be:
- Male
- Female

- Hermaphroditic in some species
Sexual systems differ among nematode species.

## 25. Molting
Nematodes grow through successive molts.
J1→J2→J3→J4→Adult 
Each transition involves shedding and replacement/modification of the cuticle.

26. Root-Knot Nematode Life Cycle
A simplified Meloidogyne cycle:
Egg→J2→Root penetration→Feeding site→Development→Adult→Egg massEgg  J 2
 Root\ penetration  Feeding\ site  Development  Adult
 Egg\ mass
The J2 is generally the major infective stage.

27. Nematode Penetration of Roots
A typical sequence is:
Nematode→Root attraction→Penetration→Migration/feeding→Feeding site 
Root exudates can influence nematode behavior and host location.

28. Root Exudates
Roots release chemical substances into the rhizosphere.
These can influence:
- Microorganisms
- Nematodes
- Nutrient cycling
- Root interactions

Plant-parasitic nematodes can respond to chemical and physical signals associated with roots.

29. Nematode Feeding and Feeding Sites
Some nematodes induce specialized feeding sites.
For example, root-knot nematodes induce giant-cell feeding structures.
Cyst nematodes induce specialized syncytial feeding sites.
These feeding structures provide nutrients required for nematode development.

30. Giant Cells
Root-knot nematodes can induce giant cells in roots.
General concept:
Nematode effectors→Host cellular changes→Giant cell formation→Nematode feedingNematode\
effectors  Host\ cellular\ changes  Giant\ cell\ formation  Nematode\
feeding
These are metabolically active feeding cells.

## 31. Syncytium
Cyst nematodes can induce a syncytium, a specialized feeding structure formed through modification
and fusion-related changes among root cells.
Nematode→Host cell modification→Syncytium→FeedingNematode  Host\ cell\
modification  Syncytium  Feeding

32. Nematode Damage to Roots
Nematodes can cause:
- Root wounds
- Root lesions
- Root galling
- Root pruning
- Reduced root branching
- Reduced root hairs

- Feeding-site formation
- Tissue necrosis
Consequently:
Root damage→Water/nutrient uptake↓→Plant growth↓Root\ damage  Water/nutrient\
uptake  Plant\ growth 33. Above-Ground Symptoms
Although nematodes often attack roots, symptoms may appear above ground.
These include:
- Stunting
- Yellowing
- Wilting
- Poor growth
- Nutrient-deficiency-like symptoms
- Uneven crop stand
- Premature senescence
- Reduced yield
This can make diagnosis difficult.

34. Nematode Disease vs Nutrient Deficiency
Nematode damage can resemble nutrient deficiency because damaged roots cannot efficiently acquire
nutrients.
Therefore:
Nematode root damage→Nutrient uptake↓→Yellowing/stuntingNematode\ root\ damage  Nutrient\ uptake  Yellowing/stunting
So:
Yellowing does not automatically mean nutrient deficiency. 

35. Nematode Damage and Water Stress
Damaged roots have reduced ability to absorb water.
Therefore:
Root damage→Water uptake↓→Water stress→WiltingRoot\ damage  Water\
uptake  Water\ stress  Wilting
This can be particularly visible under hot or dry conditions.

36. Nematodes as Disease Complexes
Nematodes can interact with other pathogens.
For example:
Nematode root damage→Root wounds→Pathogen entry→Disease complexNematode\ root\ damage
 Root\ wounds  Pathogen\ entry  Disease\ complex
They may interact with:
- Fungi
- Bacteria
- Other soil-borne pathogens
This is called a disease complex when multiple agents contribute to disease.

37. Nematode–Fungus Interaction
A nematode can predispose roots to fungal infection.
Conceptually:
Nematode→Root injury→Fungal infection→Disease severity↑Nematode  Root\ injury
 Fungal\ infection  Disease\ severity The interaction is pathogen- and crop-specific.

38. Nematode–Bacteria Interaction
Similarly:
Nematode damage→Root tissue disruption→Bacterial infection opportunityNematode\ damage
 Root\ tissue\ disruption  Bacterial\ infection\ opportunity

Again, this depends on the specific organisms involved.

39. Nematodes as Virus Vectors
Some nematodes can transmit plant viruses.
Important genera include certain:
- Xiphinema
- Longidorus
- Trichodorus
- Paratrichodorus
Thus:
Nematode→Virus acquisition→Transmission→Plant infection 
This means nematodes can cause damage both directly and indirectly.

40. Direct and Indirect Damage
Direct damage
Nematode feeds directly on plant tissue.
Nematode feeding→Root/tissue damageNematode\ feeding  Root/tissue\ damage

Indirect damage
Nematode:
- Transmits viruses
- Creates wounds
- Alters root physiology
- Facilitates secondary pathogens
Nematode→Secondary effect→Disease increaseNematode  Secondary\ effect  Disease\ increase

41. Nematode Survival
Nematodes can survive in:

- Soil
- Plant roots
- Crop residues
- Seeds or planting material in some cases
- Cysts
- Eggs
- Dormant developmental stages
- Weeds
- Volunteer plants
Survival structures and duration vary greatly by species.

## 42. Cysts
Cysts are particularly important for cyst nematodes.
A female body can become a resistant structure containing eggs.
Female→Eggs→Cyst→Survival→Juvenile emergenceFemale  Eggs  Cyst
 Survival  Juvenile\ emergence
Cysts can make eradication difficult.

43. Nematode Dissemination
Nematodes can spread through:
- Soil movement
- Water
- Irrigation
- Flooding
- Farm machinery
- Tools
- Shoes
- Animals
- Infected planting material

- Nursery plants
- Soil attached to roots/tubers
The movement of contaminated soil is particularly important.
Contaminated soil→New field→Nematode introduction 

44. Soil as the Major Habitat
Many plant-parasitic nematodes spend a significant part of their life cycle in soil.
Therefore:
Soil health+Soil sampling 
are particularly important for nematode management.

45. Nematode Population Density
Nematode populations may be measured as:
Nematodes/unit soil 
For example:
- Nematodes per 100 g soil
- Nematodes per 200 g soil
- Nematodes per root sample
depending on the sampling protocol.

46. Nematode Sampling
Sampling is essential because nematodes are usually microscopic.
A general procedure is:
Representative sampling→Laboratory extraction→Identification→Population estimation→Manageme
nt decision 

47. Soil Sampling
Collect representative soil samples from:

- Root zone
- Rhizosphere
- Areas with symptoms
- Random/zig-zag locations
- Different management zones when needed
Avoid taking only one sample from an unusual spot.

48. Root Sampling
Roots should also be examined when root-knot or other root-parasitic nematodes are suspected.
Look for:
- Galls
- Lesions
- Root pruning
- Necrosis
- Cysts
- Poor root development

49. Nematode Extraction
Laboratories may use methods such as:
- Sieving
- Centrifugal flotation
- Baermann funnel
- Mist chamber
- Decanting and sieving
- Other specialized extraction methods
The appropriate method depends on nematode type and sample.

50. Nematode Identification
Identification can use:

Morphology
- Body shape
- Stylet structure
- Esophagus/pharynx
- Tail shape
- Vulva position
- Male structures
- Juvenile characteristics

Molecular methods
- PCR
- DNA sequencing
- Species-specific molecular assays
Modern diagnosis often combines morphology and molecular methods.

51. Nematode Diagnosis
A reliable diagnosis should combine:
Crop+Above-ground symptoms+Root symptoms+Soil/root nematode sampling+Population estimation
+Identification ground\ symptoms + Root\ symptoms + Soil/root\
nematode\ sampling + Population\ estimation + Identification  
Do not diagnose nematode disease solely from stunting or yellowing.

52. Disease Triangle for Nematodes
The disease triangle can be applied as:
Susceptible Host+Plant-parasitic Nematode+Favorable Environment→Nematode disease/
damage parasitic\ Nematode + Favorable\ Environment
 Nematode\ disease/damage  
Important factors include:
- Soil temperature
- Soil moisture
- Host availability

- Soil texture
- Organic matter
- Nematode population
- Crop stage

53. Soil Temperature
Temperature influences:
- Egg development
- Juvenile activity
- Movement
- Reproduction
- Host infection
Therefore:
Temperature→Nematode development→Disease riskTemperature  Nematode\ development
 Disease\ risk
The optimum differs among species.

54. Soil Moisture
Moisture influences nematode movement through soil pores.
Too little water can restrict movement; excessive water can also alter oxygen availability and nematode
activity.
Therefore the relationship is not simply:
More water=More nematodesMore\ water=More\ nematodes
It is species- and soil-dependent.

55. Soil Texture
Soil texture affects:
- Pore space
- Water movement

- Nematode movement
- Root development
- Soil aeration
Fine and coarse soils can support different nematode populations depending on species and conditions.

56. Nematode Epidemiology
Nematode disease development depends on:
Host+Nematode+Environment+Time 
Population growth is affected by:
- Initial nematode density
- Reproduction
- Crop susceptibility
- Soil conditions
- Temperature
- Moisture
- Crop rotation
- Host availability

57. Initial Nematode Population
Initial population density can strongly influence disease.
Conceptually:
Initial population↑→Root invasion opportunity↑→Damage potential↑Initial\ population  Root\ invasion\ opportunity  Damage\ potential This is why pre-plant soil testing can be useful in known nematode problem areas.

58. Nematode Management
Nematode management should use integrated approaches.
INM-like integration=Cultural+Biological+Chemical+Host resistance+Physical+Sanitation  = Cultural+Biological+Chemical+Host\ resistance+Physical+Sanitation  

More specifically:
## 1. Prevention/exclusion
2. Resistant varieties
3. Crop rotation
4. Soil solarization
5. Organic amendments
6. Biological control
7. Trap/cover crops where appropriate
## 8. Sanitation
9. Chemical nematicides where registered
10.Integrated nematode management

## 59. Prevention
Prevent introduction through:
- Clean planting material
- Clean nursery soil
- Avoiding contaminated soil movement
- Machinery sanitation
- Clean irrigation water where relevant
- Quarantine
The goal is:
Introduction↓→Population establishment↓Introduction  Population\
establishment 60. Crop Rotation
Crop rotation is one of the important cultural methods.
General principle:
Susceptible crop→Non−host/resistant crop→Nematode reproduction↓Susceptible\ crop  Non-host/resistant\ crop  Nematode\ reproduction However, the rotation crop must actually be a poor host for the target nematode.

A crop that looks unrelated botanically may still host a particular nematode.

61. Resistant Varieties
Resistant cultivars can reduce:
- Nematode reproduction
- Feeding
- Population buildup
- Root damage
Resistance→Nematode reproduction↓→Population buildup↓ 
Resistance is often one of the most sustainable approaches.

62. Resistant vs Tolerant
Resistance
Plant limits nematode infection, development or reproduction.
Nematode reproduction↓Nematode\ reproduction Tolerance
Plant can maintain growth/yield despite nematode infection.
Damage per nematode↓Damage\ per\ nematode Therefore:
Resistance≠Tolerance 

63. Soil Solarization
Solarization involves covering moist soil with transparent plastic during a period of strong solar
heating.
General mechanism:
Solar radiation→Soil temperature↑→Pathogens/nematodes suppressedSolar\ radiation  Soil\
temperature  Pathogens/nematodes\ suppressed
Effectiveness depends on:
- Solar radiation

- Soil moisture
- Duration
- Plastic properties
- Soil depth
- Temperature achieved

64. Organic Amendments
Organic materials can influence nematodes through:
- Improved soil biological activity
- Release of toxic/intermediate compounds during decomposition
- Increased antagonistic microorganisms
- Improved plant vigor
- Changes in soil physical/chemical conditions
Examples:
- Compost
- Well-decomposed manure
- Certain oil cakes
- Crop residues
However:
Organic amendment≠Guaranteed nematode eliminationOrganic\ amendment Guaranteed\
nematode\ elimination
Effectiveness depends on material, rate, nematode and environment.

65. Biological Control
Biological control uses organisms that suppress plant-parasitic nematodes.
Potential agents include:
- Purpureocillium lilacinum
- Pochonia chlamydosporia
- Certain Trichoderma

- Predatory/fungal antagonists
- Other beneficial microorganisms
Mechanisms may include:
- Egg parasitism
- Predation
- Competition
- Antibiosis
- Host resistance induction
- Root colonization
Conceptually:
Biocontrol agent→Nematode suppression→Population↓Biocontrol\ agent  Nematode\
suppression  Population 66. Trap Crops
Some crops can be used strategically to stimulate nematode activity while reducing successful
reproduction or allowing controlled removal.
A simplified strategy is:
Trap crop→Nematode attraction→Nematode management→Population↓Trap\ crop  Nematode\ attraction  Nematode\ management  Population Trap crops are highly species-specific and require correct implementation.

67. Cover Crops
Certain cover crops can suppress nematodes through:
- Poor host status
- Biofumigant effects
- Organic matter effects
- Improved soil biology
But cover crops must be selected carefully because some can actually serve as hosts.

68. Chemical Control
Chemical nematicides can suppress plant-parasitic nematodes.
They may act through:
- Contact toxicity
- Fumigation
- Feeding disruption
- Other physiological effects
Because many nematicides have significant safety and environmental considerations:
Registered product+Correct label+Correct application 
must always be followed.

69. Fumigant vs Non-Fumigant Nematicides
Fumigant nematicides
Produce gases/volatile compounds that move through soil and can suppress soil pests.

Non-fumigant nematicides
Remain in or move through soil differently and act through specific toxicological mechanisms.
Their use depends heavily on:
- Crop
- Soil
- Target nematode
- Local registration
- Safety requirements

70. Integrated Nematode Management
INMgt=Prevention+Resistant variety+Crop rotation+Sanitation+Biological control+Organic managem
ent+Physical methods+Chemical control where appropriate 

The goal is:
Nematode population<Level causing economically unacceptable damage 

71. Nematodes and IPM
Nematode management fits the broader IPM philosophy:
IDENTIFY→SAMPLE→QUANTIFY→ASSESS RISK→DECIDE→MANAGE→RE−MONITOR - xed  IDENTIFY  SAMPLE  QUANTIFY  ASSESS\ RISK
 DECIDE  MANAGE  RE-MONITOR  
Unlike some foliar insect problems, nematode management often requires pre-plant or early-season
soil/root assessment because the pest may be hidden below ground.

75. Important Diagnostic Differences
Root-knot nematode
Meloidogyne→Root galls 

Lesion nematode
Pratylenchus→Root lesions 

Cyst nematode
Heterodera/Globodera→Cysts 

Reniform nematode
Rotylenchulus→Reniform female 

76. Common Diagnostic Mistakes
Mistake 1
“All stunted plants have nematodes.”

❌ No.

Mistake 2
“Yellowing means nitrogen deficiency.”

❌ Root damage can produce similar symptoms.

Mistake 3
“Any root swelling is root-knot nematode.”

❌ Other causes can produce swellings.
Mistake 4
“Nematodes are visible like insects.”

❌ Most plant-parasitic nematodes require magnification/extraction.
Mistake 5
“One crop rotation automatically controls all nematodes.”

❌ Rotation must consider the specific nematode and host status of the rotation crop.
Mistake 6
“More water always increases nematodes.”

❌ Soil moisture effects are species- and soil-dependent.
77. Complete Nematode Disease/Damage Cycle

↓
## Next Generation
78. Exam-Ready Answer
Nematodes are microscopic, elongated, unsegmented, worm-like animals, and plantparasitic nematodes are important agricultural pests that feed on living plant tissues
using a specialized stylet. They commonly attack roots but some species can affect
- ther plant tissues. Important plant-parasitic nematodes include root-knot nematodes
(Meloidogyne), cyst nematodes (Heterodera and Globodera), lesion nematodes
(Pratylenchus), burrowing nematodes (Radopholus) and reniform nematodes
(Rotylenchulus). Nematodes can cause root galls, lesions, root pruning, reduced root
growth and impaired water and nutrient uptake, resulting in stunting, chlorosis,
wilting and yield loss. Some nematodes can also transmit plant viruses or predispose
plants to secondary fungal and bacterial infections. Their life cycle generally consists
- f egg, juvenile stages and adult, with repeated molts between developmental stages.
They survive and spread through soil, water, infected roots, planting material,
machinery and other contaminated materials. Diagnosis requires examination of roots
and soil followed by nematode extraction, identification and population estimation.
Management is based on integrated approaches including prevention, resistant
varieties, crop rotation, sanitation, soil solarization, organic amendments, biological
control, appropriate trap/cover crops and registered nematicides where necessary.

And the key management principle:
SAMPLE → IDENTIFY → ROTATE → RESIST → SUPPRESS → REMONITOR   

## Module 16 — Plant Disease
## Management
## 8. Phytoplasmas
## 1. Introduction
Phytoplasmas are very small, wall-less, bacteria-like microorganisms associated with the phloem
tissue of plants. They cause important plant diseases characterized by abnormal growth, yellowing,
excessive branching, little leaves, witches' broom, phyllody and general decline.
They are particularly important because they are commonly transmitted by phloem-feeding insect
vectors, especially leafhoppers and planthoppers.
The basic relationship is:
Phytoplasma+Susceptible Host+Vector+Favorable Conditions→Disease 
A simplified transmission cycle is:
Infected plant→Vector acquisition→Phytoplasma multiplication/retention in vector→Healthy plant→I
nfection 

2. Definition of Phytoplasma
Simple definition
Phytoplasmas are very small, wall-less, bacteria-like plant pathogens that live mainly
in phloem tissues and are commonly transmitted by phloem-feeding insects.

Exam-ready definition
Phytoplasmas are obligate, wall-less, bacteria-like prokaryotic plant pathogens
associated primarily with the phloem of infected plants and commonly transmitted by
phloem-feeding insects such as leafhoppers and planthoppers. They cause diseases
characterized by symptoms such as yellowing, little leaf, witches' broom, phyllody,
abnormal branching, stunting and decline.

3. Most Important Concept
The easiest way to remember phytoplasmas is:

Phytoplasma = Wall-less + Phloem-associated + Insect-transmitted   
This distinguishes them from many other plant pathogens.

4. Are Phytoplasmas Bacteria?
Yes, phytoplasmas belong to the bacterial lineage, but they have unusual characteristics.
They are:
- Prokaryotic
- Wall-less
- Membrane-bound
- Very small
- Obligate parasites
- Associated with plant phloem
- Commonly transmitted by phloem-feeding insects
They are therefore often described as bacteria-like organisms in introductory plant pathology.

5. Phytoplasma vs Bacteria
Feature
Cellular

Difficult/not routinely cultured on artificial
media

yellowing

6. Why Are Phytoplasmas Wall-less?
Unlike most bacteria, phytoplasmas lack a rigid peptidoglycan cell wall.
Therefore:
Phytoplasma=Cell membrane without conventional bacterial cell wall 
This contributes to their:
- Pleomorphic shape
- Fragility
- Special host dependence
- Unusual biological characteristics

## 7. Pleomorphism
Pleomorphic means that the organism can occur in different shapes.
Because phytoplasmas lack a rigid cell wall, their shape can vary.
They may appear approximately:
- Spherical
- Ovoid
- Irregular
- Filamentous
Therefore:
No rigid cell wall→Shape flexibility 

8. Where Do Phytoplasmas Live in Plants?
Phytoplasmas are primarily associated with the:
Phloem  
The phloem transports photosynthetic products and signaling compounds through the plant.
A simplified pathway is:
Source leaf→Phloem→Sink/growing tissueSource\ leaf  Phloem  Sink/growing\
tissue

Phytoplasmas can move through this vascular system.

9. Phytoplasma Movement in Plants
A generalized sequence is:
Initial infection→Phloem colonization→Phloem movement→Systemic infection 
This explains why symptoms may appear in multiple parts of the plant.

10. How Phytoplasmas Enter Plants
Phytoplasmas are generally introduced through feeding by an infected insect vector.
Simplified:
Infected vector→Phloem feeding→Phytoplasma inoculation→Plant infection 
They are not typically spread simply by casual contact between healthy and infected leaves in the way
some mechanically transmissible viruses can be.

11. Important Phytoplasma Vectors
Major vectors include:
- Leafhoppers
- Planthoppers
- Some other phloem-feeding insects
The most important vector depends on the particular phytoplasma and crop.
Thus:
Vector identification is essential for disease management. 

12. Phytoplasma Acquisition by Vector
The process can be represented as:

Infected plant→Vector feeds on phloem→Phytoplasma acquiredInfected\ plant  Vector\
feeds\ on\ phloem  Phytoplasma\ acquired
The phytoplasma then passes through the insect's body.

13. Phytoplasma in the Insect Vector
Unlike a simple contaminant on the insect's mouthparts, phytoplasmas can multiply within suitable
insect vectors.
A generalized pathway is:
Acquisition→Insect gut→Internal movement→Hemolymph/tissues→Salivary glands→Transmission - xed  Acquisition  Insect\ gut  Internal\ movement  Hemolymph/tissues  Salivary\ glands  Transmission  
The exact biology varies among phytoplasma-vector combinations.

14. Persistent Propagative Transmission
Phytoplasma transmission is generally described as persistent and propagative in competent vectors.
This means:
- The phytoplasma persists in the vector.
- It can multiply within the vector.
- The vector can transmit it later.
Conceptually:
Acquisition→Persistence→Multiplication→Transmission 
This is a major distinction from some short-retention virus-vector relationships.

15. Transmission to a Healthy Plant
When an infected vector feeds on a susceptible plant:
Infected vector→Phloem feeding→Phytoplasma inoculation→Phloem colonizationInfected\ vector
 Phloem\ feeding  Phytoplasma\ inoculation  Phloem\ colonization
Eventually:
Local infection→Systemic infection 

16. Phytoplasma Disease Cycle
The complete simplified cycle is:
## Infected Plant
↓
Phytoplasma in phloem
↓
Vector feeds
↓
Phytoplasma acquisition
↓
Phytoplasma persists/multiplies in vector
↓
Vector reaches healthy plant
↓
Phloem feeding
↓
Phytoplasma transmission
↓
Phloem colonization
↓
Systemic movement
↓
## Symptoms
↓
New infected plant
↓
New vector acquisition

17. Major Symptoms of Phytoplasma Diseases
Phytoplasma diseases have several characteristic symptoms.
The most important are:

Yellowing+Little leaf+Witches′ broom+Phyllody+Abnormal branching+Stunting 

18. Little Leaf
Little leaf is a characteristic symptom in which leaves become unusually small.
General mechanism:
Phytoplasma→Growth/development disruption→Reduced leaf development→Little leavesPhytoplasm
a  Growth/development\ disruption  Reduced\ leaf\ development  Little\ leaves

19. Witches' Broom
Witches' broom is excessive production of numerous small shoots or branches from a region of the
plant.
The plant may develop a broom-like appearance.
Abnormal shoot proliferation→Witches′ broom 
This is one of the classic symptoms associated with phytoplasmas.

## 20. Phyllody
Phyllody is the abnormal conversion or development of floral parts into leaf-like structures.
For example:
Flower tissue→Abnormal leaf-like structuresFlower\ tissue  Abnormal\ leaf like\
structures
This can severely interfere with reproduction.

## 21. Yellowing
Phytoplasma infection can cause:
- General yellowing
- Chlorosis
- Vein-associated discoloration
However:

Yellowing alone≠Phytoplasma 
Nutrient deficiencies, viruses, root problems and other stresses can also cause yellowing.

## 22. Stunting
Infected plants may show reduced growth.
Phytoplasma→Phloem/physiological disruption→Growth disturbance→StuntingPhytoplasma
 Phloem/physiological\ disruption  Growth\ disturbance  Stunting

23. Abnormal Branching
Phytoplasma infection can disrupt normal plant architecture.
This may produce:
- Excessive branching
- Shortened internodes
- Abnormal shoots
- Broom-like growth

24. Reduced Flowering and Fruit Set
Phytoplasma infection can interfere with reproductive development.
Consequences may include:
- Poor flowering
- Abnormal flowers
- Phyllody
- Reduced fruit set
- Poor seed formation
- Reduced yield

25. General Symptom Pattern
A useful memory sequence is:

## Yellowing→Little Leaf→Abnormal Branching→Witches′ Broom→Phyllod
Y→STUNTING 
Not every disease produces every symptom.

26. Why Do Phytoplasmas Cause Abnormal
Growth?
Phytoplasmas interact with plant physiological and developmental systems.
They can interfere with:
- Hormonal regulation
- Phloem function
- Nutrient distribution
- Carbohydrate transport
- Flower development
- Shoot development
- Plant defense
- Gene regulation
Therefore:
Phytoplasma→Physiological/developmental disruption→Abnormal growthPhytoplasma  Physiological/developmental\ disruption  Abnormal\ growth

27. Phytoplasmas and Plant Hormones
Phytoplasma infection can alter plant hormonal balance, including pathways associated with:
- Auxins
- Cytokinins
- Gibberellins
- Abscisic acid
- Ethylene
- Other signaling pathways
This contributes to abnormal growth and development.

For example:
Hormonal imbalance→Abnormal shoot development→Witches′ broomHormonal\ imbalance
 Abnormal\ shoot\ development  Witches'\ broom

28. Phytoplasma and Flower Development
Phytoplasmas can disrupt genes and signaling pathways involved in floral development.
This can result in:
Normal flower→Developmental disruption→PhyllodyNormal\ flower  Developmental\
disruption  Phyllody
Thus phyllody is an important diagnostic clue.

29. Phytoplasma and Phloem
Because phytoplasmas are associated with phloem, infection can interfere with normal phloemassociated processes.
Conceptually:
Phytoplasma→Phloem colonization→Transport/signaling disruption→Plant physiological abnormalitie
sPhytoplasma  Phloem\ colonization  Transport/signaling\ disruption  Plant\ physiological\ abnormalities
The exact mechanisms are complex and vary among phytoplasma-host combinations.

30. Sources of Phytoplasma
Potential sources include:
- Infected crop plants
- Infected perennial plants
- Volunteer plants
- Weeds
- Alternative host plants
- Infected planting material in some systems
- Infected vector populations
The importance of each source depends on the disease.

31. Weed Reservoirs
Weeds can sometimes act as reservoirs.
The cycle can be:
Infected weed→Vector→CropInfected\ weed  Vector  Crop
Therefore:
Weed management 
can be important when weeds maintain phytoplasmas or their vectors.

32. Alternate Hosts
Some phytoplasmas can infect multiple plant species.
Therefore:
Crop↔Alternate host↔VectorCrop  Alternate\ host  Vector
can help maintain the disease cycle.
This is why identifying reservoir hosts can be important in epidemiology.

33. Vegetative Propagation
Some phytoplasmas can be maintained through infected vegetative planting material.
Examples may include:
- Cuttings
- Grafts
- Suckers
- Other vegetative propagules
General pathway:
Infected mother plant→Infected propagule→New infected plantInfected\ mother\ plant  Infected\ propagule  New\ infected\ plant
Therefore healthy planting material is important.

34. Seed Transmission
Seed transmission is not a universal feature of phytoplasmas.
For a particular phytoplasma-host combination, transmission through seed must be established
experimentally.
Therefore do not make the blanket statement:
“All phytoplasmas are seed transmitted.”
That is incorrect.

35. Mechanical Transmission
Phytoplasmas are generally not considered readily mechanically transmissible through ordinary
plant sap contact like some plant viruses.
Thus:
Phytoplasma transmission is primarily vector−associated 
for most important field diseases.

36. Disease Triangle
For phytoplasma disease:
Susceptible Host+Phytoplasma+Favorable Environment→Disease 
But because insect vectors are central to transmission, a more practical epidemiological model is:
Host+Phytoplasma+Vector+Environment+Time→Epidemic 

37. Disease Tetrahedron
Including time:
Disease=f(Host,Pathogen,Environment,Time) 
For phytoplasmas, vector population dynamics should additionally be considered.

38. Environmental Factors
Disease development and vector activity are affected by:
- Temperature
- Humidity
- Rainfall
- Plant growth
- Vector abundance
- Weed hosts
- Crop stage
The environment can influence both:
Plant susceptibilityPlant\ susceptibility
and:
Vector population/activityVector\ population/activity
Therefore:
Environment→Host+Vector→Disease spreadEnvironment  Host+Vector  Disease\ spread

39. Vector Population
Phytoplasma disease risk can increase when competent vector populations increase.
Conceptually:
Vector population↑→Transmission opportunities↑→Disease spread potential↑Vector\
population  Transmission\ opportunities  Disease\ spread\
potential However, vector abundance alone does not guarantee disease because the vector must be competent
and infected.

40. Phytoplasma Diagnosis
Diagnosis can be challenging because symptoms overlap with:
- Viruses
- Nutrient deficiencies

- Herbicide injury
- Abiotic stress
- Other pathogens
Therefore:
Symptoms alone≠Confirmation 

41. Field Diagnosis
Look for combinations such as:
- Little leaf
- Witches' broom
- Phyllody
- Yellowing
- Abnormal branching
- Stunting
- Poor flowering
- Vector presence
Also record:
- Crop
- Variety
- Plant age
- Growth stage
- Field distribution
- Symptom progression
- Vector abundance
- Weed hosts
- Previous crop

42. Laboratory Diagnosis
Modern diagnosis commonly uses molecular techniques.

Important methods include:
- PCR
- Nested PCR
- qPCR
- Sequencing
- DNA-based assays
Because phytoplasmas are difficult to culture using ordinary bacterial culture methods, molecular
detection is particularly important.

43. PCR Diagnosis
A generalized pathway:
Plant tissue→DNA extraction→PCR→Phytoplasma DNA detectionPlant\ tissue  DNA\
extraction  PCR  Phytoplasma\ DNA\ detection
PCR can provide evidence of phytoplasma presence.

44. Nested PCR
Nested PCR uses two rounds of amplification to improve sensitivity and specificity in many diagnostic
applications.
Conceptually:
DNA→First PCR→Second targeted PCR→DetectionDNA  First\ PCR  Second\
targeted\ PCR  Detection
It has historically been widely used in phytoplasma detection.

## 45. Sequencing
Sequencing can help:
- Confirm identity
- Characterize strains/lineages
- Compare isolates
- Study epidemiology
Thus:

PCR detection+Sequencing→More detailed characterizationPCR\ detection + Sequencing  More\ detailed\ characterization

46. Why Culture Is Difficult
Phytoplasmas are obligate host-associated organisms and are not routinely maintained using standard
artificial bacterial culture techniques.
Therefore:
Molecular detection 
is especially important.

47. Phytoplasma Management
There is generally no practical field treatment that simply eliminates an established systemic
phytoplasma infection from an infected plant.
Therefore management emphasizes:
Prevention+Vector Management+Removal of Sources+Healthy Planting Material 

48. Resistant or Tolerant Varieties
Where available, resistant or tolerant varieties can reduce disease impact.
Resistance/tolerance→Disease severity↓→Yield protectionResistance/tolerance  Disease\
severity  Yield\ protection
Availability is crop- and phytoplasma-specific.

49. Vector Management
Because vectors are critical to transmission:
Vector management=Major component of phytoplasma disease management 
Possible approaches include:
- Monitoring vectors
- Cultural practices
- Weed management

- Habitat management
- Biological control
- Selective insect control where appropriate
- Resistant crops
- Removal of infected source plants
Chemical control should follow registered local recommendations.

## 50. Roguing
Roguing means removing infected plants.
General process:
Detect→Remove→Destroy/manage safelyDetect  Remove  Destroy/manage\
safely
Removing infected plants can reduce sources available to vectors.
However:
Roguing alone≠Complete disease controlRoguing\ alone Complete\ disease\ control
because vectors and reservoir hosts may remain.

51. Weed Management
When weeds serve as reservoirs:
Weed reservoir↓→Phytoplasma source↓→Vector acquisition opportunity↓Weed\ reservoir  Phytoplasma\ source  Vector\ acquisition\ opportunity Therefore weed management can reduce disease pressure.

52. Healthy Planting Material
Use:
- Disease-free cuttings
- Healthy nursery plants
- Tested mother plants
- Clean grafting material

- Certified material where applicable
This is especially important in vegetatively propagated crops.

## 53. Quarantine
Quarantine and movement restrictions can reduce introduction of phytoplasma-infected:
- Plants
- Cuttings
- Grafts
- Nursery material
- Other regulated planting material
Concept:
Exclusion→Introduction risk↓ 

54. Integrated Phytoplasma Disease
Management
A practical management system is:
Healthy planting material+Vector monitoring+Vector management+Roguing+Weed/reservoir managem
ent+Resistant varieties+Sanitation+Quarantine 
The decision cycle is:
## Identify→Confirm→Monitor→Remove Sources→Manage Vectors→Re−Moni
TOR 

55. Phytoplasma vs Virus

59. Important Phytoplasma Symptoms — Quick
Table
Symptom
Meaning
Little leaf

Witches' broom Excessive abnormal branching

Decline
Progressive reduction in plant health

60. Common Diagnostic Mistakes
Mistake 1
“Witches' broom always means phytoplasma.”

❌ No. Other biological or physiological conditions can sometimes produce abnormal branching.
Mistake 2
“Phytoplasma is a virus.”

❌ No.

Phytoplasma≠VirusPhytoplasma Virus

Mistake 3
“Phytoplasma is a normal bacterium.”

❌ It is a specialized wall-less, host-associated bacterial lineage.
Mistake 4
“All leafhoppers transmit phytoplasmas.”

❌ Transmission is specific to competent vector-pathogen relationships.
Mistake 5
“Spraying insecticide cures infected plants.”

❌ Vector management primarily reduces further transmission; it does not normally eliminate an
established systemic infection.

Mistake 6
“Every yellowing plant has phytoplasma.”

❌ Nutritional, viral, fungal, environmental and other causes can produce yellowing.
61. Most Important Conceptual Chain
## Infected Plant→Vector Acquisition→Phytoplasma Multiplication→Vecto
R TRANSMISSION→PHLOEM COLONIZATION→SYSTEMIC DISEASE 
62. Exam-Ready Answer
Phytoplasmas are very small, wall-less, obligate, bacteria-like plant pathogens
associated mainly with the phloem of infected plants. Unlike typical bacteria, they
lack a rigid cell wall and are difficult to maintain using conventional artificial culture
methods. They are commonly transmitted by phloem-feeding insects, particularly
leafhoppers and planthoppers, in persistent propagative relationships. Important
symptoms of phytoplasma diseases include yellowing, little leaf, witches' broom,
phyllody, abnormal branching, shortened internodes, stunting and reduced
reproductive development. Phytoplasmas can move systemically through the phloem
and interfere with plant hormonal regulation, nutrient distribution and
developmental processes. Diagnosis is based on field symptoms, vector and disease
history and, importantly, molecular techniques such as PCR, nested PCR, qPCR and
sequencing. Management emphasizes prevention, healthy planting material, vector
monitoring and management, removal of infected plants, weed and reservoir-host
management, resistant or tolerant varieties where available, sanitation and
quarantine.

PHYTOPLASMA = WALL-LESS + PHLOEM + LEAFHOPPER/PLANTHOPPER + LITTLE LEAF/
PHYLLODY/WITCHES’ BROOM   
## Module 16 — Plant Disease
## Management
## 9. Disease Identification
## 1. Introduction
Disease identification is the systematic process of determining what plant disease is affecting a crop
by examining the crop, symptoms, signs, disease pattern, plant stage, environmental conditions, history
and, when necessary, laboratory evidence.
It is one of the most important steps in plant disease management because:
Correct Identification→Correct Diagnosis→Correct Management 
If the disease is incorrectly identified, the control method may be ineffective, wasteful, or even
harmful.

## 2. Definition
Simple definition
Disease identification is the process of recognizing and determining the disease
affecting a plant by examining its symptoms, signs, causal organism, field pattern,
environmental conditions and other diagnostic evidence.

Exam-ready definition
Plant disease identification is a systematic process of determining the identity and
probable cause of a plant disease through observation of symptoms and signs,
examination of the host and affected tissues, analysis of disease distribution and
environmental conditions, and laboratory or molecular confirmation when required.

3. Identification vs Diagnosis
These terms are related but not exactly identical.

Identification
Answers:
“What disease or pathogen is this?”

Diagnosis
Answers:
“What is causing this abnormal condition, and why is it occurring?”
Therefore:
Identification⊂Diagnosis 
A complete diagnosis may involve:
Host+Symptoms+Signs+Pattern+Environment+History+Laboratory Evidence 

4. Why Disease Identification Is Important
Correct identification helps determine:
- Causal organism
- Disease cycle
- Source of inoculum
- Method of spread
- Favorable environmental conditions
- Disease severity
- Risk of spread
- Appropriate management method
- Need for chemical/biological control
- Need for quarantine or removal
- Future prevention strategy
The basic chain is:

Identify→Understand→Decide→Manage 

5. The Golden Rule of Disease Identification
Never identify a disease from only one symptom.
For example:
Yellow leaves≠Automatically diseaseYellow\ leaves Automatically\ disease
Yellowing may result from:
- Nutrient deficiency
- Water stress
- Waterlogging
- Root damage
- Salinity
- Herbicide injury
- Virus
- Phytoplasma
- Fungal disease
- Bacterial disease
Therefore:
One symptom≠One disease 

6. Major Evidence Used for Identification
Disease identification generally combines:
Host+Symptoms+Signs+Pattern+Environment+History+Laboratory evidence 
Each provides different information.

7. Step 1 — Identify the Host Crop
First determine:
- Crop species

- Variety/cultivar if known
- Crop age
- Growth stage
- Planting method
- Previous crop
- Crop management
Why?
Because many diseases have specific host ranges.
For example:
Disease susceptibility=f(Crop+Variety+Stage)Disease\ susceptibility=f(Crop+Variety+Stage)

8. Step 2 — Examine the Entire Plant
Do not inspect only the visibly damaged part.
Examine:

Above ground
- Leaves
- Stems
- Branches
- Flowers
- Fruits
- Pods
- Seeds

Below ground
- Roots
- Root hairs
- Crown
- Rhizomes/tubers where applicable
A disease may begin in roots but produce symptoms on leaves.

9. Step 3 — Observe Symptoms
A symptom is the visible or measurable response of the plant to a disease-causing factor.
Symptom=Plant′s response 
Examples:
- Yellowing
- Wilting
- Necrosis
- Leaf spots
- Blight
- Canker
- Rot
- Damping-off
- Stunting
- Curling
- Mosaic
- Gall
- Dieback
- Chlorosis

10. Common Symptoms
Chlorosis
Loss/reduction of green color.
Green→YellowGreen Yellow
Possible causes:
- Nutrient deficiency
- Virus
- Root disease
- Phytoplasma

- Salinity
- Other stresses

Necrosis
Death of plant tissue.
Living tissue→Dead tissueLiving\ tissue Dead\ tissue
May appear as:
- Brown areas
- Black areas
- Dead spots
- Dead leaf margins

Wilt
Loss of plant turgidity.
Water imbalance→Loss of turgor→WiltingWater\ imbalance Loss\ of\ turgor Wilting
Possible causes include:
- Vascular pathogens
- Root disease
- Drought
- Waterlogging
- Root nematodes
- Salinity

11. Leaf Spot
Discrete dead or damaged areas on leaves.
Observe:
- Shape
- Size
- Color

- Margin
- Center
- Number
- Distribution
For example:
Spot characteristics→Diagnostic clueSpot\ characteristics  Diagnostic\ clue
But a leaf spot alone does not identify the pathogen.

## 12. Blight
Blight generally refers to rapid and extensive death/discoloration of plant tissues, especially leaves and
shoots.
A disease may progress approximately as:
Small lesion→Lesion expansion→Lesion coalescence→Large dead areaSmall\ lesion  Lesion\ expansion  Lesion\ coalescence  Large\ dead\ area

## 13. Canker
A canker is a localized diseased or dead lesion, commonly associated with stems, branches or woody
tissues.
Look for:
- Sunken lesions
- Cracks
- Discolored bark
- Dieback around lesion

## 14. Rot
Rot is the deterioration and decomposition of plant tissues.
Types include:
- Soft rot
- Dry rot
- Wet rot

- Root rot
- Fruit rot
- Stem rot

## 15. Damping-Off
Commonly affects seedlings.

Pre-emergence
Seed/seedling dies before emergence.

Post-emergence
Seedling emerges but develops a constricted/rotted stem near the soil line and collapses.
Seedling damping-off off  
is frequently associated with soil-borne pathogens, including fungi and oomycetes.

## 16. Stunting
Stunting means abnormal reduction in plant growth.
Possible causes:
- Viruses
- Phytoplasmas
- Nematodes
- Root pathogens
- Nutrient deficiency
- Water stress
- Salinity
- Poor soil conditions
Therefore:
Stunting≠Single causeStunting Single\ cause

## 17. Mosaic
Mosaic refers to an irregular pattern of:

- Light green
- Dark green
- Yellow-green
areas.
It is strongly associated with many viral diseases, but diagnosis should still be confirmed.

18. Curling and Distortion
Leaves may show:
- Upward curling
- Downward curling
- Rolling
- Twisting
- Distortion
Possible causes include:
- Viruses
- Insect feeding
- Herbicide injury
- Nutrient imbalance
- Environmental stress

19. Step 4 — Look for Signs
A sign is the actual pathogen, pathogen structure, or pathogen-associated material observed on or in
diseased tissue.
Sign=Evidence of the pathogen 
Examples:
- Fungal mycelium
- Spores
- Fruiting bodies
- Sclerotia

- Bacterial ooze
- Nematodes
- Cysts

20. Symptoms vs Signs
Feature
Symptoms
Signs
Produced by
Plant response Pathogen/pathogen structure
Example
Yellowing
Fungal spores
Example
Wilt
Bacterial ooze
Example
Necrosis
Mycelium
Example
Stunting
Nematode
Diagnostic value Important
Often highly useful

Memory:
SYMPTOM=Plant  SIGN=Pathogen 

21. Step 5 — Examine Disease Distribution
Disease distribution in the field provides important clues.
Observe whether disease occurs:
- Randomly
- In patches
- Along rows
- At field edges
- Near irrigation channels
- In low-lying areas
- Near drainage problems
- Around entry points
- Throughout the field

22. Random Distribution
Randomly distributed disease may indicate:
- Random inoculum sources

- Random planting material
- Vector movement
- Individual plant susceptibility
But distribution alone is not diagnostic.

23. Patch Distribution
Disease occurring in patches may suggest:
- Soil-borne pathogens
- Nematodes
- Waterlogging
- Soil variation
- Localized inoculum
Example:
Poor drainage→Root stress/pathogen development→Patchy diseasePoor\ drainage  Root\
stress/pathogen\ development  Patchy\ disease

24. Row Pattern
Disease following planting rows can provide clues about:
- Seed-borne infection
- Planting material
- Irrigation
- Soil movement
- Crop-to-crop spread

25. Field-Edge Pattern
Disease concentrated near field edges may suggest:
- Vector entry
- Wind-borne inoculum
- Contaminated machinery

- Adjacent infected crop
- Alternative host nearby
Again, this is evidence—not proof.

26. Low-Lying Areas
Disease concentrated in low areas may be associated with:
- Water accumulation
- Poor drainage
- High soil moisture
- Root stress
- Soil-borne pathogens
Thus:
Topography→Water distribution→Disease riskTopography  Water\ distribution  Disease\ risk

27. Step 6 — Examine the Disease Progression
Ask:
- When did symptoms first appear?
- Which plant part was affected first?
- How fast did disease develop?
- Is disease spreading?
- Are new leaves affected?
- Are older leaves affected?
- Does disease move upward/downward?
- Does disease appear after rainfall or irrigation?
This creates a disease timeline.
Initial symptom→Progression→Current symptom 

28. Step 7 — Consider Crop Growth Stage
Disease susceptibility can vary with plant age.
Consider:
- Seed stage
- Seedling
- Vegetative stage
- Flowering
- Fruiting
- Grain filling
- Maturity
For example:
Disease risk=f(Pathogen+Host stage+Environment)Disease\ risk=f(Pathogen+Host\
stage+Environment)

29. Step 8 — Examine Environmental
Conditions
Important factors include:

Temperature
Temperature→Pathogen developmentTemperature Pathogen\ development

Humidity
High humidity can favor many foliar pathogens.

Leaf wetness
Important for many foliar infections.

Rainfall
Can:
- Disperse spores
- Splash bacteria
- Increase humidity

- Create leaf wetness

Soil moisture
Important for many root diseases.

Wind
Can disperse:
- Spores
- Infected plant material
- Dust/soil particles
- Vectors indirectly

30. Step 9 — Examine Field History
Ask:
- Was the disease present last season?
- What crop was previously grown?
- Was infected residue left?
- Was the same variety planted?
- Was infected planting material used?
- Were pesticides applied?
- Was irrigation changed?
- Was there flooding?
- Were weeds abundant?
- Were vectors observed?
History can reveal the disease cycle.

31. Step 10 — Distinguish Biotic and Abiotic
Causes
This is one of the most important diagnostic steps.

Biotic
Caused by living biological agents:
- Fungi
- Oomycetes
- Bacteria
- Viruses
- Viroids
- Phytoplasmas
- Nematodes
- Parasitic plants

Abiotic
Caused by nonliving factors:
- Drought
- Flooding
- Heat
- Frost
- Salinity
- Nutrient deficiency
- Nutrient toxicity
- Herbicide injury
- Air pollution
- pH problems
Biotic or Abiotic? 
must be considered before selecting treatment.

32. Biotic vs Abiotic Symptoms
Some clues can help.

Biotic disease often
- Progresses over time
- Has characteristic patterns
- May spread between plants
- May show signs
- May have focal/patchy distribution

Abiotic disorder often
- Appears relatively uniformly across an affected zone
- Corresponds to soil/environmental variation
- May affect plants according to spray patterns or field gradients
But these are general clues, not absolute rules.

33. Step 11 — Check for Signs of Specific
Pathogens
Fungal
Look for:
- Mycelium
- Spores
- Fruiting bodies
- Sclerotia

Bacterial
Look for:
- Bacterial ooze
- Water-soaked lesions
- Soft rot

Viral
Usually no visible pathogen structure in the field.
Diagnosis often requires:

## • Elisa
- PCR/RT-PCR
- Other molecular/serological tests

Phytoplasma
Look for characteristic symptoms and confirm using molecular testing.

Nematodes
Look for:
- Root galls
- Lesions
- Cysts
- Root pruning
and examine soil/root samples.

34. Step 12 — Collect a Proper Sample
When laboratory confirmation is required, sampling quality matters.
Collect:
- Recently affected tissue
- Representative symptomatic tissue
- Healthy-looking tissue near diseased tissue where appropriate
- Roots and soil for suspected soil/root pathogens
- Whole plants for seedlings where appropriate
Avoid:
- Completely decomposed tissue
- Extremely old dead material
- Unrepresentative samples

35. Sample Labelling
Record:
- Crop

- Variety
- Location
- Date
- Growth stage
- Symptoms
- Field pattern
- Environmental conditions
- Suspected disease
- Plant part collected
Good records improve diagnostic accuracy.

36. Laboratory Diagnosis
Laboratory methods may include:

Microscopy
Used to observe:
- Spores
- Mycelium
- Bacteria in some preparations
- Nematodes
- Pathogen structures

Culture
Useful for many fungi and some bacteria.

Serology
For example:
## • Elisa
Particularly useful for some viruses.

Molecular diagnosis
- PCR
- RT-PCR

- qPCR
- Sequencing
These can identify pathogens with greater specificity.

37. Koch's Postulates
Koch's postulates are historically important principles for linking a microorganism with disease.
A simplified version:
Disease plant→Pathogen isolation→Healthy host inoculation→Same diseaseDisease\ plant  Pathogen\ isolation  Healthy\ host\ inoculation  Same\ disease
However, classical Koch's postulates are not universally applicable, particularly to:
- Viruses
- Viroids
- Phytoplasmas
- Obligate pathogens
- Complex disease systems
Modern plant pathology therefore also uses molecular and epidemiological evidence.

38. Disease Identification Workflow
The complete workflow is:
1. Identify crop 
↓
2. Examine whole plant 
↓
3. Record symptoms 
↓
4. Look for signs 
↓
5. Examine disease pattern 
↓

6. Check crop stage 
↓
7. Examine environment 
↓
8. Review field history 
↓
9. Consider biotic vs abiotic 
↓
10. Collect samples 
↓
11. Laboratory/molecular confirmation 
↓
12. Final diagnosis 
↓
13. Select management 

39. Diagnostic Triangle
For practical field diagnosis:
HOST+SYMPTOMS+ENVIRONMENT 
should be considered together.
For pathogen-focused diagnosis:
HOST+SYMPTOMS+SIGNS+PATHOGEN 

40. Disease Triangle in Identification
Disease development is explained by:
Host+Pathogen+Environment→Disease 
Therefore disease identification should ask:

Host
Is this crop susceptible?

Pathogen
What pathogen could produce these symptoms?

Environment
Were conditions favorable?
This helps narrow down possible causes.

41. Disease Tetrahedron
Time is also important:
Disease=f(Host+Pathogen+Environment+Time)
 
For example, a disease may require:
- A susceptible crop stage
- A certain temperature
- Several hours of leaf wetness
- Sufficient inoculum
- Enough time for infection

42. Differential Diagnosis
Differential diagnosis means comparing several possible causes that produce similar symptoms.
Example:
Suppose a crop is yellow and stunted.
Possible causes:
Possible cause
Evidence to examine
Nitrogen deficiency Soil/tissue test, uniformity
Root disease
Root discoloration/rot
Nematode
Galls/lesions + soil test
Virus
Mosaic/vector/specific test
Phytoplasma
Little leaf/witches' broom + PCR
Waterlogging
Field drainage pattern
Salinity
EC/soil pattern
Herbicide injury
Spray pattern/history

Then:
Evidence→Eliminate unlikely causes→Confirm most probable causeEvidence  Eliminate\
unlikely\ causes  Confirm\ most\ probable\ cause

43. Disease Identification Is Evidence-Based
A useful conceptual scoring system is:
Diagnosis=Symptoms+Signs+Pattern+Host+Stage+Environment+History+Laboratory evidence   Diagnosis= Symptoms + Signs + Pattern + Host + Stage + Environment + History + Laboratory\
evidence  
This is not a numerical formula; it represents the integration of evidence.

44. Example — Root-Knot Nematode
Observation
Tomato plants show:
- Stunting
- Yellowing
- Uneven growth
- Wilting during hot periods

Root examination
Large galls are observed.

Soil/root examination
Nematodes are detected.
Therefore:
Symptoms+Root signs+Nematode detection→Root-knot nematode diagnosis knot\ nematode\ diagnosis  

45. Example — Phytoplasma
Field observations
- Little leaves
- Excessive branching

- Phyllody
- Stunting

Vector observation
Phloem-feeding insects present.

Laboratory
Phytoplasma DNA detected using PCR.
Therefore:
Symptoms+Vector+Molecular confirmation→Phytoplasma disease 

46. Example — Fungal Disease
Suppose leaves show:
- Circular brown lesions
- Expanding spots
- Visible fungal structures
Microscopic examination detects characteristic spores.
Therefore:
Symptoms+Signs+Microscopy→Fungal disease identificationSymptoms+Signs+Microscopy
 Fungal\ disease\ identification

47. Example — Bacterial Disease
Suppose a plant has:
- Water-soaked lesions
- Rapid tissue collapse
- Bacterial ooze
Laboratory testing confirms a bacterial pathogen.
Then:
Symptoms+Sign+Laboratory confirmation→Bacterial diseaseSymptoms+Sign+Laboratory\
confirmation  Bacterial\ disease

48. Example — Viral Disease
Suppose plants show:
- Mosaic
- Leaf distortion
- Stunting
- Vector activity
But no fungal or bacterial signs are visible.
Molecular/serological testing confirms a virus.
Therefore:
Field symptoms+Vector evidence+Virus test→Viral diseaseField\ symptoms + Vector\ evidence +
Virus\ test  Viral\ disease

49. Identification by Symptoms Alone — Why It
Fails
Consider:
WiltingWilting
Possible causes include:
- Drought
- Root rot
- Vascular wilt
- Nematode damage
- Waterlogging
- Salinity
- Root injury
Therefore:
Wilting is a symptom, not a diagnosis. 
The same principle applies to:
- Yellowing
- Stunting

- Leaf spots
- Necrosis
- Curling

50. Field Pattern as a Diagnostic Tool
Pattern can provide clues about the cause.

Patchy
May suggest:
- Soil-borne pathogen
- Nematode
- Drainage
- Soil variation

Uniform
May suggest:
- Nutrient problem
- Environmental stress
- Chemical exposure

Edge-related
May suggest:
- Vector entry
- Windborne inoculum
- Adjacent infected crop

Row-related
May suggest:
- Seed
- Planting material
- Irrigation
- Soil movement
These are diagnostic clues rather than definitive rules.

51. Importance of Plant Part Affected
Plant part
Possible clues
Roots
Nematodes, root rots, nutrient/water problems
Crown
Soil-borne pathogens
Leaves
Fungi, bacteria, viruses, environmental disorders
Stems
Cankers, wilts, borers, mechanical injury
Vascular tissue Vascular pathogens
Flowers
Floral pathogens, phytoplasma-related abnormalities
Fruits
Fruit rots, viruses, physiological disorders
Seeds
Seed-borne pathogens

52. Disease Severity vs Incidence
Identification should also record disease quantity.

Incidence
How many plants are diseased?
DI(%)=Diseased plantsTotal plants×100  Total\ plants 
 100  

Severity
How much tissue is affected?
DS(%)=Diseased tissue areaTotal tissue area×100  Total\
tissue\ area  100  
Memory:
Incidence=HOW MANY?  Severity=HOW MUCH?
 

53. Disease Identification and Digital
Agriculture
Modern disease identification can use:
- Smartphone photography
- Image processing
- Computer vision

- Machine learning
- Deep learning
- Drones
- Multispectral imaging
- Hyperspectral imaging
- Remote sensing
A simplified AI workflow:
Image→Preprocessing→Feature extraction→Disease classification→Confidence→Human verification
 
AI can assist diagnosis but should not automatically replace expert confirmation, especially for highstakes crop-management decisions.

54. Common Mistakes in Disease Identification
1. Treating every abnormal plant as diseased
Wrong.

2. Assuming every fungus-like structure is the pathogen
Wrong; some fungi may be secondary colonizers.

3. Confusing symptom and sign
Symptom≠SignSymptom Sign

4. Ignoring abiotic causes
A major diagnostic error.

5. Ignoring roots
Many important diseases begin below ground.

6. Ignoring crop stage
Disease susceptibility changes with development.

7. Ignoring field pattern
Spatial distribution provides important clues.

8. Treating before confirming
Can worsen resistance, cost and environmental impact.

9. Assuming one symptom has one cause
Usually incorrect.

10. Ignoring laboratory confirmation when necessary
Some diseases cannot be reliably distinguished in the field.

55. Disease Identification vs Disease
Management
These are different steps.
Identification→Diagnosis→Management 
Do not reverse the order:
Management should follow diagnosis 
For example, spraying a fungicide against an abiotic nutrient disorder will not solve the underlying
problem.

56. Complete Disease Identification Decision
Tree
## Abnormal Plant
↓
## Identify Crop + Growth Stage
↓
## Examine Whole Plant
↓
## What Part Is Affected?
↓
## Observe Symptoms
↓
## Look For Signs
↓
## Check Field Distribution
↓
## Check Environment
↓
## Check Field History
↓
## Biotic Or Abiotic?
↓
IF BIOTIC → Which pathogen group?
↓
## Fungus / Oomycete / Bacteria / Virus /
## Phytoplasma / Nematode / Other
↓
## Collect Representative Sample
↓
## Laboratory / Molecular Confirmation
↓
## Final Diagnosis
↓
## Select Management
↓
## Monitor Response
57. Master Diagnostic Formula
Disease Identification = Host + Symptoms + Signs + Pattern + Stage + Environment + History + Labor
atory Confirmation    
Not every case requires every component, but difficult or ambiguous cases may.

58. Final Disease Identification Checklist
Before declaring a diagnosis, ask:

Host
- What crop is affected?
- Which variety?
- What growth stage?

Symptoms
- What exactly is abnormal?
- Where did it begin?
- How did it progress?

Signs
- Is the pathogen visible?
- Spores?
- Mycelium?
- Bacterial ooze?
- Nematodes?
- Cysts?

Pattern
- Random?
- Patchy?
- Row-based?
- Field edge?
- Low areas?

Environment
- Temperature?
- Humidity?
- Rainfall?
- Leaf wetness?
- Soil moisture?
- Drainage?

History
- Previous crop?
- Previous disease?
- Planting material?
- Irrigation?
- Pesticide use?
- Weather events?

Confirmation
- Is field diagnosis sufficient?
- Is microscopy required?
- Culture?
## • Elisa?
- PCR/RT-PCR?
- Nematode extraction?

59. Exam-Ready Short Answer
Disease identification is the systematic process of determining the disease affecting a
crop by examining the host, symptoms, signs, disease distribution, crop stage,
environmental conditions and field history, followed by laboratory or molecular
confirmation when necessary. Symptoms are the plant's responses to disease, whereas
signs are the pathogen or its structures. Important diagnostic observations include
chlorosis, necrosis, wilting, leaf spots, blight, canker, rot, stunting, mosaic, root galls
and abnormal growth. Disease identification must also distinguish biotic causes such
as fungi, oomycetes, bacteria, viruses, phytoplasmas and nematodes from abiotic
causes such as drought, salinity, nutrient disorders, temperature stress and chemical
injury. A reliable diagnostic approach follows the sequence: identify the crop →
examine the whole plant → record symptoms → look for signs → examine field
pattern → consider environment and history → differentiate biotic and abiotic causes
→ collect samples → confirm by laboratory/molecular methods when required →
select appropriate management.

60. Final Memory
## Crop→Symptom→Sign→Pattern→Environment→History→Confirm→Manag
E          

The four most important rules:
Symptom is not diagnosis.  
Symptom = Plant response; Sign = Pathogen evidence.   Always consider biotic AND abiotic causes.   Correct diagnosis must come before disease management.
  

## Module 16 — Plant Disease
## Management
## 10. Symptoms Of Plant Disease
## 1. Introduction
A symptom is the visible, measurable, or physiological change produced in a plant as a response to a
disease-causing agent or adverse condition.
In plant pathology, symptoms are one of the first things examined during disease identification.
Causal factor→Plant response→Symptom 
For example:
Pathogen infection→Damage/disruption of plant processes→Yellowing, wilt, necrosis, stuntingPathoge
n\ infection  Damage/disruption\ of\ plant\ processes  Yellowing,\ wilt,\ necrosis,\
stunting
However, a symptom does not automatically identify the pathogen.
One symptom≠One disease 
For example, wilting may result from a fungal vascular pathogen, bacterial wilt, nematode damage,
drought, waterlogging, salinity or severe root injury.

2. Definition of Symptom
Simple definition
A symptom is the visible or physiological response of a plant to a disease or disorder.

Plant pathology definition
A symptom is an abnormal change in the appearance, structure, growth, physiology or
function of a plant resulting from infection by a pathogen or from an abiotic disorder.

Most important distinction
SYMPTOM=Plant′s response 
SIGN=Pathogen or pathogen structure 

3. Symptom vs Sign
Feature
Symptom
Sign
Represents Plant response
Pathogen/pathogen structure
Produced by Host response to causal factor Causal organism/material
Example
Yellowing
Fungal spores
Example
Wilt
Mycelium
Example
Necrosis
Bacterial ooze
Example
Stunting
Nematode
Example
Mosaic
Sclerotia

Memory:
Symptom = what the plant does.
Sign = what the pathogen shows.

4. Why Symptoms Occur
Disease-causing factors can interfere with:
- Photosynthesis
- Respiration
- Water transport
- Mineral uptake
- Phloem transport
- Cell division

- Cell expansion
- Hormonal regulation
- Membrane function
- Reproduction
- Tissue integrity
Therefore:
Causal factor→Physiological/structural disturbance→Visible response→SymptomCausal\ factor
 Physiological/structural\ disturbance  Visible\ response  Symptom

5. Major Categories of Plant Disease Symptoms
Plant disease symptoms can broadly be grouped into:
1. Necrotic symptoms
2. Hypoplastic symptoms
3. Hyperplastic symptoms
4. Hypertrophic symptoms
5. Color-related symptoms
6. Wilt symptoms
7. Rot symptoms
8. Growth and developmental abnormalities
9. Root symptoms
10.Reproductive symptoms

6. Necrotic Symptoms
Necrosis means death of plant cells or tissues.
Living tissue→Dead tissue=Necrosis 
Common necrotic symptoms include:
- Leaf spots
- Blight
- Canker

- Dieback
- Scorch
- Lesions
- Rots

## 7. Necrosis
Necrosis is one of the most fundamental disease symptoms.
Dead tissue may appear:
- Brown
- Black
- Gray
- Tan
- Reddish-brown
depending on the plant, pathogen and environment.
Possible causes include:
- Fungi
- Oomycetes
- Bacteria
- Viruses indirectly
- Toxicity
- Heat
- Frost
- Herbicides
- Nutrient disorders

8. Leaf Spot
A leaf spot is a localized area of diseased or dead tissue on a leaf.
Characteristics to examine:
- Shape

- Size
- Color
- Margin
- Center
- Number
- Distribution
- Whether spots enlarge or merge
General progression:
Small lesion→Spot expansion→Spot coalescence→Large necrotic areaSmall\ lesion  Spot\
expansion  Spot\ coalescence  Large\ necrotic\ area

## 9. Lesion
A lesion is a localized area of abnormal or damaged tissue.
It can occur on:
- Leaves
- Stems
- Fruits
- Roots
- Petioles
- Branches
A lesion may be:
- Necrotic
- Sunken
- Raised
- Water-soaked
- Discolored

## 10. Blight
Blight generally describes rapid and extensive death or discoloration of plant tissues, especially leaves,
shoots or flowers.

Typical progression:
Infection→Small lesions→Rapid expansion→Tissue deathInfection  Small\ lesions
 Rapid\ expansion  Tissue\ death
Blight can result from different pathogens, so the word "blight" describes a symptom pattern rather
than a single pathogen.

## 11. Canker
A canker is a localized dead or diseased area, particularly on stems, branches or woody tissues.
Common characteristics:
- Sunken tissue
- Cracks
- Discoloration
- Dead bark
- Margin around the lesion
- Possible branch dieback
Stem/branch→Localized infection→Canker→DiebackStem/branch  Localized\ infection
 Canker  Dieback

## 12. Dieback
Dieback is progressive death of shoots or branches, usually beginning at the tips and moving inward.
Shoot tip→Death→Progression toward baseShoot\ tip  Death  Progression\
toward\ base
Possible causes include:
- Pathogens
- Drought
- Root damage
- Cold injury
- Nutritional stress

## 13. Scorch
Scorch appears as:
- Browning
- Drying
- Burning-like appearance
- ften around leaf margins or tips.
Possible causes include:
- Pathogens
- Heat
- Drought
- Salinity
- Fertilizer injury
- Chemical injury
Thus:
Leaf scorch≠Automatically diseaseLeaf\ scorch Automatically\ disease

## 14. Rot
Rot refers to decomposition and deterioration of plant tissue.
It can occur in:
- Roots
- Stems
- Tubers
- Bulbs
- Fruits
- Seeds
Common forms:

Soft rot
Tissue becomes:
- Soft

- Wet
- Water-soaked
- Easily broken down

Dry rot
Tissue becomes:
- Dry
- Shrunken
- Brittle

15. Root Rot
Root rot causes:
- Root browning
- Root blackening
- Root decay
- Root death
- Reduced root system
Consequences:
Root damage→Water uptake↓→Nutrient uptake↓→Wilting/stuntingRoot\ damage  Water\
uptake  Nutrient\ uptake  Wilting/stunting

16. Hypoplastic Symptoms
Hypoplasia refers to underdevelopment or reduced development of plant tissues.
Examples include:
- Stunting
- Little leaf
- Reduced internode length
- Reduced organ size
- Poor development
Conceptually:

Normal development→Development suppressed→Hypoplastic symptomNormal\ development
 Development\ suppressed  Hypoplastic\ symptom

## 17. Stunting
Stunting means reduced overall plant growth.
Affected plants may be:
- Shorter
- Smaller
- Less vigorous
- Delayed in development
Possible causes:
- Viruses
- Phytoplasmas
- Nematodes
- Root pathogens
- Nutrient deficiency
- Drought
- Salinity
Therefore:
Stunting is a symptom, not a specific diagnosis. 

18. Little Leaf
Leaves are unusually small.
This is particularly associated with some phytoplasma diseases.
Phytoplasma→Developmental disturbance→Little leafPhytoplasma  Developmental\
disturbance  Little\ leaf
But diagnosis requires additional evidence.

19. Reduced Internode Length
Internodes become shorter than normal.
This can produce a compact or dwarf appearance.
Internode length↓→Plant height↓Internode\ length  Plant\ height It may occur in certain viral, phytoplasma and physiological disorders.

20. Hyperplastic Symptoms
Hyperplasia refers to an increase in the number of cells or abnormal tissue proliferation.
Examples include:
- Galls
- Excessive branching
- Witches' broom
- Some abnormal growths
Cell proliferation↑→Abnormal tissue growthCell\ proliferation  Abnormal\ tissue\
growth

## 21. Gall
A gall is an abnormal swelling or enlargement of plant tissue.
Galls can occur on:
- Roots
- Stems
- Leaves
- Buds
A classic example is:
Meloidogyne→Root galls 
But galls can also be caused by insects, fungi, bacteria and other organisms.
Therefore:
Gall≠Automatically nematodeGall Automatically\ nematode

22. Witches' Broom
Witches' broom is characterized by excessive development of shoots or branches from a particular
region.
Abnormal shoot proliferation→Broom−like structureAbnormal\ shoot\ proliferation  Broom-like\ structure
It is strongly associated with some phytoplasma diseases, although other causes can produce similar
abnormal branching.

23. Hypertrophic Symptoms
Hypertrophy means enlargement of individual cells.
Conceptually:
Cell size↑→Tissue enlargementCell\ size  Tissue\ enlargement
Hyperplasia and hypertrophy may occur together.
Hyperplasia=Cell number↑ 
Hypertrophy=Cell size↑ 

24. Color Changes
Changes in normal plant color are extremely common symptoms.
Major examples:
- Chlorosis
- Yellowing
- Mottling
- Mosaic
- Reddening
- Purpling
- Bronzing

## 25. Chlorosis
Chlorosis means abnormal loss or reduction of green color, usually associated with reduced
chlorophyll.

Green→YellowGreen  Yellow
Possible causes:
- Nutrient deficiency
- Virus
- Phytoplasma
- Root disease
- Salinity
- Waterlogging
- Herbicide injury

26. General Yellowing
Yellowing can occur in:
- Leaves
- Veins
- Whole plant
Important:
Yellowing alone is diagnostically weak. 
Always examine:
- Distribution
- Plant age
- Soil
- Roots
- Disease signs
- Environmental conditions

27. Vein Chlorosis
Yellowing occurs mainly around or between veins.
Depending on the pattern:
- Interveinal chlorosis

- Veinal chlorosis
may provide clues about the underlying cause.

## 28. Mosaic
A mosaic is an irregular pattern of differently colored areas, commonly:
- Dark green
- Light green
- Yellow-green
It is commonly associated with viral diseases.
Mosaic→Important viral symptom 
But confirmation requires appropriate testing.

## 29. Mottling
Mottling is irregular discoloration with patches or spots of different shades.
It is less sharply defined than some mosaic patterns.
Mottling can occur with:
- Viruses
- Nutrient disorders
- Environmental stress

30. Reddening and Purpling
Some diseased plants may develop:
- Red leaves
- Purple leaves
- Purple veins
Possible causes include:
- Physiological stress
- Nutritional problems
- Pathogens

- Temperature stress
Again:
Color change≠Specific pathogenColor\ change Specific\ pathogen

## 31. Wilt
Wilting is loss of normal turgidity.
Loss of turgor→Wilt 
It occurs when water loss exceeds water uptake or when water transport is disrupted.
Possible causes:
- Drought
- Vascular pathogens
- Root rot
- Nematodes
- Waterlogging
- Salinity
- Root injury

32. Vascular Wilt
A vascular disease can interfere with water transport through vascular tissues.
General concept:
Root→Xylem→Stem→LeavesRoot  Xylem  Stem  Leaves
If vascular function is disrupted:
Water transport↓→Leaf water deficit→WiltingWater\ transport  Leaf\ water\
deficit  Wilting

## 33. Damping-Off
Damping-off is a disease symptom complex affecting seedlings.

Pre-emergence
Seed infection→Seedling death→No emergenceSeed\ infection  Seedling\ death  No\ emergence

Post-emergence
Seedling→Stem infection near soil line→Stem weakening→CollapseSeedling  Stem\
infection\ near\ soil\ line  Stem\ weakening  Collapse

34. Leaf Curling
Leaves may curl:
- Upward
- Downward
- Inward
- Outward
Potential causes include:
- Viruses
- Insect feeding
- Herbicides
- Nutrient disorders
- Water stress

35. Leaf Rolling
Leaf margins may roll toward or away from the midrib.
It can result from:
- Water stress
- Viral infection
- Physiological disorders
- Herbicide injury
- Other stresses

36. Leaf Distortion
Leaves may become:
- Twisted

- Crinkled
- Narrow
- Deformed
- Unevenly expanded
Common associations include:
- Viral infection
- Hormonal disruption
- Phytoplasma
- Herbicide injury

37. Necrotic Margin
Leaf margins may become brown or dead.
Possible causes include:
- Disease
- Salinity
- Potassium deficiency
- Drought
- Fertilizer injury
- Heat
Therefore:
Brown margin≠Automatically fungal diseaseBrown\ margin Automatically\ fungal\ disease

38. Water-Soaked Appearance
Affected tissue may appear:
- Translucent
- Wet
- Darker than normal
- Saturated
Water-soaked lesions are often associated with some bacterial and oomycete diseases.

39. Tissue Collapse
Diseased tissue may lose structural integrity and collapse.
This can occur during:
- Soft rot
- Damping-off
- Severe blight
- Vascular failure

40. Abnormal Root Symptoms
Root symptoms include:
- Galls
- Lesions
- Root rot
- Root pruning
- Root discoloration
- Root necrosis
- Reduced root hairs
- Root swelling
- Root cracking
These symptoms are particularly important because root diseases may produce only indirect aboveground symptoms.

41. Root Galling
Root swelling/galls 
Important possible causes:
- Root-knot nematodes
- Certain bacteria
- Fungi

- Insects
Therefore laboratory examination may be necessary.

42. Root Lesions
Root lesions appear as:
- Brown
- Black
- Reddish-brown
- Necrotic
areas.
Possible causes include:
- Nematodes
- Fungi
- Oomycetes
- Other soil-borne pathogens

43. Flower Symptoms
Diseases can affect:
- Flower initiation
- Flower development
- Flower color
- Flower shape
- Fertility
- Pollination
- Fruit set
Examples:
Phytoplasma→PhyllodyPhytoplasma Phyllody

## 44. Phyllody
Phyllody is the abnormal transformation/development of floral organs into leaf-like structures.
Flower organ→Leaf-like structureFlower\ organ  Leaf like\ structure
It is a characteristic symptom associated with some phytoplasmas.

45. Fruit Symptoms
Fruit symptoms include:
- Rot
- Spots
- Cracks
- Deformation
- Discoloration
- Premature drop
- Poor development
- Internal browning

46. Seed Symptoms
Diseases may cause:
- Seed discoloration
- Shriveling
- Poor development
- Reduced germination
- Seed rot
- Abnormal seed size
Seed-borne pathogens can therefore become an important source of primary inoculum.

47. Reproductive Symptoms
Disease can reduce:

- Flowering
- Pollination success
- Fertilization
- Fruit set
- Seed formation
- Grain filling
Thus:
Disease→Reproductive disruption→Yield lossDisease  Reproductive\ disruption
 Yield\ loss

48. Symptom Development Over Time
Symptoms can change as disease progresses.
A generalized sequence may be:
Initial infection→Early symptom→Lesion/deformation→Expansion→Severe damage→Tissue death - xed  Initial\ infection  Early\ symptom  Lesion/deformation  Expansion  Severe\ damage  Tissue\ death  
The progression rate can provide diagnostic information.

49. Primary vs Secondary Symptoms
Primary symptom
Occurs directly at or near the initial infection/damage site.

Secondary symptom
