Appears elsewhere because of systemic effects or physiological consequences.
For example:
Root infection→Root damage→Water uptake↓→Leaf wiltingRoot\ infection  Root\ damage
 Water\ uptake  Leaf\ wilting
The root disease is primary, while leaf wilting is a secondary consequence.

50. Local vs Systemic Symptoms
Local symptoms
Restricted to the area initially affected.
Examples:
- Leaf spot
- Local lesion
- Local canker

Systemic symptoms
Spread throughout or affect multiple parts of the plant.
Examples:
- General stunting
- Systemic yellowing
- Widespread mosaic
- Systemic wilt
Local→Restricted 
Systemic→Multiple plant regions 

51. Disease Symptoms and Plant Physiology
Symptoms often reflect disruption of major physiological processes.

Photosynthesis
Disease→Leaf damage→Photosynthesis↓→Growth↓Disease  Leaf\ damage  Photosynthesis  Growth Water transport
Root/vascular damage→Water transport↓→WiltRoot/vascular\ damage  Water\
transport  Wilt

Nutrient uptake
Root damage→Nutrient uptake↓→Chlorosis/stuntingRoot\ damage  Nutrient\
uptake  Chlorosis/stunting

Phloem function
Phloem disruption→Carbohydrate/signaling imbalance→Growth abnormalitiesPhloem\ disruption
 Carbohydrate/signaling\ imbalance  Growth\ abnormalities

52. Symptoms Caused by Different Pathogen
Groups
Pathogen group
Common symptom associations
Fungi
Leaf spots, rusts, wilts, rots, blights
Oomycetes
Blights, downy mildew, damping-off, rots
Bacteria
Blights, spots, wilts, cankers, soft rots
Viruses
Mosaic, mottling, curling, ringspots, stunting
Viroids
Stunting, deformation, abnormal growth
Phytoplasmas
Little leaf, witches' broom, phyllody, yellowing
Nematodes
Galls, lesions, root pruning, stunting, wilting
These are associations—not exclusive rules.

53. Symptoms and Abiotic Disorders
Symptoms may also be caused by nonliving factors.
Abiotic factor
Possible symptoms
Drought
Wilting, leaf rolling, scorch
Waterlogging
Yellowing, root death, wilting
Salinity
Leaf burn, chlorosis, stunting
Heat
Scorch, wilting, tissue injury
Frost
Necrosis, blackening, collapse
Nutrient deficiency Chlorosis, stunting, abnormal growth
Nutrient toxicity
Burn, chlorosis, necrosis
Herbicide injury
Curling, distortion, chlorosis, necrosis
Air pollution
Leaf injury, chlorosis, necrosis
This is why:
Symptom observation must include abiotic differential diagnosis. 

54. Symptom Pattern Is More Valuable Than a
Single Symptom
Suppose a crop shows:
Yellowing + root galls + patchy distribution + poor growth
This is much more informative than:
Yellowing
Therefore:
Diagnostic value↑when multiple compatible observations are combined 

55. Symptom Identification Checklist
When examining a diseased plant, record:

Color
- Green
- Yellow
- Brown
- Black
- Red
- Purple
- Gray

Shape
- Circular
- Irregular
- Angular
- Linear
- Elongated
- Sunken
- Raised

Location
- Leaf
- Stem
- Root
- Flower
- Fruit
- Seed

Distribution
- Random
- Patchy
- Uniform
- Row-based
- Edge-based

Progression
- Slow
- Rapid
- Expanding
- Static

Severity
- Mild
- Moderate
- Severe

56. Disease Identification Using Symptoms
A practical diagnostic sequence is:
## Crop→Plant Part→Symptom→Pattern→Progression→Signs→Environment
→CONFIRMATION 
57. Example: Wilting
Observation:
WiltingWilting
Possible causes:
Drought ∣ Vascular disease ∣ Root rot ∣ Nematode ∣ Waterlogging ∣ Salinity 
Therefore examine:
- Soil moisture
- Roots
- Vascular tissue
- Field distribution
- Pathogen signs
- Nematode population
- Weather

58. Example: Yellowing
Observation:
YellowingYellowing
Possible causes:
- Nitrogen deficiency
- Iron deficiency
- Root disease
- Virus
- Phytoplasma
- Waterlogging
- Salinity
Therefore:
Yellowing→Pattern+Stage+Roots+Soil+Signs+TestingYellowing  Pattern+Stage+Roots+Soil+Signs+Testing

59. Example: Root Galls
Observation:
Root gallingRoot\ galling
Potential causes:
- Root-knot nematodes
- Certain bacterial diseases
- Other organisms
Confirmation:
Root examination+Soil/root nematode extraction+Microscopy/molecular testingRoot\ examination +
Soil/root\ nematode\ extraction + Microscopy/molecular\ testing

60. Example: Mosaic
Observation:
MosaicMosaic
Likely consideration:
VirusVirus
But also examine:
- Vector presence
- Plant stage
- Distribution
- Other symptoms
- Laboratory testing

61. Example: Witches' Broom
Observation:
Excessive abnormal branchingExcessive\ abnormal\ branching
Possible consideration:
PhytoplasmaPhytoplasma
But confirmation should consider:

- Little leaf
- Phyllody
- Vector presence
- Field pattern
- Molecular detection

62. Disease Symptoms and Yield
Symptoms ultimately matter because they can reduce:
- Photosynthetic area
- Water uptake
- Nutrient uptake
- Reproductive development
- Fruit/seed formation
- Biomass accumulation
Thus:
Disease→Physiological disruption→Growth reduction→Yield/quality lossDisease  Physiological\ disruption  Growth\ reduction  Yield/quality\ loss

63. Important Concept — Symptom ≠ Pathogen
This is perhaps the most important examination concept.
SYMPTOM describes WHAT THE PLANT LOOKS LIKE 
It does not necessarily tell you:
WHAT CAUSED IT 
For that, you need:
Symptoms+Signs+Pattern+Environment+History+ConfirmationSymptoms+Signs+Pattern+Environme
nt+History+Confirmation

64. Common Mistakes
Mistake 1
“Yellow leaf = nitrogen deficiency.”

❌ Not necessarily.
Mistake 2

“Brown spot = fungal disease.”

❌ Not necessarily.
Mistake 3

“Wilt = vascular fungus.”

❌ Could have many causes.
Mistake 4
“Gall = nematode.”

❌ Not every gall is caused by nematodes.
Mistake 5
“Mosaic = automatically one particular virus.”

❌ Multiple viruses and other conditions can produce mosaic-like patterns.
Mistake 6
“Witches' broom = phytoplasma without confirmation.”

❌ It is an important clue, not absolute proof.

65. High-Yield Classification
Necrotic
Death of tissue 
Examples:
- Leaf spot
- Blight
- Canker
- Dieback

- Rot

Hypoplastic
Reduced development 
Examples:
- Stunting
- Little leaf
- Dwarfing

Hyperplastic
Excessive cell proliferation 
Examples:
- Galls
- Abnormal branching

Hypertrophic
Cell enlargement 
May contribute to abnormal tissue enlargement.

Color symptoms
Chlorosis+Mosaic+Mottling+Reddening+Purpling 

Wilt
Loss of turgidity 

66. Final Symptom Identification Framework
WHAT IS WRONG?   
↓
Color
↓
Shape
↓
Plant part

↓
Tissue condition
↓
Growth pattern
↓
Distribution
↓
Progression
↓
Signs
↓
Environment
↓
History
↓
Laboratory confirmation

67. Exam-Ready Answer
A symptom is the visible, physiological or structural response of a plant to a diseasecausing agent or abiotic disorder. Important plant disease symptoms include chlorosis,
necrosis, leaf spots, blight, canker, dieback, rot, wilt, damping-off, stunting, little leaf,
mosaic, mottling, leaf curling, distortion, gall formation, witches' broom and phyllody.
Symptoms may be classified broadly as necrotic symptoms, hypoplastic symptoms,
hyperplastic symptoms, hypertrophic symptoms, color abnormalities and wilting
symptoms. Symptoms may be local or systemic and primary or secondary. Correct
identification requires examining not only the symptom but also the affected plant
part, disease distribution, progression, crop stage, environmental conditions, field
history and pathogen signs. Because the same symptom can result from different
biotic and abiotic causes, symptoms alone are generally insufficient for definitive
diagnosis.

68. Final Memory Map
SYMPTOMS   

Necrotic
Leaf spots+Blight+Canker+Dieback+RotLeaf\ spots+Blight+Canker+Dieback+Rot

Hypoplastic
Stunting+Little leaf+DwarfingStunting+Little\ leaf+Dwarfing

Hyperplastic
Galls+Excessive branchingGalls+Excessive\ branching

Hypertrophic
Cell/tissue enlargementCell/tissue\ enlargement

Color
Chlorosis+Mosaic+Mottling+Reddening+PurplingChlorosis+Mosaic+Mottling+Reddening+Purpling

Water/vascular
Wilt+Water-soakingWilt+Water soaking

Developmental
Curling+Distortion+Phyllody+Witches′ broomCurling+Distortion+Phyllody+Witches'\ broom

Root
Galls+Lesions+Rot+PruningGalls+Lesions+Rot+Pruning
And the most important rule:
SYMPTOM = PLANT RESPONSE   
SIGN = PATHOGEN EVIDENCE   
   
SYMPTOMS + SIGNS + PATTERN + ENVIRONMENT + HISTORY + CONFIRMATION  wCORRECT DIAGNOSIS   

## Module 16 — Plant Disease
## Management
## 11. Signs Of Plant Disease
## 1. Introduction
In plant pathology, a sign is the actual presence of a pathogen, its body, reproductive structure, or
pathogen-produced material that can be observed on or in a diseased plant.
This is fundamentally different from a symptom.
Sign = Evidence of the pathogen  
while:
Symptom = Response of the plant  
For example:
- Yellowing → symptom
- Wilting → symptom
- Fungal spores visible on a leaf → sign
- Fungal mycelium → sign
- Bacterial ooze → sign
- Nematodes observed in roots/soil → sign

2. Definition of Sign
Simple definition
A sign is the visible presence of a pathogen, pathogen structure, or pathogen-produced
material associated with a diseased plant.

Exam-ready definition
A sign is the physical evidence of the causal pathogen or its structures present on or
within diseased plant tissue, such as fungal mycelium, spores, fruiting bodies,
bacterial ooze, sclerotia, nematodes or other pathogen structures.

3. Sign vs Symptom
This distinction is extremely important.
Feature
Symptom
Sign
Represents Plant response Pathogen evidence
Origin
Host plant
Pathogen/causal organism
Example Yellowing
Fungal spores
Example Wilt
Mycelium
Example Necrosis
Bacterial ooze
Example Stunting
Nematode
Example Mosaic
Sclerotia

Memory rule
SYMPTOM=WHAT THE PLANT DOES 
SIGN=WHAT THE PATHOGEN SHOWS 

4. Why Signs Are Important
Signs are extremely valuable for disease identification because they provide more direct evidence of
the causal organism.
For example:
Leaf yellowingLeaf\ yellowing
could have many causes.
But:
Leaf yellowing+visible fungal sporesLeaf\ yellowing+visible\ fungal\ spores
provides much stronger evidence for a fungal disease.
Therefore:
Symptoms→Clues 
Signs→Direct pathogen evidence 
However, even a sign may require confirmation because a visible organism can sometimes be a
secondary colonizer rather than the primary cause.

5. Major Types of Disease Signs
Important signs include:
## 1. Mycelium
## 2. Spores
3. Fruiting bodies
## 4. Sclerotia
## 5. Sporangia
## 6. Sporangiophores
## 7. Conidiophores
8. Bacterial ooze
9. Bacterial streaming
10.Nematodes
11.Nematode eggs
12.Cysts
13.Pathogen structures inside tissues
14.Virus inclusion bodies in specialized diagnostic contexts

6. Fungal Signs
Fungi often produce highly visible structures.
General sequence:
Fungal infection→Growth→Reproductive structures→SporesFungal\ infection  Growth
 Reproductive\ structures  Spores
Common fungal signs include:
- Mycelium
- Spores
- Fruiting bodies
- Sclerotia
- Sporangiophores
- Conidiophores
- Acervuli
- Pycnidia
- Perithecia
- Cleistothecia/chasmothecia

- Rust pustules
- Smut sori

## 7. Mycelium
Mycelium is the network of fungal hyphae.
Hyphae→Mycelium 
It may appear as:
- White growth
- Gray growth
- Cottony growth
- Powdery growth
- Dense fungal mats
Mycelium can occur:
- On leaf surfaces
- Inside tissues
- On roots
- On fruits
- On seeds
- On dead plant residues

## 8. Hypha
A hypha is an individual filamentous structure of a fungus.
Many hyphae collectively form:
Hyphae→Mycelium 
Microscopic examination may be required to observe hyphae clearly.

## 9. Spores
Spores are reproductive or survival structures produced by many fungi and fungus-like organisms.
They can be:

- Sexual
- Asexual
- Airborne
- Water-dispersed
- Soil-borne
General disease cycle:
Spore→Germination→Penetration→Infection→New sporesSpore  Germination  Penetration  Infection  New\ spores
Thus:
Spores=Important source of inoculum 

10. Powdery Growth
Some fungal diseases produce powdery white or gray growth on plant surfaces.
The visible powder may contain:
- Mycelium
- Conidiophores
- Conidia
This can be an important field sign.
However:
White powder≠Automatically one particular fungusWhite\ powder Automatically\ one\ particular\
fungus
Microscopic or laboratory confirmation may be needed.

11. Fruiting Bodies
Some fungi produce specialized reproductive structures called fruiting bodies.
They may contain or produce spores.
Examples include structures such as:
- Pycnidia
- Acervuli
- Perithecia

- Apothecia
- Other sexual/asexual reproductive structures
Their presence can provide strong diagnostic evidence.

## 12. Pycnidia
Pycnidia are flask- or spherical-shaped asexual reproductive structures of some fungi.
They can contain conidia.
Under suitable conditions:
Pycnidium→Conidia→DisseminationPycnidium  Conidia  Dissemination
They may appear as small dark dots on diseased tissue.

## 13. Acervuli
Acervuli are subepidermal or embedded asexual fruiting structures produced by some fungi.
They can produce large numbers of conidia.
They may appear as:
- Small dark structures
- Slightly raised areas
- Structures that rupture the plant epidermis

14. Rust Pustules
Rust diseases can produce characteristic pustules containing spores.
Typical appearance may include:
- Orange
- Yellow
- Brown
- Reddish-brown
- Black
pustules depending on rust stage.
Rust pustule=Important fungal sign 

15. Smut Sori
Smut fungi can produce masses of dark spores called sori.
They may replace:
- Grains
- Flowers
- Seeds
- Other reproductive tissues
The dark powdery spore mass is an important sign.

## 16. Sclerotia
A sclerotium is a compact mass of hardened fungal tissue that helps the fungus survive unfavorable
conditions.
Sclerotium=Survival structure 
It may be:
- Black
- Brown
- Dark
- Hard
Sclerotia can survive in:
- Soil
- Plant residues
- Seeds
- Infected tissues

## 17. Sporangia
A sporangium is a structure in which spores are produced in certain groups of organisms, including
- omycetes.
Some oomycetes can produce sporangia under suitable environmental conditions.

Sporangium→Spores/zoospores→InfectionSporangium  Spores/zoospores  Infection

## 18. Sporangiophores
A sporangiophore is a specialized structure that bears sporangia.
The combination of:
Sporangiophore+SporangiumSporangiophore+Sporangium
can be diagnostically useful.

## 19. Conidiophores
A conidiophore is a specialized fungal hypha that produces or bears conidia.
Conidiophore→ConidiaConidiophore  Conidia
Conidia are asexual fungal spores.

20. Bacterial Signs
Bacterial diseases may produce different kinds of signs from fungi.
The most important field sign is often:
Bacterial ooze 
Other evidence can include bacterial streaming under suitable microscopy procedures.

21. Bacterial Ooze
Bacterial ooze is a fluid or slimy material containing large numbers of bacterial cells that emerges from
infected plant tissue.
It may appear:
- Creamy
- White
- Yellowish
- Sticky
- Slimy

It can occur from:
- Lesions
- Cankers
- Cut stems
- Vascular tissues
- Water-soaked areas

22. Bacterial Streaming
Bacterial streaming is a diagnostic technique in which bacterial cells may be observed streaming from
infected vascular tissue into water under suitable conditions.
Conceptually:
Infected vascular tissue→Water→Bacterial streamingInfected\ vascular\ tissue  Water
 Bacterial\ streaming
It can support a bacterial diagnosis, but laboratory confirmation may still be necessary.

23. Bacterial Biofilm or Slime
Some bacterial diseases can produce slimy material associated with infected tissue.
This can be particularly evident in:
- Soft rots
- Vascular infections
- Wet conditions
Again, visible slime alone should not be treated as absolute proof of a specific bacterium.

24. Nematode Signs
Plant-parasitic nematodes are microscopic or very small animals, so their signs may require:
- Root examination
- Soil sampling
- Extraction
- Microscopy

Important signs include:
- Adult nematodes
- Juveniles
- Eggs
- Egg masses
- Cysts

## 25. Nematodes
Nematodes are microscopic, elongated, unsegmented roundworms.
Some species are plant parasites.
They may occur in:
- Soil
- Roots
- Root surfaces
- Plant tissues
Nematode detection+compatible root symptoms→Strong diagnostic evidence 

26. Nematode Eggs
Nematode eggs may be found:
- Inside roots
- On root surfaces
- In egg masses
- In soil
Eggs may contribute to the survival and spread of the nematode population.

27. Nematode Egg Masses
Some root-knot nematodes produce egg masses associated with roots.
The sequence is approximately:

Egg→Juvenile→Adult→Egg productionEgg  Juvenile  Adult  Egg\
production
Egg masses can be useful for diagnosis.

28. Nematode Cysts
Some nematodes produce females that become cyst-like structures containing eggs.
These cysts may remain in soil after the crop is removed.
Therefore:
Cyst→Survival→Future nematode populationCyst  Survival  Future\ nematode\
population

29. Root Galls — Symptom or Sign?
This distinction is important.
A root gall itself is primarily a symptom, because it is abnormal plant tissue.
For example:
Nematode→Root feeding→Plant tissue enlargement→Root gallNematode  Root\ feeding
 Plant\ tissue\ enlargement  Root\ gall
The nematode itself or its eggs are the sign.
Thus:
Root gall=Symptom  Nematode/eggs=Sign 

30. Viral Disease Signs
Viruses are fundamentally different from many fungi and bacteria because individual virus particles are
generally not visible to the naked eye.
Therefore viral diseases often have:

Symptoms
- Mosaic
- Mottling
- Curling
- Ringspots

- Stunting
- Deformation
rather than obvious field-visible signs.
Confirmation may require:
## • Elisa
- PCR
- RT-PCR
- Electron microscopy in specialized settings
- Sequencing

31. Virus Inclusion Bodies
Some viruses produce characteristic inclusion bodies inside plant cells.
These can sometimes be observed microscopically and provide diagnostic clues.
However:
Virus symptom≠Visible virus particleVirus\ symptom  Visible\ virus\ particle
Molecular or serological confirmation is frequently more reliable.

32. Phytoplasma Signs
Phytoplasmas are very small, wall-less bacteria-like organisms associated primarily with phloem
tissues.
Unlike fungal diseases, they generally do not produce obvious field-visible structures such as spores or
mycelium.
Therefore diagnosis often relies on:
- Symptoms
- Vector association
- Molecular detection
Important symptoms include:
- Little leaf
- Witches' broom

- Phyllody
- Yellowing
- Stunting
These are symptoms, not direct visible phytoplasma signs.

33. Phytoplasma Confirmation
Because phytoplasmas are difficult to detect visually:
Symptoms+Vectorevidence+Molecular testing→ConfirmationSymptoms + Vector evidence +
Molecular\ testing  Confirmation
PCR-based methods are commonly used for confirmation.

34. Signs Associated With Different Pathogens
Pathogen group
Important signs/evidence
Fungi
Mycelium, spores, fruiting bodies, sclerotia
Oomycetes
Sporangia, sporangiophores, oospores in relevant diseases
Bacteria
Ooze, bacterial streaming, bacterial colonies after isolation
Viruses
Usually no naked-eye sign; inclusion bodies/molecular evidence
Phytoplasmas
Usually no obvious field-visible sign; molecular detection
Nematodes
Nematodes, eggs, egg masses, cysts
Parasitic plants Visible parasite structures such as haustorial connections in some cases

35. Sign and Disease Identification
A useful diagnostic sequence is:
Symptom→Search for Sign→Identify Pathogen Group→Confirm 
For example:

Case 1
Leaf spot+Fungal sporesLeaf\ spot + Fungal\ spores
↓
Fungal disease becomes more likely.

Case 2
Wilt+Bacterial oozeWilt + Bacterial\ ooze
↓
Bacterial disease becomes more likely.

Case 3
Root gall+Nematodes/eggsRoot\ gall + Nematodes/eggs
↓
Nematode involvement becomes more likely.

36. Direct vs Indirect Evidence
Direct evidence
Actual pathogen or pathogen structure:
- Spores
- Mycelium
- Bacterial cells/ooze
- Nematodes

Indirect evidence
Evidence associated with pathogen activity:
- Disease pattern
- Vector presence
- Environmental conditions
- Disease progression
Direct evidence>Indirect evidence 
for pathogen confirmation in many situations, but both are useful.

37. Primary Pathogen vs Secondary Colonizer
This is an important diagnostic concept.
A microorganism found on diseased tissue is not necessarily the original cause.

For example:
Primary pathogen→Tissue damage/death→Secondary microorganismsPrimary\ pathogen  Tissue\ damage/death  Secondary\ microorganisms
Dead tissue can be colonized by many microorganisms.
Therefore:
Microorganism detected≠Automatically causal pathogen 

38. Signs and Disease Cycle
Signs are closely connected with pathogen reproduction and survival.
General disease cycle:
Survival→Inoculum→Infection→Colonization→Reproduction→New signs→DisseminationSurvival
 Inoculum  Infection  Colonization  Reproduction
 New\ signs  Dissemination
For fungi:
Mycelium→Spores→SpreadMycelium  Spores  Spread
For bacteria:
Bacterial multiplication→Ooze→DisseminationBacterial\ multiplication  Ooze  Dissemination
For nematodes:
Egg→Juvenile→Adult→EggsEgg  Juvenile  Adult  Eggs

39. Signs as Sources of Inoculum
Many signs are also sources of future infection.
For example:
Fungal spores→Wind/rain→Healthy plant→New infectionFungal\ spores  Wind/rain
 Healthy\ plant  New\ infection
Therefore signs are not merely diagnostic; they may be epidemiologically important.

40. Signs and Disease Spread
Wind
Can disperse:
- Fungal spores
- Some plant debris

Rain
Can disperse:
- Spores
- Bacterial cells

Irrigation
Can spread:
- Soil-borne pathogens
- Waterborne inoculum

Soil
Can carry:
- Sclerotia
- Resting spores
- Nematodes
- Infected residues

41. Signs and Sampling
When signs are suspected, collect appropriate material.

Leaf disease
Collect:
- Diseased leaves
- Healthy/diseased transition tissue
- Visible structures

Root disease
Collect:
- Roots
- Crown
- Associated soil

Fruit disease
Collect:
- Fresh lesion
- Adjacent healthy tissue
- Any visible pathogen structures

Nematode problem
Collect:
- Roots
- Rhizosphere soil

42. Signs and Laboratory Confirmation
Field signs can provide strong clues, but laboratory confirmation may be needed.

Microscopy
Useful for:
- Spores
- Mycelium
- Fungal structures
- Nematodes

Culture
Useful for isolating certain:
- Fungi
- Bacteria

Serology
Useful for some:

- Viruses

Molecular methods
- PCR
- RT-PCR
- qPCR
- Sequencing
Useful for confirming many:
- Bacteria
- Viruses
- Phytoplasmas
- Fungi
- Other pathogens

43. Signs vs Symptoms — Examples
Observation
Classification
Yellow leaves
Symptom
Wilt
Symptom
Leaf spot
Symptom
Stunting
Symptom
Root gall
Symptom
Mosaic
Symptom
Mycelium
Sign
Fungal spores
Sign
Sclerotia
Sign
Bacterial ooze
Sign
Nematode
Sign
Nematode eggs
Sign
Cyst
Sign
Virus inclusion body Diagnostic sign/evidence in appropriate context

44. Important Diagnostic Combinations
Fungal disease
Lesion+Mycelium/Spores+Microscopy 

Bacterial disease
Water-soaked lesion+Ooze+Laboratory confirmation soaked\
lesion+Ooze+Laboratory\ confirmation  

Nematode disease
Root symptoms+Nematodes/Eggs/Cysts 

Viral disease
Mosaic/Distortion+Vector evidence+Serological/Molecular confirmation 

Phytoplasma disease
Little leaf/Witches′ broom/Phyllody+Molecular confirmation 

45. Why Some Diseases Have Few Visible Signs
Some pathogens are microscopic or occur mainly inside plant tissues.
For example:

Viruses
Virus particles→Usually invisible to naked eyeVirus\ particles Usually\ invisible\ to\ naked\
eye

Phytoplasmas
Phytoplasma→Phloem−associated microscopic organismPhytoplasma Phloem-associated\
microscopic\ organism
Therefore:
No visible sign≠No pathogenNo\ visible\ sign No\ pathogen
Laboratory techniques may be necessary.

46. Sign-Based Diagnostic Workflow
## Diseased Plant
↓
## Observe Symptoms
↓
## Look For Visible Signs
↓
## Mycelium / Spores / Fruiting Body?
↓
YES → Consider fungal/oomycete cause
↓
## Bacterial Ooze?
↓
YES → Consider bacterial cause
↓
## Nematodes / Eggs / Cysts?
↓
YES → Consider nematode cause
↓
## No Obvious Sign?
↓
Consider viruses, phytoplasmas, abiotic disorders
↓
Laboratory / molecular confirmation

47. Common Mistakes
Mistake 1: Calling every symptom a sign
Incorrect.
Yellowing is a symptom, not a sign.

Mistake 2: Calling a root gall a nematode sign
The gall is primarily a plant symptom.
The nematode or eggs are the sign.

Mistake 3: Assuming visible fungus is always the causal pathogen
A fungus may be a secondary colonizer.

Mistake 4: Assuming no visible sign means no disease
Viruses and phytoplasmas often require laboratory confirmation.

Mistake 5: Ignoring microscopic signs
Nematodes and fungal structures may require microscopy.

Mistake 6: Treating before identification
Finding a sign should lead to diagnosis, not automatically to pesticide application.

48. Practical Field Observation Table
Observation
What to ask
Mycelium
What type, color, location?
Spores
Shape, color, arrangement?
Fruiting body Which structure?
Sclerotia
Size, color, location?
Ooze
Where does it emerge?
Nematode
Which life stage?
Eggs
Where located?
Cysts
Present in soil/root zone?
Inclusion body Microscopic confirmation?

49. Golden Diagnostic Principle
Disease identification should combine:
SYMPTOMS+SIGNS+HOST+PATTERN+ENVIRONMENT+HISTORY 
and when necessary:
+LABORATORY/MOLECULAR CONFIRMATION 
The complete chain is:
Observation→Evidence→Identification→Diagnosis→Management 

50. Exam-Ready Answer
A sign is the actual physical evidence of a plant pathogen or its structures present on
- r within diseased plant tissue. Unlike symptoms, which represent the plant's response
to disease, signs represent the pathogen itself or materials produced by it. Important
signs include fungal mycelium, spores, fruiting bodies, sclerotia, sporangia,

sporangiophores, conidiophores, bacterial ooze, bacterial streaming, nematodes,
nematode eggs, egg masses and cysts. Viral and phytoplasma diseases often do not
produce obvious field-visible signs and therefore may require serological or molecular
confirmation. Signs are valuable in disease diagnosis because they provide more direct
evidence of the causal organism, but the organism detected must be confirmed as the
primary causal agent rather than a secondary colonizer.

51. Final Memory Map
SIGNS = PATHOGEN EVIDENCE  

Fungi / Oomycetes
Mycelium+Spores+Fruiting bodies+Sclerotia+Sporangia 

Bacteria
Ooze+Streaming+Isolated bacterial colonies 

Nematodes
Nematodes+Eggs+Egg masses+Cysts 

Viruses
Usually no naked-eye sign→Serology/Molecular confirmation eye\
sign  Serology/Molecular\ confirmation  

Phytoplasmas
Usually no obvious field-visible sign→Molecular confirmation visible\ sign  Molecular\ confirmation  
And the most important distinction:
SYMPTOM = PLANT RESPONSE   
SIGN = PATHOGEN OR PATHOGEN STRUCTURE   
SIGN + SYMPTOM + PATTERN + ENVIRONMENT  IDENTIFICATION d     
## Module 16 — Plant Disease
## Management
## 12. Disease Cycle
## 1. Introduction
The disease cycle is the sequence of events through which a plant pathogen survives, reaches a host,
infects the plant, develops, reproduces, and spreads to new hosts.
In simple terms:
Disease cycle = How a disease survives, develops, reproduces and spreads   
A pathogen does not simply “appear” on a crop. It passes through a series of biological and
epidemiological stages.

Master sequence
Survival→Dissemination→Inoculation→Penetration→Infection→Colonization→Symptom Developm
ent→Reproduction→New Inoculum→Dissemination 
This cycle may repeat multiple times during one growing season.

2. Definition of Disease Cycle
Simple definition
Disease cycle is the sequence of events involved in the survival, spread, infection,
development, reproduction and dissemination of a plant pathogen.

Exam-ready definition
The disease cycle is the series of successive events by which a plant pathogen survives
between host generations or seasons, produces or reaches inoculum, contacts a
susceptible host, penetrates and infects it, colonizes the host, produces symptoms and
reproductive structures, generates new inoculum and disseminates it to other
susceptible hosts.

3. Disease Cycle vs Disease Triangle
These are related but different concepts.

Disease triangle
Explains what conditions are required for disease development:
Host+Pathogen+Environment→Disease 

Disease cycle
Explains how the pathogen actually develops and spreads:
Survival→Infection→Development→Reproduction→Spread 

Disease tetrahedron
Time is added:
Host+Pathogen+Environment+Time→Disease 

4. Basic Disease Cycle
The complete concept can be represented as:
## Survival
↓
## Primary Inoculum
↓
## Dissemination
↓
## Inoculation
↓
## Penetration
↓
## Infection
↓
## Colonization
↓
## Symptom Development
↓
## Reproduction
↓
## Secondary Inoculum
↓
## Dissemination
↓
## New Host Infection
↓
## Repeats
The exact sequence varies among pathogen groups.

5. 1 — Survival
Before a new crop becomes infected, the pathogen must survive between favorable periods.
This is particularly important when:
- The host is absent.
- Weather becomes unfavorable.
- The crop is harvested.
- Temperature becomes extreme.
- Moisture becomes unsuitable.
Survival→Future source of inoculum 

6. Sources of Pathogen Survival
Pathogens may survive in:

Soil
- Spores
- Sclerotia
- Oospores
- Chlamydospores
- Nematodes

Plant residues
Infected residue→Survival→New crop infectionInfected\ residue Survival New\
crop\ infection

Seeds
Pathogens may survive:
- On seed surface
- Inside seed
- With seed tissues

Vegetative planting material
Examples:
- Tubers
- Bulbs
- Rhizomes
- Cuttings
- Grafts

Alternate hosts
Some pathogens survive on:
- Weeds
- Wild plants
- Other crop species

Perennial hosts
Pathogens may survive in living perennial tissues.

7. Pathogen Survival Structures
Different pathogens use different structures.

Fungi
- Mycelium
- Sclerotia
- Chlamydospores

- Resting spores

Oomycetes
- Oospores
- Persistent mycelium
- Other survival structures

Bacteria
May survive in:
- Plant residues
- Soil
- Seeds
- Volunteer plants
- Alternate hosts
- Irrigation water

Viruses
May survive in:
- Living plants
- Seeds
- Vegetative propagules
- Vectors
- Weed hosts

Nematodes
May survive as:
- Eggs
- Juveniles
- Adults
- Cysts

8. 2 — Primary Inoculum
Inoculum is any pathogen structure or material capable of initiating infection.

Examples:
- Fungal spores
- Bacterial cells
- Virus particles
- Nematodes
- Oospores
- Sclerotia-derived inoculum
Inoculum=Material capable of initiating infection 

9. Primary Inoculum
The primary inoculum is the inoculum responsible for initiating disease in a new crop or at the
beginning of a disease cycle.
Examples:
Overwintered spore→Primary infectionOverwintered\ spore Primary\ infection
Infected seed→Seedling infectionInfected\ seed Seedling\ infection
Surviving nematode eggs→New crop infestationSurviving\ nematode\ eggs New\ crop\
infestation

10. Secondary Inoculum
Secondary inoculum is produced after the pathogen has already infected hosts during the current
disease cycle and can cause additional infections.
Primary infection→Pathogen reproduction→Secondary inoculum→New infectionPrimary\ infection
 Pathogen\ reproduction  Secondary\ inoculum  New\ infection
This is especially important in polycyclic diseases.

11. Primary vs Secondary Inoculum
Feature Primary inoculum
Secondary inoculum
Source Surviving pathogen
Newly infected host
Timing Beginning of epidemic During epidemic
Function Starts disease
Spreads disease
Example Overwintered spores Newly produced spores
Memory:

Primary=Starts  Secondary=Spreads 

12. 3 — Dissemination
Dissemination means movement or spread of pathogen inoculum from one location to another.
Dissemination=Movement of inoculum 

13. Agents of Dissemination
Wind
Important for:
- Fungal spores
- Some airborne propagules
Spore→Wind→Healthy plantSpore Wind Healthy\ plant

Rain
Rain can splash:
- Spores
- Bacterial cells
- Soil particles

Irrigation water
Can spread:
- Soil-borne pathogens
- Waterborne inoculum

Soil
Can transport:
- Nematodes
- Resting spores
- Sclerotia
- Infected debris

Seeds
Can transport seed-borne pathogens.

Machinery
Movement of contaminated:
- Soil
- Plant debris
- Sap
can spread pathogens.

Humans and animals
Workers, animals and insects may transport pathogens.

Insects/vectors
Particularly important for:
- Viruses
- Phytoplasmas
- Some bacteria

14. 4 — Inoculation
Inoculation occurs when pathogen inoculum reaches and comes into contact with a susceptible host.
Inoculum+Host surface→Inoculation 
For example:
Fungal spore→Wind→Leaf surface→InoculationFungal\ spore  Wind  Leaf\
surface  Inoculation
Important:
Inoculation≠Infection 
The pathogen may reach the plant without successfully infecting it.

15. 5 — Penetration
After reaching the host, the pathogen must enter the plant or reach an appropriate infection site.
Possible routes include:

Direct penetration
Some pathogens penetrate directly through the plant surface.

Natural openings
Examples:
- Stomata
- Hydathodes
- Lenticels

Wounds
Created by:
- Insects
- Machinery
- Hail
- Pruning
- Root injury
- Cracks
General:
Inoculation→Penetration→InfectionInoculation  Penetration  Infection

16. 6 — Infection
Infection occurs when the pathogen successfully establishes itself in the host and begins a compatible
disease interaction.
Successful pathogen establishment=Infection 
This means the pathogen has moved beyond simply landing on the plant.

17. Infection Requirements
Successful infection depends on:

Host
- Susceptibility
- Age
- Physiological condition

Pathogen
- Virulence
- Viability
- Sufficient inoculum

Environment
- Temperature
- Moisture
- Humidity
- Leaf wetness
- Soil conditions

Time
The favorable conditions must persist long enough.
Thus:
Infection=f(Host,Pathogen,Environment,Time)
 

18. Infection Court
An infection court is a specific plant site where conditions are favorable for pathogen infection.
Examples may include:
- Leaf surface
- Stomata
- Wounds
- Root tips
- Flowers
- Fruit surfaces
Conceptually:
Suitable infection site+Suitable conditions→Infection court 

19. 7 — Colonization
After successful infection, the pathogen grows or multiplies within or on the host.
Infection→Colonization 
The pathogen may spread through:
- Intercellular spaces
- Cells
- Vascular tissues
- Root tissues
- Leaf tissues
depending on the pathogen.

20. Local vs Systemic Colonization
Local
Pathogen remains relatively close to the initial infection site.
Infection→Local lesionInfection Local\ lesion

Systemic
Pathogen spreads through a substantial portion of the plant.
Examples include some:
- Viruses
- Phytoplasmas
- Vascular pathogens
Infection→Movement→Multiple plant tissuesInfection  Movement  Multiple\
plant\ tissues

21. 8 — Symptom Development
As the pathogen interacts with the plant, physiological and structural changes occur.
Pathogen activity→Host physiological disruption→SymptomsPathogen\ activity  Host\
physiological\ disruption  Symptoms
Symptoms may include:

- Chlorosis
- Necrosis
- Wilt
- Leaf spots
- Blight
- Rot
- Canker
- Stunting
- Mosaic
- Mottling
- Galls
- Dieback
- Deformation

22. Symptoms vs Disease Cycle
Symptoms are one stage/result of the disease process, not the entire disease cycle.
Infection→Colonization→Physiological disturbance→SymptomsInfection  Colonization
 Physiological\ disturbance  Symptoms
The pathogen can continue developing after symptoms appear.

23. 9 — Reproduction
Once established in the host, the pathogen reproduces.

Fungi
May produce:
- Spores
- Fruiting bodies
- Other reproductive structures

Bacteria
Multiply mainly by cell division.

1 cell→2→4→8→...1\ cell 2 4 8 ...

Viruses
Replicate using host-cell machinery.

Nematodes
Produce:
- Eggs
- Juveniles
- Adults

Phytoplasmas
Multiply within infected plant/vector systems.

24. 10 — New Inoculum Formation
Reproduction produces new infectious material.
Reproduction→New inoculumReproduction  New\ inoculum
For fungi:
Infection→Sporulation→SporesInfection  Sporulation  Spores
For bacteria:
Multiplication→Bacterial cellsMultiplication  Bacterial\ cells
For nematodes:
Reproduction→Eggs/JuvenilesReproduction  Eggs/Juveniles

25. 11 — Dissemination Again
New inoculum leaves the infected plant or becomes available for transmission.
New inoculum→Dissemination→New hostNew\ inoculum  Dissemination  New\ host
This allows the disease epidemic to expand.

26. Monocyclic Disease
A monocyclic disease has approximately one major infection cycle during a growing season.

General pattern:
Primary inoculum→Infection→Disease→Reproduction 
There is little or no important secondary spread during the same season.

Management emphasis
Because secondary multiplication is limited, controlling initial inoculum can be especially important.
Examples can include some diseases dominated by long-lived soil survival structures.

27. Polycyclic Disease
A polycyclic disease undergoes multiple infection cycles during one growing season.
Primary inoculum→Infection→Secondary inoculum→Infection→Secondary inoculum→...
 
This can cause rapid epidemic development.
Common examples include many:
- Rust diseases
- Leaf spots
- Powdery mildews

28. Monocyclic vs Polycyclic
Feature
Monocyclic
Polycyclic
Major cycles/season
 1
Multiple
Secondary inoculum
Limited importance
Very important
Epidemic multiplication Relatively limited
Can be rapid
Main management focus Reduce initial inoculum
Reduce initial + secondary spread
Example types
Some soil/residue-borne diseases Many rusts/leaf spots/mildews

Memory
MONO=ONE  POLY=MANY 

29. Polycyclic Disease Explosion
Suppose one infected site produces 10 new infectious units:

1→10→100→1000→100001 10 100 1000 10000
This illustrates how repeated infection cycles can rapidly increase disease.
The actual population increase depends on:
- Reproduction rate
- Survival
- Host availability
- Weather
- Dispersal
- Host resistance
- Control measures

30. Disease Cycle of a Fungal Pathogen
A simplified fungal cycle:
Survival→Spore production→Spore dispersal→Spore deposition→Germination→Penetration→Infecti
- n→Colonization→Symptoms→Sporulation→New spores 
The cycle then repeats.

31. Disease Cycle of a Bacterial Pathogen
A simplified bacterial cycle:
Survival→Dissemination→Entry→Multiplication→Colonization→Symptoms→Bacterial multiplicatio
n→Ooze/dissemination→New host 
Bacteria may enter through:
- Wounds
- Stomata
- Hydathodes
- Natural openings

depending on the pathogen.

32. Disease Cycle of a Virus
A simplified viral disease cycle:
Survival→Vector/seed/vegetative transmission→Entry→Replication→Cell−to−cell movement→Syste
mic movement→Symptoms→New vector/host 
Important:
Viruses usually depend on living host tissue for replication.

33. Disease Cycle of Phytoplasma
A simplified phytoplasma cycle:
Infected plant→Vector acquisition→Vector multiplication/retention→Transmission→Healthy plant→P
hloem colonization→Symptoms 
Leafhoppers and related insects are important vectors for many phytoplasmas.

34. Disease Cycle of Nematodes
A simplified nematode cycle:
Egg→Juvenile→Adult→Reproduction→Eggs 
A plant-parasitic nematode cycle also includes:
Soil→Host root→Feeding→Development→Reproduction→SoilSoil  Host\ root  Feeding  Development  Reproduction  Soil

35. Disease Cycle and Environment
Environmental conditions can determine whether a particular stage succeeds.
Important factors include:
- Temperature

- Moisture
- Relative humidity
- Leaf wetness
- Rainfall
- Soil moisture
- Wind
- Light
- Crop density
For example:
Favorable temperature+leaf wetness→Spore germination↑Favorable\ temperature+leaf\ wetness
 Spore\ germination while:
Unfavorable conditions→Infection probability↓Unfavorable\ conditions  Infection\
probability The exact response is pathogen-specific.

36. Disease Cycle and Temperature
Pathogens generally have temperature ranges for:
- Survival
- Germination
- Growth
- Reproduction
- Infection
A simplified relationship:
Temperature→Pathogen development rateTemperature  Pathogen\ development\ rate
Degree-day concepts can sometimes be used for pathogen or vector phenology:
GDD=∑max⁡(0,Tmean−Tb) -T b)  
But disease-specific models may use more complex temperature and moisture relationships.

37. Disease Cycle and Moisture
Moisture can influence:
- Spore germination
- Bacterial multiplication
- Infection
- Root diseases
- Disease spread
For many foliar pathogens:
Leaf wetness↑→Infection opportunity↑Leaf\ wetness  Infection\
- pportunity But the exact response varies by pathogen.

38. Disease Cycle and Host Susceptibility
The host is not equally susceptible at all stages.
Disease susceptibility can vary with:
- Seedling stage
- Vegetative stage
- Flowering
- Fruiting
- Maturity
Thus:
Host stage→Susceptibility→Disease riskHost\ stage  Susceptibility  Disease\
risk

39. Disease Cycle and Disease Forecasting
Understanding the disease cycle allows prediction of disease risk.
For example:
Temperature+Humidity+Leaf wetness+Host stage+Inoculum→Disease riskTemperature+Humidity+Le
af\ wetness+Host\ stage+Inoculum  Disease\ risk

Forecasting can help determine:
- When disease may appear
- When infection is likely
- When monitoring should increase
- When management may be required

40. Disease Cycle and Epidemiology
Plant disease epidemiology studies disease occurrence, development and spread in plant populations.
The disease cycle provides the biological foundation for epidemiology.
Disease cycle→Epidemic development 
Important epidemiological factors:
Host+Pathogen+Environment+TimeHost+Pathogen+Environment+Time

41. Disease Cycle and Management
Every stage of the disease cycle provides an opportunity for management.

Survival
Remove infected residuesRemove\ infected\ residues

Primary inoculum
Healthy seed/planting materialHealthy\ seed/planting\ material

Dissemination
Sanitation+Vector managementSanitation+Vector\ management

Infection
Protective measuresProtective\ measures

Colonization
Host resistance/appropriate treatmentsHost\ resistance/appropriate\ treatments

Reproduction
Prevent sporulation/spreadPrevent\ sporulation/spread
Thus:

Disease management=Break the disease cycle 

42. Breaking the Disease Cycle
A fundamental plant disease management principle is:
BREAK ANY IMPORTANT LINK IN THE CYCLE 
Examples:

Reduce survival
- Crop rotation
- Remove infected residues
- Sanitation

Reduce primary inoculum
- Disease-free seed
- Resistant varieties
- Removal of infected plants

Reduce dissemination
- Vector management
- Sanitation
- Avoid moving contaminated soil
- Irrigation management

Prevent infection
- Protective treatments
- Avoid prolonged leaf wetness where feasible
- Appropriate planting time

Reduce reproduction
- Timely disease management
- Resistant cultivars
- Appropriate registered pesticides/biologicals

43. Disease Cycle and Cultural Control
Cultural practices can interrupt several stages simultaneously.
Examples:
- Crop rotation
- Sanitation
- Proper spacing
- Drainage
- Irrigation management
- Healthy seed
- Removal of volunteer plants
- Weed management
- Residue management
- Appropriate planting time
Conceptually:
Cultural management→Survival↓+Inoculum↓+Spread↓Cultural\ management  Survival + Inoculum + Spread 44. Disease Cycle and Biological Control
Beneficial organisms can interfere with pathogen development through:
- Competition
- Antibiosis
- Parasitism/mycoparasitism
- Predation in relevant systems
- Induced resistance
For example:
Biocontrol organism→Pathogen suppression→Infection↓Biocontrol\ organism  Pathogen\
suppression  Infection 45. Disease Cycle and Chemical Control
Registered fungicides, bactericides or other disease-control products may interfere with selected stages.
Depending on product:
Spore germination↓Spore\ germination - r:
Pathogen growth↓Pathogen\ growth - r:
Infection establishment↓Infection\ establishment Timing must be based on the disease, pathogen biology, crop stage, product label and resistancemanagement requirements.

46. Disease Cycle and Host Resistance
A resistant host can interrupt the cycle at the infection or colonization stage.
Resistant host→Infection/colonization↓→Disease development↓ 
This can also reduce pathogen reproduction and subsequent inoculum.

47. Disease Cycle and Quarantine
Quarantine and exclusion aim to prevent introduction of a pathogen into a new area.
Pathogen→Movement blocked→New area remains uninfectedPathogen  Movement\
blocked  New\ area\ remains\ uninfected
This is particularly important for pathogens that can spread through:
- Seed
- Planting material
- Soil
- Machinery
- Insects

48. Disease Cycle and Seed-Borne Pathogens
A simplified cycle:
Infected seed→Germination→Seedling infection→Disease→New inoculumInfected\ seed  Germination  Seedling\ infection  Disease  New\ inoculum
Therefore:
Healthy seed→Reduced primary inoculum 

49. Disease Cycle and Soil-Borne Pathogens
Soil→Survival structure→Root contact→Infection→Root disease→New survival structuresSoil
 Survival\ structure  Root\ contact  Infection  Root\ disease
 New\ survival\ structures
Important management methods include:
- Rotation
- Resistant varieties
- Sanitation
- Drainage
- Healthy planting material
- Appropriate biological/chemical methods

50. Disease Cycle and Vector-Borne Diseases
For a vector-borne pathogen:
Infected plant→Vector acquisition→Vector→Healthy plant→TransmissionInfected\ plant  Vector\ acquisition  Vector  Healthy\ plant  Transmission
This is particularly important for:
- Viruses
- Phytoplasmas
- Some bacteria
Therefore vector management can break the cycle.

51. Complete Disease Cycle
The entire concept can be remembered as:
Survival↓Primary inoculum↓Dissemination↓Inoculation↓Penetration↓Infection↓Colonization↓Sympto
ms↓Reproduction↓Secondary inoculum↓Dissemination↓New infection 
&Survival\\ & \\ &Primary\ inoculum\\ & \\ &Dissemination\\ & \\
&Inoculation\\ & \\ &Penetration\\ & \\ &Infection\\ & \\
&Colonization\\ & \\ &Symptoms\\ & \\ &Reproduction\\ & \\
&Secondary\ inoculum\\ & \\ &Dissemination\\ & \\ &New\ infection
   

52. Disease Cycle vs Life Cycle
These terms should not be confused.

Pathogen life cycle
Focuses on:
Growth+Development+ReproductionGrowth+Development+Reproduction
- f the pathogen.

Disease cycle
Focuses on:
Survival+Transmission+Infection+Disease+Reproduction+SpreadSurvival+Transmission+Infection+Di
sease+Reproduction+Spread
in relation to the host and environment.
Therefore:
Disease cycle=Pathogen biology+Host+Environment+Transmission 

53. Important Terminology
Term
Meaning
Pathogen
Disease-causing organism/agent
Inoculum
Material capable of initiating infection
Primary inoculum
Starts the epidemic/cycle
Secondary inoculum Produced during disease development and causes further infections
Inoculation
Arrival/contact of inoculum with host
Penetration
Entry into host

Term
Infection
Colonization
Dissemination
Sporulation
Survival
Epidemic

Meaning
Successful establishment
Growth/multiplication in host
Movement/spread of inoculum
Production of spores
Persistence through unfavorable conditions
Development/spread of disease in a host population

54. Common Mistakes
Mistake 1
Thinking inoculation and infection are the same.
They are not:
Inoculation→InfectionInoculation Infection
but successful inoculation does not always result in infection.

Mistake 2
Thinking every disease has only one cycle.
Polycyclic diseases can repeat several times during one season.

Mistake 3
Thinking symptoms are the beginning of the disease cycle.
The pathogen may have already survived, dispersed, penetrated and infected the plant before symptoms
become visible.

Mistake 4
Ignoring survival.
Many pathogens survive between seasons in soil, residues, seed or alternate hosts.

Mistake 5
Treating disease without understanding its cycle.
Effective disease management often depends on interrupting the most vulnerable stage.

55. Exam-Ready Answer
The disease cycle is the sequence of events through which a plant pathogen survives,
produces inoculum, reaches a susceptible host, causes infection, develops within the

host, produces symptoms and reproduces to generate new inoculum that spreads to
- ther hosts. The major stages include survival, production of primary inoculum,
dissemination, inoculation, penetration, infection, colonization, symptom
development, reproduction and production of secondary inoculum. Monocyclic
diseases have approximately one major infection cycle during a season, whereas
polycyclic diseases undergo multiple infection cycles through repeated production and
dissemination of secondary inoculum. Knowledge of the disease cycle is essential for
disease management because cultural, biological, chemical, genetic, physical and
regulatory methods can be used to interrupt important stages of the cycle.

56. Final Memory Map
The 10-step disease cycle
1. Survival  ↓ 2. Primary inoculum 
↓ 3. Dissemination  ↓ 4. Inoculation  ↓ 5. Penetration  ↓ 6. Infection  ↓ 7. Colonization 
↓ 8. Symptoms  ↓ 9. Reproduction  ↓ 10. New inoculum→Spread→New infection 

One-line memory:
## Survive → Spread → Contact → Enter → Infect → Colonize → Show Sympto
MS → REPRODUCE → SPREAD AGAIN 
 

Most important management principle:
BREAK THE DISEASE CYCLE = PREVENT OR REDUCE DISEASE   
## Module 16 — Plant Disease
## Management
## 13. Infection
## 1. Introduction
Infection is one of the most important stages in the plant disease cycle.

It occurs after a pathogen reaches a suitable host and successfully establishes a relationship with the
plant.
The basic sequence is:
Inoculum→Inoculation→Penetration→Infection→Colonization→Symptoms 
In simple words:
Infection is the successful establishment of a pathogen in a host plant after it reaches
and enters an appropriate site.

2. Definition of Infection
Simple definition
Infection is the successful establishment and development of a pathogen in a
susceptible host plant.

Exam-ready definition
Infection is the process in which a viable pathogen, after reaching a susceptible host
and successfully overcoming the host's barriers, establishes itself in or on the host
tissues and begins a compatible disease-causing interaction.
The key word is:
Establishment  
Merely landing on a plant does not necessarily mean infection.

3. Infection in the Disease Cycle
Infection occurs approximately here:
Survival→Inoculum→Dissemination→Inoculation→Penetration→Infection→Colonization→Sympto
ms→Reproduction   Colonization
 Symptoms  Reproduction  
Thus:
- Inoculation = pathogen reaches the host.
- Penetration = pathogen enters the host.
- Infection = pathogen successfully establishes itself.

- Colonization = pathogen grows/multiplies and spreads within the host.

4. Inoculation vs Penetration vs Infection
This is a very important distinction.
Stage
Meaning
Inoculation Inoculum reaches/contact the host
Penetration Pathogen enters the host
Infection
Pathogen successfully establishes
Colonization Pathogen grows/spreads in host
Disease
Host develops harmful disturbance

Memory
CONTACT → ENTER → ESTABLISH → SPREAD   
5. Infection Does Not Always Mean Visible
Disease
A plant may become infected before symptoms appear.
Therefore:
Infection≠Immediate visible symptoms 
There may be an incubation period between infection and symptom appearance.
For example:
Infection→Pathogen multiplication→Physiological disruption→Visible symptomsInfection  Pathogen\ multiplication  Physiological\ disruption  Visible\ symptoms
This is why apparently healthy plants can sometimes already carry a pathogen.

6. Requirements for Successful Infection
Successful infection generally requires compatibility between:

## 1. Host
The plant must be sufficiently susceptible.

## 2. Pathogen
The pathogen must be:
- Viable
- Infective
- Capable of attacking that host

## 3. Environment
Conditions must support infection.

## 4. Time
Favorable conditions must persist long enough.
Therefore:
Infection=f(Host+Pathogen+Environment+Time)
 
This is closely related to the disease triangle and disease tetrahedron.

7. Host Factors
Host susceptibility depends on:
- Plant species
- Variety/cultivar
- Age
- Growth stage
- Nutritional condition
- Water status
- Physical injuries
- Genetic resistance
- Plant defense mechanisms
For example:
Susceptible variety→Infection probability↑Susceptible\ variety  Infection\
probability while:

Resistant variety→Successful infection/colonization↓Resistant\ variety  Successful\
infection/colonization 8. Pathogen Factors
The pathogen must possess characteristics that allow it to infect the host.
Important factors include:
- Virulence
- Pathogenicity
- Infectivity
- Inoculum viability
- Inoculum concentration
- Enzymes
- Toxins
- Effectors
- Adhesion
- Ability to overcome host defenses

9. Environmental Factors
Environmental conditions strongly influence infection.
Important factors include:
- Temperature
- Relative humidity
- Leaf wetness
- Rainfall
- Soil moisture
- Wind
- Light
- Soil conditions
For many foliar fungal pathogens:

Leaf wetness+Suitable temperature→Spore germination→InfectionLeaf\ wetness+Suitable\
temperature  Spore\ germination  Infection
But the exact requirement differs among pathogens.

## 10. Time
Even when host, pathogen and environment are suitable, infection may require a minimum duration.
For example:
Favorable conditions→Sufficient duration→Successful infectionFavorable\ conditions  Sufficient\ duration  Successful\ infection
If favorable conditions last too briefly:
Infection probability↓Infection\ probability This is why leaf-wetness duration can be important in disease forecasting.

11. Infection Court
An infection court is a plant location where conditions are favorable for pathogen infection.
Possible infection courts include:
- Leaves
- Stems
- Roots
- Flowers
- Fruits
- Seeds
- Wounds
- Stomata
- Hydathodes
- Lenticels
- Root tips
Suitable site+Suitable conditions→Infection court 

12. Routes of Pathogen Entry
Pathogens can enter plants through different routes.

A. Direct penetration
Some pathogens penetrate directly through the plant surface.

B. Natural openings
Examples:
- Stomata
- Hydathodes
- Lenticels

C. Wounds
Wounds can be created by:
- Insects
- Hail
- Machinery
- Pruning
- Wind
- Root damage
- Cracks

D. Vectors
Some pathogens enter through vector feeding.
For example:
Vector feeding→Pathogen introduction→Plant infectionVector\ feeding  Pathogen\
introduction  Plant\ infection

13. Direct Penetration
Some pathogens produce structures capable of penetrating plant surfaces.
A simplified sequence is:

Spore→Germination→Germ tube→Appressorium/penetration structure→Host entrySpore  Germination  Germ\ tube  Appressorium/penetration\ structure  Host\
entry
The exact mechanism depends on the pathogen.

14. Penetration Through Stomata
Some pathogens enter through stomata.
Spore→Leaf surface→Stoma→Internal tissueSpore  Leaf\ surface  Stoma
 Internal\ tissue
Stomatal opening, humidity and environmental conditions can influence infection by particular
pathogens.

15. Penetration Through Wounds
Wounds remove or damage plant barriers.
Wound→Physical barrier weakened→Pathogen entry→InfectionWound  Physical\ barrier\
weakened  Pathogen\ entry  Infection
Examples of wound-causing agents:
- Insects
- Hail
- Pruning
- Machinery
- Root-feeding organisms
- Cracks caused by environmental stress

16. Infection Process in Fungi
A simplified fungal infection sequence is:
Spore→Germination→Germ tube→Attachment→Penetration→Infection→Colonization→Sporulation
 
The exact structures and mechanisms differ between fungal species.

17. Spore Germination
A fungal spore may germinate when environmental conditions are favorable.
Important factors:
- Moisture
- Temperature
- Oxygen
- Suitable surface
- Time
Suitable conditions→Spore germinationSuitable\ conditions  Spore\ germination
If conditions are unfavorable:
Spore germination↓Spore\ germination 18. Germ Tube
After germination, some fungal spores produce a germ tube.
Spore→Germ tubeSpore  Germ\ tube
The germ tube may grow toward an appropriate infection site.

## 19. Appressorium
Some plant-pathogenic fungi produce an appressorium, a specialized structure associated with
attachment and penetration.
Conceptually:
Spore→Germ tube→Appressorium→PenetrationSpore  Germ\ tube  Appressorium  Penetration
An appressorium can generate mechanical and/or biochemical forces that help the pathogen enter the
host.

20. Infection by Bacteria
Bacteria generally do not penetrate plant surfaces in exactly the same way as many fungi.
They may enter through:

- Stomata
- Hydathodes
- Wounds
- Natural openings
- Insect feeding sites
Simplified sequence:
Bacteria→Entry→Multiplication→Colonization→DiseaseBacteria  Entry  Multiplication  Colonization  Disease

21. Infection by Viruses
Viruses require living host cells for replication.
A simplified sequence:
Virus→Entry→Uncoating→Replication→Cell−to−cell movement→Systemic movement→Symptoms
Virus  Entry  Uncoating  Replication  Cell-to-cell\
movement  Systemic\ movement  Symptoms
Virus entry may occur through:
- Mechanical wounds
- Insect vectors
- Other vectors
- Grafting
- Vegetative propagation
depending on the virus.

22. Infection by Phytoplasmas
Phytoplasmas are generally transmitted by insect vectors such as leafhoppers and related insects.
Simplified:
Infected plant→Vector acquisition→Vector→Healthy plant→Transmission→Phloem colonizationInfec
ted\ plant  Vector\ acquisition  Vector  Healthy\ plant  Transmission  Phloem\ colonization
Symptoms can include:
- Little leaf

- Phyllody
- Witches' broom
- Yellowing
- Stunting

23. Infection by Nematodes
Plant-parasitic nematodes generally infect plants by entering or feeding on roots or other tissues.
Simplified sequence:
Nematode→Root contact→Penetration/feeding→Establishment→Feedingsite→Development→Plant d
amageNematode  Root\ contact  Penetration/feeding  Establishment
 Feeding site  Development  Plant\ damage
Some nematodes establish specialized feeding sites within roots.

24. Host Defense Against Infection
Plants are not passive.
They possess several defense mechanisms.

Physical barriers
- Cuticle
- Cell wall
- Bark
- Waxes

Chemical defenses
Plants may produce:
- Phenolics
- Phytoalexins
- Antimicrobial compounds
- Reactive oxygen species

Structural responses
- Cell wall strengthening

- Callose deposition
- Lignification
- Papilla formation

Immune responses
Plants can recognize pathogen-associated signals and activate defense pathways.
Thus:
Pathogen attack→Host recognition→Defense responsePathogen\ attack  Host\ recognition
 Defense\ response

25. Successful vs Failed Infection
Not every pathogen-host contact leads to disease.

Successful
Pathogen→Host recognition/entry→Defense overcome→Establishment→InfectionPathogen
 Host\ recognition/entry  Defense\ overcome  Establishment
 Infection

Failed
Pathogen→Host defense→Pathogen suppressed→No successful infectionPathogen  Host\
defense  Pathogen\ suppressed  No\ successful\ infection
This is why host resistance is important.

26. Compatible and Incompatible Interactions
Compatible interaction
The pathogen successfully establishes itself.
Host susceptibility+Pathogen capability→Successful infectionHost\ susceptibility + Pathogen\
capability  Successful\ infection

Incompatible interaction
Host defenses prevent or strongly restrict pathogen establishment.
Host resistance+Pathogen→Infection restrictedHost\ resistance + Pathogen  Infection\
restricted

27. Infection and Incubation Period
The incubation period is the time between successful infection and appearance of recognizable
symptoms.
Infection→Incubation period→Symptoms 
The duration depends on:
- Pathogen
- Host
- Temperature
- Environment
- Host age
- Pathogen growth rate

28. Infection and Latent Period
The latent period generally refers to the time between infection and the production of new infectious
propagules/inoculum.
Conceptually:
Infection→Latent period→New infectious inoculumInfection  Latent\ period  New\ infectious\ inoculum
This is particularly important in epidemiology.

Difference
Term
Ends when...
Incubation period Symptoms appear
Latent period
New infectious propagules are produced
They can overlap but are not necessarily identical.

29. Infection and Colonization
These terms are closely related but different.

Infection
Pathogen establishes 

Colonization
Pathogen grows/multiplies and occupies host tissue 
Thus:
Infection→Colonization 
A pathogen may establish at one location and subsequently colonize additional tissues.

30. Infection and Symptoms
Symptoms are usually a consequence of pathogen activity and host response.
Pathogen establishment→Host−pathogen interaction→Physiological disruption→SymptomsPathogen\
establishment  Host-pathogen\ interaction  Physiological\ disruption  Symptoms
Examples:
Infection→Vascular disruption→WiltInfection Vascular\ disruption Wilt
Infection→Tissue death→NecrosisInfection Tissue\ death Necrosis
Infection→Pigment disruption→ChlorosisInfection Pigment\ disruption Chlorosis

31. Infection and Disease Severity
Successful infection does not automatically mean severe disease.
Disease severity depends on:
- Number of infection events
- Pathogen aggressiveness
- Host susceptibility
- Environmental conditions
- Duration
- Pathogen multiplication
- Host defense
Therefore:
Infection≠Automatically Severe Disease 

32. Multiple Infection Events
A plant can experience repeated infections.
For a polycyclic disease:
Infection1→New inoculum→Infection2→New inoculum→Infection3→...Infection 1  New\ inoculum  Infection 2  New\ inoculum  Infection 3  ...
This can cause rapid epidemic development.

33. Infection and Secondary Inoculum
This is especially important in polycyclic diseases.
Primary infection→Pathogen reproduction→Secondary inoculum→Secondary infectionPrimary\
infection  Pathogen\ reproduction  Secondary\ inoculum  Secondary\
infection
Therefore:
Preventing secondary infectioncan greatly slow disease epidemics 

34. Infection and Disease Forecasting
Disease forecasting attempts to predict when infection is likely.
A conceptual model is:
Host susceptibility+Inoculum+Temperature+Moisture+Leaf wetness+Time→Infection risk 
Forecasting can help determine when disease management should be initiated.

35. Infection and Weather
Weather can determine whether an inoculum can successfully infect.

High humidity
May favor some fungal pathogens.

Leaf wetness
Can be essential for infection of some foliar pathogens.

Rain
Can:
- Provide moisture
- Disperse inoculum
- Splash bacteria/spores

Temperature
Controls pathogen growth and infection rates.
However:
The effect of weather is pathogen specific. 

36. Infection and Plant Stage
Plants may be more susceptible during particular developmental stages.
Examples can include:
- Seedling stage
- Flowering
- Fruiting
- Young leaves
- Root development
Therefore:
Crop stage→Host susceptibility→Infection riskCrop\ stage  Host\ susceptibility  Infection\ risk

37. Infection and Disease Management
The best strategy is often to prevent or reduce infection.

Avoidance
Avoid conditions favorable to infection.

Exclusion
Prevent pathogen introduction.

Host resistance
Reduce successful establishment.

Protection
Protect infection courts.

Biological control
Suppress pathogen activity.

Chemical control
Use appropriate registered products when needed.

Cultural practices
Reduce inoculum and favorable conditions.

38. How to Break Infection
The infection stage can be interrupted by:
Reduce inoculum  Prevent host−pathogen contact  Reduce favorable environmental conditions  Use resistant varieties 
Protect susceptible plant surfaces 
For example:
Inoculum↓→Infection events↓→Disease pressure↓Inoculum  Infection\
events  Disease\ pressure 39. Infection Court Protection
If a pathogen requires a particular surface or opening:
Protective measure→Pathogen entry↓→Infection↓Protective\ measure  Pathogen\
entry  Infection This is one reason some disease-control products are applied before infection rather than after
symptoms become severe.
The exact timing depends on the pathogen and registered product.

40. Disease Cycle Position
The infection stage can be summarized as:
## Survival
↓
## Inoculum
↓
## Dissemination
↓
## Inoculation
↓
## Penetration
↓
## ★ Infection ★
↓
## Colonization
↓
## Symptoms
↓
## Reproduction
↓
## New Inoculum
41. Infection Across Major Pathogen Groups
Pathogen
Typical infection process
Fungi
Germination → penetration → establishment → colonization
Oomycetes
Spore/zoospore → host contact → penetration/entry → colonization
Bacteria
Entry through openings/wounds → multiplication → colonization
Viruses
Entry/inoculation → replication → movement → systemic infection
Phytoplasmas Vector transmission → phloem establishment → multiplication/movement
Nematodes
Root/tissue contact → penetration/feeding → establishment → development
These are simplified generalizations; individual pathogen biology varies.

42. Important Terms Related to Infection
Term
Meaning
Inoculum
Infective material
Inoculation
Arrival/contact with host
Penetration
Entry into host
Infection
Successful establishment
Colonization
Growth/spread within host
Incubation period Infection → visible symptoms
Latent period
Infection → production of new infectious propagules
Infection court
Suitable site for infection
Primary infection
Infection initiated by primary inoculum
Secondary infection Infection caused by secondary inoculum
Susceptibility
Host tendency to permit infection
Resistance
Ability to restrict infection/disease

43. Common Mistakes
Mistake 1: Inoculation = infection
Incorrect.
Inoculation≠Infection 
Inoculation is contact; infection is successful establishment.

Mistake 2: Penetration = infection
Not exactly.
A pathogen can enter tissue but successful establishment involves subsequent biological compatibility.

Mistake 3: Infection = symptoms
Incorrect.
Symptoms may appear later.
Infection→Incubation→SymptomsInfection Incubation Symptoms

Mistake 4: Every infection causes severe disease
Incorrect.
Disease severity depends on host, pathogen, environment and time.

Mistake 5: Healthy-looking plant = pathogen-free plant
Not necessarily.

The pathogen may be present before symptoms become visible.

44. Complete Infection Sequence
The most useful conceptual chain is:
Inoculum→Inoculation→Penetration→Establishment→Infection→Colonization→Symptoms         
And for a complete disease cycle:
Survival→Inoculum→Dissemination→Inoculation→Penetration→Infection→Colonization→Sympto
ms→Reproduction→New inoculum 

45. Exam-Ready Answer
Infection is the successful establishment of a viable plant pathogen in a susceptible
host after inoculation and penetration. It is an important stage of the plant disease
cycle and occurs between penetration and colonization. Successful infection depends
- n the compatibility of the host and pathogen, favorable environmental conditions and
sufficient time. Pathogens may enter plants through direct penetration, natural
- penings, wounds or vectors, depending on the pathogen. Following infection, the
pathogen establishes and colonizes host tissues, causing physiological disturbances
and symptoms. Infection may occur without immediate visible symptoms because an
incubation period may exist between infection and symptom development.
Understanding infection is important for disease management because reducing
inoculum, preventing pathogen entry, modifying favorable environmental conditions,
using resistant varieties and protecting susceptible infection sites can reduce
successful infection.

46. Final Memory
The five critical words:
CONTACT → ENTER → ESTABLISH → COLONIZE → DISEASE   
More precisely:

Inoculation → Penetration → Infection → Colonization → Symptoms   

Most important distinction:
Inoculation = Pathogen reaches the host   
Penetration = Pathogen enters the host   
Infection = Pathogen successfully establishes    Colonization = Pathogen grows/spreads in the host   
Symptoms = Visible/physiological response of the plant   
So, in one sentence:
Infection is the successful establishment of a pathogen in a susceptible host after it
reaches and enters an appropriate infection site.

## Module 16 — Plant Disease
## Management
## 14. Disease Spread
## 1. Introduction
Disease spread refers to the movement and increase of a plant disease from an initial source of
infection to other susceptible plants, plant parts, fields, or regions.
In simple terms:
Disease spread is the process by which a pathogen moves from an infected source to
new susceptible hosts and establishes new infections.
The basic sequence is:
Infected source→Inoculum→Dissemination→New host→Infection→New disease 
When this process repeatedly occurs within a crop population, a disease epidemic can develop.

## 2. Definition
Exam-ready definition
Disease spread is the dissemination and transmission of a plant pathogen or its
inoculum from an infected source to susceptible hosts, resulting in new infections and
expansion of disease within a plant population, field or geographical area.

3. Disease Spread in the Disease Cycle
Disease spread is closely connected with the disease cycle:
Survival→Inoculum→Dissemination→Inoculation→Infection→Disease→New inoculum→Dissemina
tion 
The critical step for spread is:
Movement of infectious material from source to susceptible host   

4. Disease Spread vs Disease Development
These are related but different.

Disease spread
Focuses on:
Where does the pathogen go?  

Disease development
Focuses on:
How does disease increase after infection?  
For example:
Infected plant→Spores→Wind→Neighboring plantInfected\ plant  Spores  Wind
 Neighboring\ plant
is spread.
Then:
New infection→Colonization→Symptoms→New sporesNew\ infection  Colonization
 Symptoms  New\ spores

is disease development.

5. Sources of Disease Spread
Pathogens can spread from:
- Infected plants
- Diseased crop residues
- Infected seeds
- Infected planting material
- Soil
- Irrigation water
- Rain
- Wind
- Insects
- Machinery
- Tools
- Workers
- Animals
- Weeds
- Alternate hosts
- Contaminated storage facilities

6. Major Methods of Disease Spread
The major dissemination pathways are:
## 1. Wind
2. Rain and water
## 3. Soil
## 4. Seed
5. Vegetative planting material
6. Insects and vectors

## 7. Humans
8. Machinery and tools
## 9. Animals
10.Infected plant debris
11.Alternate hosts/weeds

7. Wind-Borne Spread
Wind is one of the most important mechanisms for dispersal of many fungal spores.
The general pathway is:
Infected plant→Spore production→Wind→Healthy plant→Infection 
Wind can transport inoculum:
- Within a field
- Between nearby fields
- Sometimes over much greater distances

8. Pathogens Commonly Associated With Wind
Spread
Wind can disseminate:
- Many fungal spores
- Some airborne bacterial material
- Some plant debris
- Certain propagules
Important examples include many diseases caused by:
- Rust fungi
- Powdery mildew fungi
- Some leaf-spot fungi
The exact distance and importance of wind spread depends on pathogen, spore characteristics, weather
and landscape.

9. Factors Affecting Wind Spread
Important factors include:

Wind speed
Wind speed↑→Potential dispersal↑Wind\ speed  Potential\ dispersal But the relationship is not simply linear in every disease.

Wind direction
Determines where inoculum is likely to move.

Spore characteristics
- Size
- Shape
- Weight
- Survival

Humidity
Affects spore survival and infection.

Source strength
More infectious material can increase the amount of inoculum available for dispersal.

10. Rain Splash
Rain can spread pathogens over short distances.
The pathway:
Rainfall→Splash→Inoculum movement→Nearby plantRainfall  Splash  Inoculum\ movement  Nearby\ plant
Rain splash is particularly important for:
- Some fungal spores
- Bacterial cells
- Soil particles carrying pathogens

11. Irrigation-Water Spread
Water can transport pathogens from one location to another.
Contaminated water→Irrigation→Healthy plants→InfectionContaminated\ water  Irrigation
 Healthy\ plants  Infection
This can be important for:
- Soil-borne pathogens
- Waterborne pathogens
- Some bacterial diseases
- Some oomycete diseases

12. Surface Water and Runoff
Heavy rainfall can cause:
Soil erosion/runoff→Pathogen movement→New areaSoil\ erosion/runoff  Pathogen\
movement  New\ area
This is especially relevant where:
- Slopes are present
- Soil is exposed
- Fields are poorly drained
- Pathogens survive in soil or residues

13. Soil-Borne Disease Spread
Soil can transport and maintain pathogens.
Potential soil-borne organisms include:
- Fungi
- Oomycetes
- Nematodes
- Some bacteria
General cycle:

Pathogen in soil→Root contact→Infection→New infected plantPathogen\ in\ soil  Root\
contact  Infection  New\ infected\ plant

14. Movement of Contaminated Soil
Soil can be moved by:
- Farm machinery
- Vehicles
- Tools
- Workers' footwear
- Animals
- Water
- Wind erosion
Thus:
Contaminated soil→Movement→New fieldContaminated\ soil  Movement  New\ field
can introduce pathogens.

15. Seed-Borne Disease Spread
Seeds are important vehicles for pathogen movement.
Pathogens may be:
- On the seed surface
- Inside the seed
- Associated with seed tissues
The pathway is:
Infected seed→Sowing→Seedling→DiseaseInfected\ seed  Sowing  Seedling
 Disease
Seed can therefore spread disease:
- Within a farm
- Between farms
- Between regions

- Through commercial seed systems

16. Vegetative Planting Material
Disease can spread through infected:
- Tubers
- Bulbs
- Rhizomes
- Cuttings
- Suckers
- Grafts
- Budwood
- Nursery plants
For example:
Infected planting material→New field→New plantsInfected\ planting\ material  New\ field
 New\ plants
This is especially important for pathogens that persist in living plant tissue.

17. Vector-Borne Disease Spread
Some pathogens depend on vectors.
A vector is an organism that transmits a pathogen from one host to another.
Important vectors include:
- Aphids
- Whiteflies
- Leafhoppers
- Planthoppers
- Thrips
- Beetles
- Other insects
The general cycle is:

Infected plant→Vector acquisition→Vector→Healthy plant→Pathogen transmission 

18. Virus Spread by Insects
Many plant viruses are transmitted by insects.
For example:
Virus-infected plant→Aphid/whitefly/etc.→Healthy plant→Virus transmissionVirus infected\
plant  Aphid/whitefly/etc.  Healthy\ plant  Virus\ transmission
Different viruses have different relationships with their vectors.
Therefore:
Vector management can be an important part of virus disease management. 

19. Phytoplasma Spread
Phytoplasmas are commonly transmitted by phloem-feeding insect vectors.
Simplified cycle:
Infected plant→Vector→Acquisition→Vector transmission→Healthy plantInfected\ plant  Vector  Acquisition  Vector\ transmission  Healthy\ plant
Important vector groups include:
- Leafhoppers
- Planthoppers
- Psyllids in some systems

20. Human-Mediated Spread
Humans can unintentionally move pathogens.
Examples:
- Contaminated hands
- Clothing
- Footwear
- Tools

- Pruning equipment
- Harvest equipment
- Vehicles
- Plant material
Thus:
Human activity→Contaminated material→New plant/fieldHuman\ activity  Contaminated\
material  New\ plant/field

21. Machinery and Tools
Machinery can move:
- Infected plant debris
- Soil
- Water
- Sap
- Plant residues
Examples:
- Tractors
- Harvesters
- Cultivators
- Pruning tools
- Cutting equipment
Therefore sanitation is important when moving between fields.

22. Animal-Mediated Spread
Animals can sometimes transport pathogen material externally or internally.
Potential carriers include:
- Livestock
- Wild animals
- Birds

- Rodents
- Insects
Their importance varies greatly with pathogen and cropping system.

23. Infected Crop Residues
Crop residues can serve as both:
Survival source 
and:
Inoculum source 
The cycle can be:
Infected residue→Pathogen survival→New inoculum→New cropInfected\ residue  Pathogen\ survival  New\ inoculum  New\ crop
This is why residue management can influence disease pressure.

24. Weed and Alternate-Host Spread
Some pathogens survive or multiply on:
- Weeds
- Volunteer crops
- Wild hosts
- Alternate crop species
The pathway may be:
Crop→Weed/alternate host→Pathogen survival→CropCrop  Weed/alternate\ host
 Pathogen\ survival  Crop
Therefore weed management can sometimes contribute to disease management.

25. Local Spread
Local spread occurs over relatively short distances.
Examples:
- Plant to neighboring plant

- Leaf to nearby leaf
- Rain splash
- Short-distance wind dispersal
- Plant-to-plant contact
- Insect movement within a field
Example:
Plant1→Plant2→Plant3→Plant4Plant 1  Plant 2  Plant 3  Plant 4

26. Long-Distance Spread
Long-distance spread can occur through:
- Wind
- Infected seed
- Planting material
- Trade
- Vehicles
- Machinery
- Human movement
- Insect migration
- Animals
Example:
Region A→Pathogen transport→Region BRegion\ A  Pathogen\ transport  Region\ B
Long-distance introduction can be especially important for emerging or invasive diseases.

27. Horizontal Spread
Horizontal spread means transmission between plants or hosts of the same generation/crop population.
Infected plant→Healthy plant 
For example:
Plant→Vector→PlantPlant Vector Plant

- r:
Plant→Wind-borne spore→PlantPlant Wind borne\ spore Plant

28. Vertical Transmission
Vertical transmission refers to pathogen transmission from parent plant or propagative material to the
next generation.
Examples:
Parent plant→Seed→SeedlingParent\ plant  Seed  Seedling
- r:
Infected mother plant→Vegetative propagule→New plantInfected\ mother\ plant  Vegetative\ propagule  New\ plant
This is particularly important for some seed-borne and vegetatively propagated pathogens.

29. Disease Spread Through Contact
Some pathogens can spread when infected and healthy plant tissues come into contact.
Infected tissue↔Healthy tissueInfected\ tissue  Healthy\ tissue
Mechanical contact may occur through:
- Wind movement
- Agricultural operations
- Pruning
- Harvesting
- Handling

30. Disease Spread Through Plant Sap
Some viruses can spread mechanically through infected sap.
Infected plant sap→Wound→Healthy plantInfected\ plant\ sap  Wound  Healthy\
plant
This can occur through:
- Cutting tools
- Handling

- Plant-to-plant contact
depending on the virus.

31. Disease Spread Through Vectors vs
Dissemination
These terms are related but not identical.

Dissemination
Movement of pathogen/inoculum.

Vector transmission
A living organism actively transfers a pathogen.
Vector=Living carrier/transmitter 
For example:
Virus→Aphid→Healthy plantVirus Aphid Healthy\ plant

32. Disease Spread and Disease Incidence
As new plants become infected:
Diseased plants↑Diseased\ plants Therefore:
Disease incidence=Diseased plantsTotal plants×100  Total\ plants  100  
Example:
100 plants examined and 20 diseased:
DI=20100×100=20%DI=  100  100=20\%

33. Disease Spread and Disease Severity
Spread can increase the number of infected plants, while disease development can increase the amount
- f damage on individual plants.
Incidence=How many plants?  Severity=How much damage?
 

A disease can therefore have:
- High incidence but low severity
- Low incidence but high severity
- Both high
- Both low

34. Disease Spread in Monocyclic Diseases
In monocyclic diseases, there is approximately one major infection cycle.
The important process is often:
Primary inoculum→Initial infection→DiseasePrimary\ inoculum  Initial\ infection
 Disease
Management therefore emphasizes:
Reduce initial inoculum 
Examples of approaches:
- Healthy seed
- Crop rotation
- Residue management
- Soil management
- Resistant varieties

35. Disease Spread in Polycyclic Diseases
Polycyclic diseases repeatedly produce new inoculum.
I0→I1→I2→I3→...I 0  I 1  I 2  I 3  ...
where II represents successive infection/inoculum generations.
Therefore:
Secondary spread becomes extremely important 
Management must reduce both:
- Primary inoculum

- Secondary spread

36. Disease Spread and Epidemics
When disease spreads through a plant population over time, an epidemic may develop.
Conceptually:
Initial infection→New infections→More inoculum→More infections→Disease epidemicInitial\
infection  New\ infections  More\ inoculum  More\ infections
 Disease\ epidemic
Thus:
