Disease spread+Time+Susceptible host population→Epidemic 

37. Factors Affecting Disease Spread
Disease spread depends on several interacting factors:
Spread=f(Pathogen+Host+Environment+Vector+Time+Human activity)
 

Pathogen
- Amount of inoculum
- Survival
- Reproduction
- Dispersal ability
- Infectivity

Host
- Density
- Susceptibility
- Growth stage
- Variety
- Spatial arrangement

Environment
- Temperature

- Humidity
- Rainfall
- Wind
- Leaf wetness
- Soil moisture

Vector
- Abundance
- Movement
- Feeding behavior
- Vector competence

Human activity
- Seed movement
- Machinery
- Trade
- Planting material
- Irrigation

38. Crop Density and Disease Spread
Dense crops can facilitate spread of some diseases.
Plant density↑→Plant contact/microclimate↑→Spread potential↑Plant\ density  Plant\ contact/microclimate  Spread\ potential Dense canopies can also increase:
- Humidity
- Leaf wetness duration
- Reduced air movement
which may favor some pathogens.
But the exact response is disease-specific.

39. Weather and Disease Spread
Rain
Can:
- Splash spores
- Move bacteria
- Increase moisture

Wind
Can:
- Carry spores
- Move infected material

Humidity
Can affect:
- Spore survival
- Germination
- Infection

Temperature
Affects:
- Pathogen growth
- Reproduction
- Vector activity
Therefore:
Weather→Dispersal+Infection→Disease spreadWeather  Dispersal+Infection  Disease\ spread

40. Disease Spread and Vectors
For vector-borne diseases:
Disease spread≈Vector abundance×Vector movement×Transmission efficiency×Host availabilityDiseas
e\ spread  Vector\ abundance  Vector\ movement  Transmission\ efficiency  Host\ availability

This is a conceptual relationship rather than a universal quantitative equation.

41. Disease Spread and Human Activities
Agricultural practices can either increase or decrease spread.

Can increase spread
- Using infected planting material
- Moving contaminated soil
- Poor sanitation
- Excessive movement between fields
- Sharing contaminated tools
- Unregulated movement of plants

Can reduce spread
- Certified healthy planting material
- Tool sanitation
- Equipment cleaning
- Field sanitation
- Quarantine
- Proper residue management

42. Disease Spread Management
The objective is:
Reduce pathogen movement+Reduce successful new infections 
Major strategies include:

## 1. Exclusion
Prevent pathogen introduction.

## 2. Sanitation
Remove sources of inoculum.

3. Resistant varieties
Reduce successful infection.

4. Vector management
Reduce transmission where appropriate.

5. Cultural control
Modify the crop environment and production system.

6. Chemical/biological protection
Reduce infection or pathogen reproduction when appropriate.

## 7. Quarantine
Prevent movement between regions.

## 43. Exclusion
Exclusion means preventing a pathogen from entering a field or production system.
Methods:
- Certified seed
- Disease-free planting material
- Quarantine
- Inspection
- Equipment sanitation
- Nursery hygiene
Prevent entry→Prevent disease establishment 

## 44. Sanitation
Sanitation removes pathogen sources.
Examples:
- Remove diseased plants where appropriate
- Remove infected residues
- Clean tools

- Clean machinery
- Manage volunteer plants
- Control alternate hosts where relevant
Sanitation→Inoculum↓→Spread↓Sanitation  Inoculum  Spread 45. Crop Rotation
Crop rotation can reduce diseases when the pathogen depends strongly on a particular host.
Crop A→Non−host crop→Pathogen population↓Crop\ A  Non-host\ crop  Pathogen\ population However, rotation effectiveness depends on:
- Pathogen survival
- Host range
- Soil persistence
- Rotation duration

46. Resistant Varieties
Resistance can reduce:
- Infection
- Colonization
- Pathogen reproduction
- Disease severity
Resistance→Disease development↓→Secondary spread↓ 

47. Vector Management
For vector-borne diseases:
Vector population/activity↓→Transmission opportunities↓Vector\ population/activity  Transmission\ opportunities Management may include:

- Resistant varieties
- Weed/alternate-host management
- Physical exclusion where practical
- Biological control
- Appropriate registered vector-control measures
The correct strategy depends on the specific pathogen-vector system.

48. Integrated Disease Management
Disease spread is best managed through an integrated approach:
IDM=Cultural+Biological+Chemical+Genetic+Physical+Regulatory+Other compatible methods   IDM= Cultural+ Biological+ Chemical+ Genetic+ Physical+ Regulatory+ Other\ compatible\
methods  
Basic sequence:
Diagnose→Assess→Prevent→Monitor→Manage→Evaluate 

49. Disease Spread Monitoring
Monitoring should look for:
- New infected plants
- Disease incidence
- Disease severity
- Spatial pattern
- New lesions
- Pathogen signs
- Vector populations
- Weather conditions

Disease incidence
DI(%)=Diseased plantsTotal plants×100DI(\%)=   Total\ plants  100

Disease severity
DS(%)=Diseased tissue areaTotal tissue area×100DS(\%)=   Total\ tissue\
area  100

50. Spatial Pattern of Disease Spread
Disease distribution can provide clues.

Random
Disease appears scattered.

Aggregated/patchy
Disease occurs in clusters.
Often associated with:
- Soil-borne pathogens
- Localized inoculum
- Water movement

Linear
Disease follows:
- Irrigation channels
- Field boundaries
- Machinery movement
- Rows

Edge-associated
Disease is concentrated near field borders.
However:
Field pattern provides clues, not definitive diagnosis.

53. Primary vs Secondary Spread
Primary spread
Initial inoculum reaches plants.
Survival source→Primary infectionSurvival\ source  Primary\ infection

Secondary spread
Newly infected plants produce inoculum that infects additional plants.
Infected plant→New inoculum→New plantInfected\ plant  New\ inoculum  New\ plant
This distinction is particularly important in polycyclic diseases.

54. Example — Fungal Disease Spread
Suppose a diseased leaf produces spores:

Diseased leaf→Spores→Wind→Healthy leaf→Germination→InfectionDiseased\ leaf  Spores  Wind  Healthy\ leaf  Germination  Infection
The newly infected leaf then produces more spores:
New disease→More spores→More plantsNew\ disease  More\ spores  More\
plants
This produces:
Secondary spread 

55. Example — Virus Spread
Virus-infected plant→Aphid→Healthy plant→Virus infectionVirus infected\ plant  Aphid  Healthy\ plant  Virus\ infection
Then:
Newly infected plant→More vectors acquire virus→More plantsNewly\ infected\ plant  More\ vectors\ acquire\ virus  More\ plants

56. Example — Soil-Borne Disease
Infected residue/soil→Pathogen survives→Root contact→Infection→DiseaseInfected\ residue/soil
 Pathogen\ survives  Root\ contact  Infection  Disease
Then contaminated soil may be moved by:
Machinery→New fieldMachinery  New\ field
This creates geographical spread.

57. Disease Spread vs Pathogen Spread
They are related but not identical.

Pathogen spread
Movement of the pathogen or its infectious material.

Disease spread
Expansion of disease through new infections and diseased hosts.
Therefore:
Pathogen movement→New infection→Disease spread 

A pathogen may move without establishing infection.

58. Important Factors in Disease Spread
Remember:
I+S+E+T 
Where:
- I = Inoculum
- S = Spread/dissemination mechanism
- E = Environment
- T = Time
But successful disease expansion also requires:
Susceptible Host 
Therefore the complete concept is:
Inoculum+Dissemination+Susceptible Host+Favorable Environment+Time→New infections 

59. Common Mistakes
Mistake 1: Thinking only wind spreads disease
Incorrect.
Disease can spread through:
Wind+Water+Soil+Seed+Vectors+Humans+Machinery+AnimalsWind+Water+Soil+Seed+Vectors+Hu
mans+Machinery+Animals

Mistake 2: Confusing spread with infection
Spread gets the pathogen to a new location.
Infection is successful establishment in the host.

Mistake 3: Assuming every vector carries every virus
Vector-pathogen relationships are often highly specific.

Mistake 4: Ignoring seed and planting material
These are major pathways for many pathogens.

Mistake 5: Assuming all disease spread is visible
A pathogen can spread before obvious symptoms appear.

60. Exam-Ready Answer
Disease spread is the dissemination and transmission of a plant pathogen or its
inoculum from an infected source to susceptible hosts, resulting in new infections and
expansion of disease within a crop population or geographical area. Pathogens can
spread through wind, rain, irrigation water, soil, infected seed, vegetative planting
material, insects and other vectors, machinery, tools, humans, animals, plant residues
and alternate hosts. Disease spread may be local or long-distance, and may occur
horizontally between plants or vertically through seed and vegetative propagules. In
polycyclic diseases, secondary inoculum produced by newly infected plants can cause
repeated cycles of infection and rapid epidemic development. Disease spread can be
reduced through exclusion, sanitation, resistant varieties, crop rotation, vector
management, cultural practices, biological and chemical protection, and quarantine.

61. Final Memory Map
Main pathway
## Infected Source→Inoculum→Dissemination→New Host→Infection→New Di
SEASE        

Major spread agents
WIND + WATER + SOIL + SEED + VECTORS + HUMANS + MACHINERY + ANIMALS   

Disease epidemic
Infection→Reproduction→New inoculum→Spread→New infection→Repeat 

Management principle
STOP THE PATHOGEN FROM MOVING OR STOP IT FROM SUCCESSFULLY INFECTING THE
NEXT HOST   
And the most important distinction:

DISSEMINATION = MOVEMENT   
INFECTION = SUCCESSFUL ESTABLISHMENT   
DISEASE SPREAD = NEW INFECTIONS + EXPANSION OF DISEASE   
## Module 16 — Plant Disease
## Management
## 15. Resistant Varieties
## 1. Introduction
Resistant varieties are crop varieties or cultivars that possess genetic characteristics allowing them to
reduce, restrict, or tolerate disease development when exposed to a particular pathogen.
They are one of the most important components of integrated disease management (IDM) because
they can reduce disease without requiring repeated external chemical inputs.
The basic principle is:
Resistant variety→Pathogen development↓→Disease severity↓→Yield loss↓ 
However, resistance is not necessarily absolute. A variety may resist one pathogen strain but remain
susceptible to another.

## 2. Definition
Simple definition
A resistant variety is a crop variety that has the ability to restrict or reduce the
development and harmful effects of a particular pathogen.

Exam-ready definition
Disease-resistant varieties are crop varieties possessing heritable genetic
characteristics that reduce pathogen infection, colonization, reproduction, disease
development, or resulting damage compared with susceptible varieties under similar
conditions.

3. Why Resistant Varieties Are Important
Disease-resistant varieties can:
- Reduce disease incidence
- Reduce disease severity
- Reduce pathogen multiplication
- Reduce secondary inoculum
- Reduce disease spread
- Protect yield and quality
- Reduce dependence on pesticides
- Reduce production costs in some situations
- Reduce environmental exposure to pesticides
- Support sustainable agriculture
- Help manage diseases over large areas
The overall concept is:
Host resistance→Disease development↓ 

4. Resistance in the Disease Triangle
Recall the disease triangle:
Host+Pathogen+Environment→Disease 
If the host is resistant:
Resistant Host+Pathogen+Environment→Disease development reduced 
Resistance therefore modifies one of the three major components of disease development: the host.

5. Resistant vs Susceptible Variety
Feature
Pathogen establishment
Pathogen multiplication
Disease severity

Resistant variety
Susceptible variety
Restricted
More successful
Often reduced
Often greater
Generally lower Generally higher

Feature
Resistant variety
Susceptible variety
Inoculum production
Often reduced
Often greater
Disease spread
Can be slower
Can be faster
Pesticide dependence
May be reduced May be higher
Yield loss under disease pressure Generally lower Generally higher
Genetic basis
Heritable traits
Lacks sufficient resistance to that pathogen

6. Resistance Is Pathogen-Specific
A variety is not simply "resistant to disease" in a universal sense.
It is better to say:
Variety X is resistant to pathogen Y or a particular disease/pathogen population under
specified conditions.
For example:
Variety A→Resistant to Pathogen XVariety\ A  Resistant\ to\ Pathogen\ X
does not necessarily mean:
Variety A→Resistant to Pathogen YVariety\ A  Resistant\ to\ Pathogen\ Y

7. Resistance vs Immunity
These terms should not be confused.

Immunity
The host does not become infected or does not support pathogen development under the relevant
conditions.

Resistance
The host can restrict pathogen development or disease compared with a susceptible host.
Conceptually:
Immunity>Strong resistance>Moderate resistance>SusceptibilityImmunity > Strong\ resistance >
Moderate\ resistance > Susceptibility
But these terms should be interpreted according to the specific disease/pathogen system.

8. Resistance vs Tolerance
This is a very important distinction.

Resistance
The plant restricts the pathogen or disease development.
Resistance→Pathogen/disease development↓Resistance  Pathogen/disease\
development Tolerance
The plant may allow substantial pathogen infection or disease but maintains relatively good
growth/yield.
Tolerance→Disease present+Yield loss relatively limitedTolerance  Disease\ present +
Yield\ loss\ relatively\ limited
Therefore:
Resistance=Restrict the pathogen/disease 
Tolerance=Limit the damage caused by disease 
A tolerant plant can still support pathogen multiplication and potentially contribute to disease spread.

9. Types of Disease Resistance
Several classifications are used.

A. Vertical resistance
Usually associated with relatively strong resistance to particular pathogen races or strains.
It is often described as:
Race−specific resistance 
It may provide strong protection but can be overcome when the pathogen population changes.

10. Horizontal Resistance
Horizontal resistance is generally:
Broad−spectrum+Often partial+Less race−specific 
It can reduce disease development across several pathogen races or populations.
Often it is associated with multiple genes and multiple plant defense mechanisms.

11. Vertical vs Horizontal Resistance
Feature
Vertical resistance
Horizontal resistance
Specificity
Often race-specific
Generally broader
Effect
Often strong
Often partial
Genetic basis
Frequently fewer major genes
Often multiple genes
Pathogen variation Can be overcome relatively quickly
Often more durable
Disease reduction Potentially high against matching races Often moderate but broader
Durability
Can be less durable
Often more durable
These are useful conceptual categories, but actual resistance genetics can be more complex.

12. Qualitative Resistance
Qualitative resistance is often associated with the presence of one or a few genes with relatively large
effects.
Conceptually:
Major resistance gene→Strong responseMajor\ resistance\ gene  Strong\ response
It may produce a relatively distinct resistant/susceptible classification.

13. Quantitative Resistance
Quantitative resistance generally involves multiple genes, each contributing smaller effects.
Gene1+Gene2+Gene3+⋯→Overall disease reductionGene 1+Gene 2+Gene 3+  Overall\ disease\ reduction
It often produces a continuous range of disease responses.

14. Major-Gene Resistance
A major resistance gene can recognize a specific pathogen-derived signal or effector.
Simplified:
Resistance gene+Pathogen recognition→Defense activationResistance\ gene + Pathogen\ recognition
 Defense\ activation
This can result in:
- Rapid defense
- Localized cell death in some interactions

- Restriction of pathogen growth
- Reduced colonization

15. Polygenic Resistance
Polygenic resistance involves several genes.
Multiple genes→Multiple defense mechanisms→Reduced disease 
It may be more difficult for a pathogen to overcome completely because several components contribute
to resistance.

16. Mechanisms of Disease Resistance
Plants can resist pathogens through several mechanisms.

1. Structural barriers
- Cuticle
- Cell wall
- Bark
- Thickened tissues
- Surface waxes

2. Chemical defenses
- Phenolic compounds
- Phytoalexins
- Antimicrobial compounds

3. Cellular defense
- Callose deposition
- Lignification
- Cell wall reinforcement
- Localized cell death in some interactions

4. Molecular recognition
Plant receptors can recognize pathogen-associated signals and activate defense responses.

17. Structural Resistance
The plant can physically restrict pathogen entry.
Strong physical barrier→Pathogen penetration↓ 
Examples include:
- Thick cuticle
- Strong cell walls
- Tough epidermis
- Structural barriers
- Reduced natural openings or unfavorable infection sites in some host-pathogen combinations

18. Biochemical Resistance
Plants may produce compounds that inhibit pathogen development.
Examples include:
- Phenolics
- Phytoalexins
- Antimicrobial proteins
- Reactive oxygen species
- Enzyme inhibitors
Conceptually:
Pathogen→Host recognition→Defense chemicals→Pathogen growth↓Pathogen  Host\
recognition  Defense\ chemicals  Pathogen\ growth 19. Hypersensitive Response
In some resistant interactions, the plant rapidly responds at or near the infection site.
One mechanism can involve localized death of host cells around the infection site.
Pathogen recognition→Rapid defense→Localized cell death→Pathogen spread restrictedPathogen\
recognition  Rapid\ defense  Localized\ cell\ death  Pathogen\ spread\
restricted

This is called the hypersensitive response (HR).
It is particularly associated with some gene-for-gene interactions.

20. Gene-for-Gene Concept
A classical concept in plant pathology is:
Resistance gene in host↔Corresponding avirulence factor in pathogen 
When recognition occurs:
Recognition→Defense activation→Disease restrictionRecognition  Defense\ activation
 Disease\ restriction
If the pathogen changes or loses the recognized factor:
Recognition fails→Resistance may be overcomeRecognition\ fails  Resistance\ may\ be\
- vercome

21. Pathogen Race and Resistance
Pathogen populations may contain different races, strains or genotypes that interact differently with
host resistance genes.
Therefore:
Resistance to Race 1≠Resistance to Race 2Resistance\ to\ Race\ 1  Resistance\ to\ Race\ 2
A pathogen population can evolve or shift toward variants capable of overcoming a resistance gene.

22. Breakdown of Resistance
One of the major limitations of resistant varieties is resistance breakdown.
A simplified process:
Resistant variety→Pathogen population exposed→Rare compatible variant→Survival/
reproduction→Variant frequency↑→Resistance effectiveness↓Resistant\ variety  Pathogen\
population\ exposed  Rare\ compatible\ variant  Survival/reproduction
 Variant\ frequency  Resistance\ effectiveness This is an example of pathogen adaptation and selection.

23. Factors That Can Cause Resistance
Breakdown
Resistance effectiveness can decline because of:
- Pathogen genetic variation
- Mutation
- Selection
- Recombination
- Introduction of new pathogen populations
- Large-scale cultivation of one resistance gene
- Continuous monoculture
- High pathogen pressure
- Environmental conditions
- Breakdown of specific host recognition

24. Durability of Resistance
Durable resistance is resistance that remains effective over a relatively long period and across
pathogen populations and environments.
Durability is influenced by:
Durability=f(Host+Pathogen+Environment+Management)Durability=f(Host+Pathogen+Environment+
Management)
No resistance should be assumed permanently durable.

25. Pyramiding Resistance Genes
Gene pyramiding means combining multiple resistance genes in one variety.
Conceptually:
R1+R2+R3→Broader/stronger resistanceR 1+R 2+R 3  Broader/stronger\ resistance
The purpose is to make it more difficult for the pathogen population to overcome all resistance
mechanisms simultaneously.

26. Multiline Varieties
A multiline approach uses mixtures of genetically different lines that may share desirable agronomic
characteristics but carry different resistance genes.
Conceptually:
Line1(R1)+Line2(R2)+Line3(R3)Line 1(R 1)+Line 2(R 2)+Line 3(R 3)
This can reduce the uniformity of host resistance at the field scale.

27. Variety Mixtures
Growing multiple compatible varieties with different resistance characteristics can sometimes slow
disease spread.
Instead of:
100% Variety A100\%\ Variety\ A
a mixture may contain:
Variety A+Variety B+Variety CVariety\ A+Variety\ B+Variety\ C
Disease transmission between identical susceptible hosts may be interrupted or reduced depending on
the disease.

28. Resistant Varieties and Disease Spread
This is especially important in your Disease Spread topic.
A resistant variety can reduce:
Infection→Colonization→Pathogen reproduction→Inoculum→Secondary spreadInfection  Colonization  Pathogen\ reproduction  Inoculum  Secondary\ spread
Thus:
Resistance→Secondary inoculum↓→Disease spread↓ 

29. Resistant Varieties and Disease Epidemics
In polycyclic diseases:
Infection→Reproduction→New inoculum→New infectionInfection  Reproduction
 New\ inoculum  New\ infection

Resistance can reduce one or more stages.
Therefore:
Resistance→Infection rate↓+Pathogen multiplication↓+Inoculum production↓ 
This can slow the entire epidemic.

30. Resistant Varieties and Integrated Disease
Management
Resistance should not necessarily be used alone.
A stronger strategy is:
Resistant Variety+Healthy Seed+Crop Rotation+Sanitation+Monitoring+Biological/Chemical Protectio
n when needed 
This is Integrated Disease Management (IDM).

31. Advantages of Resistant Varieties
1. Reduces disease
Disease severity↓Disease\ severity 2. Can reduce pesticide requirement
Chemical dependence↓Chemical\ dependence when disease pressure is reduced sufficiently.

3. Environmentally beneficial
Less pesticide use can reduce exposure to:
- Non-target organisms
- Soil
- Water
- Pollinators
when pesticide applications are actually avoided.

4. Economically useful
Potential benefits include:
- Lower disease-control costs
- Better yield stability
- Lower crop loss

5. Easy to integrate
Resistance can be incorporated into normal seed selection.

32. Limitations of Resistant Varieties
Resistance is not perfect.
Potential limitations include:

Pathogen evolution
Pathogen evolution→Resistance breakdownPathogen\ evolution  Resistance\ breakdown

Pathogen specificity
A variety resistant to one disease may remain susceptible to another.

Environmental effects
Resistance expression can sometimes vary with temperature or other environmental conditions.

Genetic trade-offs
Breeding for resistance can sometimes interact with:
- Yield
- Quality
- Maturity
- Plant architecture
- Other traits

Availability
Suitable resistant varieties may not be available for every crop-pathogen combination.

33. Resistance and Environment
Resistance can sometimes be environment-dependent.
Therefore:
Resistance phenotype=f(Genotype+Environment+Pathogen)Resistance\
phenotype=f(Genotype+Environment+Pathogen)
A variety may perform differently under different:
- Temperatures
- Moisture conditions
- Pathogen populations
- Crop stages
This is why field testing is important.

34. Resistant Variety Selection
When selecting a variety for disease resistance, consider:
1. Target crop
2. Target disease
3. Pathogen species
4. Local pathogen population/races
5. Resistance level
6. Yield potential
## 7. Quality
## 8. Maturity
9. Climate suitability
10.Soil suitability
11.Market requirements
12.Seed availability
13.Other disease resistance
14.Insect/pest resistance
15.Compatibility with local production practices

The ideal selection is not simply:
"Choose the variety with the word resistant."
It should be:
Crop+Disease+Pathogen+Region+Environment+Agronomic traits→Variety selection 

35. Disease-Resistant Seed
Using resistant seed is particularly valuable because:
Healthy resistant seed→Strong crop establishment→Disease risk↓Healthy\ resistant\ seed  Strong\ crop\ establishment  Disease\ risk However:
Resistant seed≠Disease-proof cropResistant\ seed Disease proof\ crop
Environmental conditions and pathogen populations still matter.

36. Resistant Variety vs Disease-Free Seed
These are different concepts.

Resistant variety
Genetically better able to restrict disease.

Disease-free seed
Seed that is free or sufficiently free from the specified pathogen according to the relevant seed-health
standard.
Therefore:
Resistant variety≠Disease-free seedResistant\ variety  Disease free\ seed
Ideally:
Resistant variety+Healthy seed 

37. Resistant Variety vs Chemical Protection
Resistant variety
Genetic/biological host trait
Present throughout crop
Can reduce disease development

Chemical protection
External input
Applied at specific times
Can directly suppress pathogen

Resistant variety
Chemical protection
May be relatively low recurring input Repeated application may be required
Can be pathogen-specific
Product/MOA-specific
Can be overcome
Pathogen resistance to chemicals can develop
Integrated with other methods
One component of IDM
These methods can complement each other.

38. Resistant Variety and Fungicide Resistance
Do not confuse:
Host resistance 
with:
Pathogen resistance to fungicide 

Host resistance
Plant restricts disease.

Fungicide resistance
Pathogen population becomes less sensitive to a fungicide.
They are biologically different phenomena.

39. Role in Sustainable Agriculture
Resistant varieties can contribute to sustainability by:
Disease resistance→Disease pressure↓→Pesticide applications potentially↓→Input use↓Disease\
resistance  Disease\ pressure  Pesticide\ applications\
potentially  Input\ use They can support:
- Integrated disease management
- Reduced pesticide dependence
- Yield stability
- Resource efficiency
- Environmental protection
But sustainability requires considering the whole production system.

40. Disease Resistance and Crop Breeding
Breeding for resistance generally involves:
Resistance source→Crossing→Selection→Evaluation→Multi−location testing→Variety development
Resistance\ source  Crossing  Selection  Evaluation  Multi-location\ testing  Variety\ development
Resistance sources may come from:
- Existing cultivars
- Landraces
- Wild relatives
- Germplasm collections
Modern breeding can use:
- Marker-assisted selection
- Genomic selection
- Molecular breeding
- Gene editing in appropriate regulatory contexts

41. Marker-Assisted Selection
If a resistance-associated genetic marker is known:
DNA marker→Identify desirable genotype→Select plants→Breed resistant varietyDNA\ marker
 Identify\ desirable\ genotype  Select\ plants  Breed\ resistant\ variety
This can speed selection compared with relying only on visible disease symptoms.

42. Gene Editing and Resistance
Modern breeding can sometimes modify specific genes associated with disease susceptibility or
resistance.
Conceptually:
Target gene→Genetic modification→Altered host−pathogen interaction→Potential disease resistanceT
arget\ gene  Genetic\ modification  Altered\ host-pathogen\ interaction
 Potential\ disease\ resistance
The practical and regulatory status of such varieties differs by crop and jurisdiction.

43. Resistant Varieties in the Disease Triangle
The complete concept can be visualized as:
## Pathogen
/\
/

\

/

\

/

\

/

\

## Host ------- Environment
↑
|
Resistant variety
reduces disease

The resistant host changes the outcome of the interaction.

44. Resistant Varieties and Disease Identification
Resistance information can also assist diagnosis.
Suppose:
- Variety A is known to resist Disease X.
- Variety A shows no symptoms.
- A susceptible variety nearby shows typical symptoms.
This information can provide useful evidence.
However:
Variety response alone is not sufficient for diagnosis. 
Diagnosis should still consider:
Crop+Symptoms+Signs+Pattern+Environment+HistoryCrop+Symptoms+Signs+Pattern+Environment
+History

45. Practical Example
Imagine a field contains a disease capable of producing repeated secondary inoculum.

Susceptible variety
Infection→Rapid pathogen growth→More spores→Secondary infections→Disease epidemicInfection
 Rapid\ pathogen\ growth  More\ spores  Secondary\ infections
 Disease\ epidemic

Resistant variety
Infection attempts→Pathogen growth restricted→Less pathogen reproduction→Less inoculum→Disea
se spread slowedInfection\ attempts  Pathogen\ growth\ restricted  Less\
pathogen\ reproduction  Less\ inoculum  Disease\ spread\ slowed
Therefore:
Resistant host→Epidemic development slowed 

46. Important Comparison
Concept
Resistance
Tolerance
Immunity
Susceptibility
Pathogen
resistance
Disease escape

Meaning
Plant restricts pathogen/disease
Plant suffers relatively limited economic damage despite disease
Host does not support the relevant disease/infection under defined conditions
Host permits disease development
Pathogen becomes less sensitive to a control product
Plant avoids disease because exposure/timing/environment is unfavorable, not
necessarily because of genetic resistance

Disease escape is especially important.
A plant may appear healthy because:
Disease pressure avoidedDisease\ pressure\ avoided
rather than:
Genetic resistanceGenetic\ resistance
For example, different maturity timing can allow a crop to avoid peak pathogen pressure.

47. Resistance vs Escape
True resistance
Host genetics→Pathogen development restrictedHost\ genetics  Pathogen\ development\
restricted

Escape
Crop timing/location/environment→Pathogen exposure reducedCrop\ timing/location/environment
 Pathogen\ exposure\ reduced
Therefore:
Healthy plant does not automatically mean genetic resistance. 

48. Common Mistakes
Mistake 1: "Resistant means disease-proof."
Not necessarily.
Resistance can be partial, pathogen-specific or environment-dependent.

Mistake 2: One resistant variety resists every disease.
Incorrect.
Resistance is generally disease/pathogen-specific.

Mistake 3: Resistance and tolerance are the same.
Incorrect.
Resistance=Restrict pathogen/diseaseResistance=Restrict\ pathogen/disease
Tolerance=Limit damageTolerance=Limit\ damage

Mistake 4: Resistance never breaks down.
Incorrect.
Pathogen populations can evolve.

Mistake 5: Resistant variety means no need for monitoring.
Incorrect.
Monitoring remains important because:
- Pathogen populations change

- Resistance may be partial
- Environment changes
- Other diseases may occur

49. Integrated Use of Resistant Varieties
The strongest disease-management strategy is often:
Healthy Seed+Resistant Variety+Crop Rotation+Sanitation+Good Nutrition+Proper Irrigation/
Drainage+Monitoring+Biological/Chemical Control when required 
This is the practical foundation of Integrated Disease Management.

50. Complete Conceptual Flow
Resistance gene→Host defense→Pathogen establishment↓→Colonization↓→Pathogen reproduction↓
→Inoculum↓→Disease spread↓→Yield loss↓ 
This connects resistant varieties directly to the previous topics:
Disease cycle→Infection→Disease spread→Resistant variety→Spread reductionDisease\ cycle
 Infection  Disease\ spread  Resistant\ variety  Spread\
reduction

51. Exam-Ready Answer
Resistant varieties are crop varieties possessing heritable genetic characteristics that
restrict pathogen infection, colonization, reproduction or disease development
compared with susceptible varieties. Disease resistance is an important component of
integrated disease management because it can reduce disease severity, pathogen
multiplication, secondary inoculum and disease spread. Resistance may be racespecific or broad-spectrum, qualitative or quantitative, and may involve single or
multiple genes. Plants resist pathogens through structural barriers, biochemical
defenses, immune recognition and other defense mechanisms. Resistant varieties
should not be confused with tolerant varieties: resistance restricts the pathogen or
disease, whereas tolerance limits the harmful effects of disease on plant performance.
Resistance may also be overcome by changes in pathogen populations, so resistant

varieties should be combined with healthy planting material, crop rotation, sanitation,
monitoring and other compatible disease-management practices.

52. Final Memory
The central idea:
RESISTANT VARIETY→PATHOGEN DEVELOPMENT↓→DISEASE↓→SPREAD↓         

Remember the three terms:
Resistance = Restrict the pathogen  
Tolerance = Reduce the damage  
Escape = Avoid exposure  

And the most important IDM principle:
Resistant variety + monitoring + cultural practices + other compatible controls   
Resistance is one of the most powerful tools in plant disease management, but it should be treated
as a component of an integrated system rather than as a permanent guarantee of disease-free
crops.

## Module 16 — Plant Disease
## Management
## 16. Sanitation
## 1. Introduction
Sanitation is a disease-management practice that aims to remove, destroy, clean, or prevent sources
- f plant pathogens and infectious material from the crop production system.
The central idea is simple:
Sanitation→Inoculum↓→New infections↓→Disease spread↓ 
In plant pathology, sanitation is particularly important because many pathogens survive between
cropping seasons in:

- Infected crop residues
- Diseased plants
- Seeds
- Planting material
- Weeds
- Volunteer plants
- Soil
- Tools
- Machinery
- Storage structures

## 2. Definition
Simple definition
Sanitation is the removal, destruction, cleaning or exclusion of sources of plant
pathogens to reduce disease development and spread.

Exam-ready definition
Sanitation is a disease-management practice involving the removal, destruction or
decontamination of infected plants, plant debris, soil, tools, equipment, planting
material and other pathogen sources in order to reduce inoculum and prevent or
minimize the spread of plant diseases.

3. Basic Principle
The most important concept is:
Remove the source before it produces or spreads new inoculum.   
The disease cycle can be interrupted as follows:
Infected material→Pathogen survival→New inoculum→Dissemination→New infectionInfected\
material  Pathogen\ survival  New\ inoculum  Dissemination
 New\ infection
Sanitation attempts to break this chain:

Infected material→Removal/Destruction→Inoculum↓→Disease spread↓ 

4. Sanitation in the Disease Cycle
Recall the disease cycle:
Survival→Inoculum→Dissemination→Inoculation→Penetration→Infection→Colonization→Sympto
ms→Reproduction 
Sanitation primarily targets:
Survival+Inoculum+Dissemination 
Therefore:
Sanitation→Primary/secondary inoculum↓ 

5. Why Sanitation Is Important
Sanitation helps to:
- Reduce pathogen populations
- Remove infected sources
- Reduce primary inoculum
- Reduce secondary inoculum
- Prevent disease spread
- Reduce disease carry-over between seasons
- Reduce contamination of tools and machinery
- Reduce pathogen movement between fields
- Protect healthy planting material
- Support other IDM practices

6. Major Sources of Pathogen Inoculum
Pathogens may survive in:

Plant sources
- Infected leaves
- Stems
- Roots
- Fruits
- Seeds
- Tubers
- Bulbs
- Rhizomes
- Cuttings
- Grafts

Field sources
- Crop residues
- Volunteer plants
- Weeds
- Alternate hosts
- Soil

Human-mediated sources
- Tools
- Machinery
- Containers
- Footwear
- Gloves
- Hands

Other sources
- Irrigation systems
- Nursery structures
- Storage facilities
- Packing materials

7. Removal of Diseased Plants
One important sanitation practice is removal of infected plants.
Diseased plant→Removal→Inoculum source↓Diseased\ plant  Removal  Inoculum\ source This may be especially useful for diseases that spread rapidly from infected plants to healthy plants.

## 8. Roguing
Roguing means identifying and removing diseased, off-type or otherwise unwanted plants from a crop
- r planting population.
In disease management:
Detect diseased plant→Remove plant→Destroy/dispose appropriately 
It is particularly useful when:
- Disease occurs in scattered plants
- Early detection is possible
- Removed plants are significant inoculum sources
- The disease spreads from plant to plant

9. Importance of Early Roguing
Early removal is generally more useful than waiting until heavily diseased plants produce large
amounts of inoculum.
Early detection→Early removal→Inoculum production↓Early\ detection  Early\ removal
 Inoculum\ production For rapidly spreading diseases:
Delay→More inoculum→More infectionsDelay  More\ inoculum  More\
infections

10. Removal of Infected Crop Residues
Crop residues can allow pathogens to survive between seasons.

Infected residue→Pathogen survival→New inoculumInfected\ residue  Pathogen\ survival
 New\ inoculum
Therefore appropriate residue management can reduce disease pressure.
Possible strategies include:
- Removal
- Destruction where appropriate
- Proper decomposition
- Incorporation where scientifically appropriate
- Accelerated decomposition
- Composting under suitable controlled conditions
The correct method depends on the pathogen.

11. Crop Residue Management
Not all residue removal is automatically beneficial.
Crop residues can provide:
- Soil organic matter
- Nutrients
- Erosion protection
- Moisture conservation
- Habitat
Therefore the objective is not simply:
"Remove all residues."
Instead:
Manage residues so that soil benefits are retained while pathogen survival and inoculum production are
minimized. 

12. Sanitation Through Composting
Some organic residues can be composted under properly managed conditions.

Organic residue→Controlled decomposition→Stabilized organic matterOrganic\ residue  Controlled\ decomposition  Stabilized\ organic\ matter
Proper composting can reduce many pathogens and weed seeds when appropriate temperature, time,
moisture and aeration conditions are achieved.
However:
Composting is not automatically pathogen elimination. 
Poorly managed compost may retain viable pathogens.

13. Removal of Fallen Fruits
Diseased fruits that fall to the soil can become sources of inoculum.
Diseased fruit→Pathogen survival/reproduction→New inoculumDiseased\ fruit  Pathogen\
survival/reproduction  New\ inoculum
Removing and appropriately disposing of infected fruits can reduce disease pressure in suitable
systems.

14. Pruning Diseased Parts
In perennial crops, infected branches or shoots may be removed.
Diseased branch→Pruning→Infected tissue removedDiseased\ branch  Pruning  Infected\ tissue\ removed
Important:
Pruning tools can themselves spread pathogens.
Therefore:
Pruning+Tool sanitation 
should be considered together.

15. Tool Sanitation
Tools can carry pathogens from diseased plants to healthy plants.
Examples:
- Pruning shears

- Knives
- Grafting tools
- Cutting tools
- Harvesting equipment
The pathway is:
Diseased plant→Contaminated tool→Healthy plantDiseased\ plant  Contaminated\ tool
 Healthy\ plant
Sanitation interrupts this:
Clean/disinfect tool→Transmission↓ 
The disinfectant and concentration should be appropriate for the crop/pathogen/use and handled
according to applicable safety instructions.

16. Machinery Sanitation
Machinery can move:
- Infected soil
- Plant debris
- Root fragments
- Water
- Pathogen-containing material
For example:
Diseased field→Tractor→Healthy fieldDiseased\ field  Tractor  Healthy\ field
Therefore cleaning equipment before entering a clean field can reduce pathogen movement.

17. Footwear and Worker Hygiene
Workers can unintentionally move contaminated:
- Soil
- Plant sap
- Plant debris
- Pathogen propagules

between fields.
Potential measures include:
- Cleaning footwear
- Cleaning tools
- Changing contaminated protective equipment where appropriate
- Avoiding unnecessary movement from diseased to healthy areas
- Maintaining clean nursery/production areas

18. Nursery Sanitation
Nurseries require especially careful sanitation because many plants are grown closely together.
Important practices include:
- Healthy seed
- Clean planting material
- Clean containers
- Clean tools
- Clean benches
- Good drainage
- Removal of diseased seedlings
- Proper irrigation management
- Avoiding unnecessary leaf wetness
- Separation of infected and healthy material
The goal is:
Prevent a small infection focus from becoming a large nursery epidemic. 

19. Seed Sanitation
Seed can carry pathogens:
Infected seed→Seedling→DiseaseInfected\ seed  Seedling  Disease
Sanitation-related seed management may include:

- Certified seed
- Disease-tested seed
- Cleaning
- Appropriate seed treatment
- Removal of contaminated seed lots
- Proper storage
Seed treatment itself may be chemical, biological or physical depending on the pathogen and crop.

20. Planting Material Sanitation
Vegetative planting material can transmit pathogens.
Examples:
- Tubers
- Bulbs
- Rhizomes
- Cuttings
- Suckers
- Grafts
- Budwood
- Nursery seedlings
Therefore:
Healthy mother plant→Healthy propagule→Lower disease riskHealthy\ mother\ plant  Healthy\ propagule  Lower\ disease\ risk

21. Weed Sanitation
Weeds may act as:
- Alternative hosts
- Pathogen reservoirs
- Vector reservoirs
The pathway can be:

Crop→Weed→Pathogen survival→CropCrop  Weed  Pathogen\ survival
 Crop
Removing important alternative hosts can reduce disease pressure where the relationship is established.
However, not every weed is a pathogen host.

22. Volunteer Crop Plants
Volunteer plants are plants that emerge from previous crops without intentional sowing.
They may carry pathogens between crop cycles:
Previous crop→Volunteer plant→Pathogen survival→Next cropPrevious\ crop  Volunteer\
plant  Pathogen\ survival  Next\ crop
Therefore management of volunteers can be an important sanitation measure for some diseases.

23. Alternate Host Management
Some pathogens require or benefit from alternate hosts for part of their life cycle.
The conceptual pathway is:
Crop→Alternate host→Pathogen survival→CropCrop  Alternate\ host  Pathogen\ survival  Crop
Removing or managing the alternate host can therefore reduce pathogen survival.
But this should only be done when the alternate host relationship is established.

24. Sanitation in Storage
Post-harvest sanitation is also important.
Storage areas can harbor:
- Fungal spores
- Bacteria
- Infected residues
- Contaminated containers
- Infected produce
Important measures include:

- Cleaning storage structures
- Removing old diseased material
- Cleaning containers
- Maintaining suitable environmental conditions
- Separating diseased produce
- Preventing contamination of healthy produce

25. Packing-House Sanitation
In post-harvest systems:
Infected fruit→Handling/packing→Healthy fruit contaminationInfected\ fruit  Handling/packing  Healthy\ fruit\ contamination
Therefore:
- Clean surfaces
- Clean containers
- Proper sorting
- Removal of diseased produce
- Equipment sanitation
can reduce disease spread.

26. Irrigation and Sanitation
Contaminated irrigation water can transport pathogens.
Contaminated water→Irrigation→Healthy cropContaminated\ water  Irrigation  Healthy\ crop
Management may involve:
- Avoiding contaminated water sources where possible
- Proper drainage
- Preventing runoff from diseased areas into clean areas
- Managing irrigation to reduce prolonged leaf wetness
- Maintaining irrigation-system hygiene where relevant

27. Drainage and Sanitation
Poor drainage can increase disease pressure by creating favorable conditions for some soil- and waterassociated pathogens.
Waterlogging→Rootstress+Favorableconditions for some pathogens→Disease risk↑Waterlogging
 Root stress + Favorable conditions\ for\ some\ pathogens  Disease\ risk Therefore:
Good drainage→Unfavorable conditions for some pathogens→Disease risk↓Good\ drainage
 Unfavorable\ conditions\ for\ some\ pathogens  Disease\ risk Drainage is therefore a cultural practice that can complement sanitation.

28. Field Hygiene
Field hygiene involves keeping the production area free from unnecessary sources of infection.
Examples:
- Remove heavily infected material when appropriate
- Manage volunteers
- Manage relevant weeds
- Clean tools
- Clean machinery
- Avoid moving contaminated soil
- Manage crop residues
- Use healthy planting material

29. Sanitation and Primary Inoculum
One of the most important benefits is reduction of primary inoculum.
Sanitation→Survival source↓→Primary inoculum↓→Initial infection↓Sanitation  Survival\
source  Primary\ inoculum  Initial\ infection This is particularly important for diseases where pathogens survive between seasons.

30. Sanitation and Secondary Inoculum
Sanitation can also reduce secondary spread.
For example:
Diseased leaf→Spore production→New infectionsDiseased\ leaf  Spore\ production
 New\ infections
Removing the infected leaf before significant sporulation can reduce:
Secondary inoculum↓Secondary\ inoculum Thus:
Sanitation can act against both primary and secondary inoculum. 

31. Sanitation and Disease Spread
The relationship is:
Sanitation→Inoculum↓→Dissemination↓→New infections↓→Disease spread↓ 
This directly connects sanitation with the previous topic Disease Spread.

32. Sanitation and Disease Triangle
Recall:
Host+Pathogen+Environment→Disease 
Sanitation primarily reduces the pathogen component.
Pathogen population/inoculum↓→Disease risk↓Pathogen\ population/inoculum  Disease\ risk It may also indirectly change the crop environment by reducing infected material and disease foci.

33. Sanitation and Disease Cycle
Sanitation can interrupt several stages:
Survival→Inoculum→Dissemination→Infection  Inoculum
  Dissemination   Infection  

The objective is to make it harder for the pathogen to complete its cycle.

34. Sanitation in Monocyclic Diseases
For monocyclic diseases:
Primary inoculum→Initial infection→DiseasePrimary\ inoculum  Initial\ infection
 Disease
Sanitation can be particularly valuable because reducing initial inoculum can strongly reduce disease
establishment.
Primary inoculum↓→Initial disease↓ 

35. Sanitation in Polycyclic Diseases
For polycyclic diseases:
Infection→Reproduction→Secondary inoculum→New infection→RepeatInfection  Reproduction  Secondary\ inoculum  New\ infection  Repeat
Sanitation can reduce the number of infectious sources, especially when infected tissues can be
removed before producing large amounts of secondary inoculum.
But sanitation alone may not be sufficient for rapidly spreading polycyclic diseases.

36. Sanitation and Resistant Varieties
These methods complement each other.

Resistant variety
Host resistance→Disease development↓Host\ resistance  Disease\ development Sanitation
Inoculum↓→Disease pressure↓Inoculum  Disease\ pressure Together:
Resistant host+Low inoculum→Disease risk↓ 

37. Sanitation and Crop Rotation
Crop rotation can reduce pathogens that depend on a particular host.
Sanitation removes sources of inoculum, while rotation removes the preferred host.
Sanitation+Rotation→Pathogen survival↓Sanitation+Rotation  Pathogen\
survival This combination can be particularly useful for some soil- and residue-borne diseases.

38. Sanitation and Chemical Control
Sanitation can reduce the amount of pathogen that chemical control needs to manage.
Sanitation→Inoculum↓Sanitation  Inoculum followed, where necessary, by:
Appropriate chemical protection→Further disease suppressionAppropriate\ chemical\ protection
 Further\ disease\ suppression
This can be more sustainable than relying entirely on repeated chemical applications.

39. Sanitation and Biological Control
Sanitation can complement biological control.
For example:
Inoculum↓+Beneficial microorganism→Disease pressure↓Inoculum + Beneficial\
microorganism  Disease\ pressure However, excessive or poorly timed sanitation practices can sometimes disturb useful organisms or soil
processes, so management should remain integrated.

40. Important Principle — Don't Spread the
Disease While Sanitizing
A major practical mistake is moving infected material through the field during removal.
Incorrect:
Diseased plant→Dragged through healthy cropDiseased\ plant  Dragged\ through\ healthy\
crop

This can spread pathogen material.
Better principle:
Remove carefully→Contain→Dispose/treat appropriately 
The exact disposal method depends on the pathogen, crop and local regulations.

41. Disposal of Diseased Material
Possible approaches include:
- Removal from the field
- Controlled destruction where permitted
- Deep burial where appropriate and permitted
- Proper composting when pathogen biology and composting conditions make it suitable
- Other approved disposal methods
There is no universal disposal method.
Disposal method=Pathogen+Crop+Environment+Regulation 

42. Sanitation Does Not Mean "Destroy
Everything"
Sanitation must be targeted.
For example:
- Not every leaf with discoloration is infected.
- Not every fungus is a plant pathogen.
- Not every residue should be removed.
- Not every insect is harmful.
- Some microorganisms are beneficial.
Therefore:
Correct diagnosis→Correct sanitation decision 

43. Diagnosis Before Sanitation
This is especially important.
Before removing plants or applying sanitation measures:
Symptoms+Signs+Pattern+Crop+Environment+History→Diagnosis 
Then:
Diagnosis→Identify pathogen source→Choose sanitation methodDiagnosis  Identify\
pathogen\ source  Choose\ sanitation\ method
Incorrect diagnosis can result in:
- Unnecessary plant removal
- Wasted labor
- Unnecessary costs
- Loss of beneficial organisms
- Ineffective disease control

44. Sanitation in Different Production Systems
Open-field crops
Focus on:
- Crop residues
- Volunteers
- Weeds
- Machinery
- Soil movement
- Irrigation
- Infected plants

Orchards
Focus on:
- Diseased branches
- Fallen fruits

- Pruning tools
- Mummified fruits
- Dead wood
- Alternate hosts

Nurseries
Focus on:
- Seedlings
- Containers
- Tools
- Water
- Benches
- Planting material

Greenhouses
Focus on:
- Tools
- Benches
- Containers
- Irrigation systems
- Plant debris
- Worker movement
- Infected plants

Storage
Focus on:
- Containers
- Floors/walls
- Diseased produce
- Equipment
- Humidity and temperature management

45. Sanitation Workflow
A practical sanitation process can be represented as:
Identify→Locate pathogen source→Remove/clean→Contain→Dispose/
decontaminate→Monitor 

Step 1 — Identify
Determine whether the abnormality is actually disease.

Step 2 — Locate the source
Find:
- Infected plants
- Residues
- Soil
- Tools
- Water
- Planting material

Step 3 — Remove or clean
Use an appropriate sanitation method.

Step 4 — Prevent contamination
Avoid spreading the pathogen during removal.

Step 5 — Dispose/decontaminate
Use an appropriate method.

Step 6 — Monitor
Look for new infections.

46. Sanitation Checklist
Before planting
- Use healthy seed
- Use healthy planting material

- Clean nursery equipment
- Remove old diseased residues
- Clean machinery
- Inspect field

During crop growth
- Scout regularly
- Rogue diseased plants where appropriate
- Remove important inoculum sources
- Clean tools
- Avoid moving contaminated soil
- Manage relevant weeds and volunteers

During harvest
- Avoid unnecessary plant injury
- Clean harvesting equipment
- Separate diseased produce
- Avoid spreading infected material

After harvest
- Manage residues appropriately
- Clean equipment
- Clean storage areas
- Remove infected produce
- Manage volunteer plants

47. Advantages of Sanitation
1. Reduces inoculum
Inoculum↓Inoculum 2. Reduces disease spread
Spread↓Spread 3. Reduces initial infection
Initial infection↓Initial\ infection 4. Can reduce pesticide dependence
When sanitation is effective, pathogen pressure may be lower.

5. Supports IDM
It works with:
- Resistance
- Rotation
- Biological control
- Chemical protection
- Cultural practices

6. Can be relatively simple
Some practices require basic field hygiene rather than sophisticated equipment.

## 48. Limitations
Sanitation also has limitations.

Labor requirement
Roguing and removal can be labor-intensive.

Disposal challenge
Diseased material must be handled appropriately.

Not effective against every disease
Some pathogens spread very rapidly or survive widely in soil/water.

Hidden infection
Some plants may be infected before symptoms appear.

Large-scale crops
Removing individual plants may not always be economically practical.

Environmental survival
Some pathogens produce durable structures that survive for years.

Therefore:
Sanitation is powerful, but pathogen biology determines its effectiveness. 

49. Common Mistakes
Mistake 1: Removing a diseased plant but leaving the infected residue
The pathogen may continue producing inoculum.

Mistake 2: Carrying diseased plants through healthy plants
This can spread the pathogen.

Mistake 3: Using dirty pruning tools
The tool may transmit pathogens.

Mistake 4: Assuming every residue should be burned
Burning is not universally appropriate and can cause environmental and nutrient losses.

Mistake 5: Ignoring volunteer plants
They may act as pathogen reservoirs for some diseases.

Mistake 6: Ignoring planting material
Infected cuttings/tubers/bulbs can introduce disease into otherwise clean fields.

Mistake 7: Sanitizing without diagnosis
Removing the wrong material does not solve the actual disease problem.

50. Important Comparison
Method
Main target
Resistant variety
Host susceptibility
Sanitation
Pathogen/inoculum source
Crop rotation
Host availability/pathogen survival
Biological control Pathogen or disease process
Chemical protection Pathogen development
Vector management Disease transmission
Quarantine
Pathogen introduction
This gives a useful IDM framework:

Attack the Host+Pathogen+Environment+Transmission pathways 

51. Sanitation and Integrated Disease
Management
Sanitation should be combined with other methods:
IDM=Resistance+Sanitation+Cultural practices+Biological control+Chemical protection+Monitoring - xed  IDM= Resistance+ Sanitation+ Cultural\ practices+ Biological\ control+ Chemical\ protection+
Monitoring  
The exact combination depends on the disease.
A practical sequence is:
Diagnose→Reduce inoculum→Use resistant host→Modify environment→Monitor→Apply additional
controls if required 

52. Complete Conceptual Flow
Diseased tissue→Pathogen survival→Inoculum→Dissemination→New infection 
Sanitation interrupts it:
Diseased tissue→Removal/Decontamination→Inoculum↓→Disease spread↓ 

53. Exam-Ready Answer
Sanitation is an important plant disease-management practice involving the removal,
destruction, cleaning or decontamination of infected plants, crop residues, seeds,
planting materials, tools, machinery, soil, weeds, volunteer plants and other sources of
plant pathogens. Its primary objective is to reduce pathogen survival and inoculum,
thereby reducing initial infection and subsequent disease spread. Important sanitation
practices include roguing diseased plants, removal or appropriate management of
infected residues, pruning of diseased plant parts, tool and machinery sanitation, use
- f healthy planting material, nursery hygiene, management of alternate hosts and
volunteers, and cleaning of storage and packing facilities. Sanitation is particularly

effective when it is performed early, before infected material produces large quantities
- f secondary inoculum. It is most effective when integrated with resistant varieties,
crop rotation, biological control, appropriate chemical protection and regular disease
monitoring.

54. Final Memory
One-line definition:
## Sanitation = Remove Or Decontaminate Pathogen Sources To Reduce Inocul
UM AND DISEASE SPREAD   
Main pathway:
REMOVE SOURCE→INOCULUM↓→SPREAD↓→INFECTION↓→DISEASE↓           

Four words to remember:
IDENTIFY → REMOVE → DECONTAMINATE → MONITOR   
And the key connection with your previous topics:
Resistant Variety = Attack the Host Side   
Sanitation = Attack the Pathogen/Inoculum Side   
Together = Lower Disease Pressure and Slow Disease Spread   

## Module 16 — Plant Disease
## Management
## 17. Biological Control
## 1. Introduction
Biological control of plant diseases is the management of plant pathogens and diseases using living
- rganisms or their biological products rather than relying solely on synthetic chemical pesticides.
The central idea is:

Beneficial organism→Suppresses pathogen→Disease development↓ 
Biological control is an important component of Integrated Disease Management (IDM).

## 2. Definition
Simple definition
Biological control is the suppression of plant diseases using beneficial living organisms
- r their biological products.

Exam-ready definition
Biological control of plant diseases is the use or manipulation of beneficial
microorganisms and other biological agents to reduce the survival, growth, infection,
reproduction or spread of plant pathogens and thereby reduce disease development.

3. Basic Principle
The main principle is:
Beneficial organism+Pathogen→Antagonism/competition/parasitism/induced resistance→Disease↓ xed  Beneficial\ organism + Pathogen  Antagonism/competition/parasitism/induced\
resistance  Disease  
A biological control agent may:
- Compete with the pathogen
- Produce antimicrobial compounds
- Parasitize the pathogen
- Destroy pathogen structures
- Produce enzymes that degrade pathogen cells
- Colonize infection sites
- Improve plant defense
- Induce systemic resistance
- Modify the rhizosphere or phyllosphere

4. Biological Control in the Disease Triangle
Recall:
Host+Pathogen+Environment→Disease 
Biological control mainly acts on the pathogen, but it can also influence the host and environment.
Biocontrol agent→Pathogen pressure↓Biocontrol\ agent  Pathogen\ pressure and sometimes:
Biocontrol agent→Plant defense↑Biocontrol\ agent  Plant\ defense Therefore:
Biological control→Pathogen suppression+Host defense enhancement 

5. Major Biological Control Agents
Important biological agents include:

Fungi
- Trichoderma spp.
- Certain beneficial yeasts
- Other antagonistic fungi

Bacteria
- Bacillus spp.
- Pseudomonas spp.
- Other beneficial rhizobacteria

Actinomycetes
- Some Streptomyces spp.

Other biological agents
Depending on the disease system, biological control can involve:
- Beneficial microorganisms
- Mycorrhizal fungi
- Bacteriophages against specific bacterial pathogens
- Beneficial microbial consortia

- Naturally occurring antagonists

## 6. Trichoderma
Trichoderma is one of the most widely studied and used fungal biological-control groups.
It can suppress pathogens through several mechanisms.
Trichoderma→Competition+Mycoparasitism+Antibiosis+Enzymes+Plant defense 
Potential target pathogens include various soil- and root-associated fungi and fungus-like pathogens,
depending on species and formulation.

## 7. Bacillus
Species and strains of Bacillus can act as biological control agents.
They may:
- Colonize plant surfaces or roots
- Produce antimicrobial compounds
- Compete with pathogens
- Produce enzymes
- Form durable spores in some species
- Stimulate plant defense
Conceptually:
Bacillus→Antimicrobial compounds+Competition+Plant defense→Disease↓Bacillus  Antimicrobial\ compounds + Competition + Plant\ defense  Disease ## 8. Pseudomonas
Certain beneficial Pseudomonas strains are important plant-growth-promoting and biocontrol bacteria.
They may suppress pathogens through:
- Competition
- Antibiotic production
- Siderophore production
- Enzyme production

- Rhizosphere colonization
- Induced plant defense

## 9. Streptomyces
Some Streptomyces species can produce antimicrobial compounds and colonize soil/rhizosphere
environments.
Streptomyces→Antimicrobial metabolites→Pathogen suppressionStreptomyces  Antimicrobial\ metabolites  Pathogen\ suppression

10. Mycorrhiza and Disease Suppression
Mycorrhizal associations can sometimes contribute to disease suppression.
They can:
- Improve root nutrient acquisition
- Improve plant vigor
- Occupy root space
- Alter root chemistry
- Influence microbial communities
- Activate plant defense responses
Therefore:
Mycorrhizal association→Plant vigor/defense↑→Disease impact↓Mycorrhizal\ association  Plant\ vigor/defense  Disease\ impact The effect is pathogen-, crop-, strain- and environment-dependent.

11. Major Mechanisms of Biological Control
The most important mechanisms are:
## 1. Competition
## 2. Antibiosis
## 3. Mycoparasitism
4. Predation or direct antagonism in relevant systems
5. Lytic enzyme production

6. Nutrient competition
7. Siderophore production
8. Induced resistance
9. Plant-growth promotion
10.Colonization/exclusion

## 12. Competition
Competition occurs when the beneficial organism and pathogen require the same limited resources.
They may compete for:
- Nutrients
- Carbon
- Nitrogen
- Iron
- Space
- Infection sites
- Root surfaces
Conceptually:
Beneficial organism+Pathogen→Resource competition→Pathogen growth↓Beneficial\ organism +
Pathogen  Resource\ competition  Pathogen\ growth 13. Competition for Space
A beneficial microorganism may colonize a surface before the pathogen reaches it.
Beneficial microorganism→Root/leaf colonization→Available infection site↓Beneficial\
microorganism  Root/leaf\ colonization  Available\ infection\ site Therefore:
Early colonization→Pathogen establishment↓ 
This is sometimes described as competitive exclusion.

14. Competition for Nutrients
Microorganisms compete for nutrients present around roots or on plant surfaces.
For example:
Nutrients→Beneficial organismNutrients  Beneficial\ organism
leaving fewer resources available for pathogen growth.
However, competition is dynamic and depends on environmental conditions and microbial populations.

15. Competition for Iron — Siderophores
Iron can be limited in many microbial environments.
Some beneficial bacteria produce siderophores, which bind iron strongly.
Fe3++Siderophore→Fe-siderophore complexFe  3+  + Siderophore  Fe 
siderophore\ complex
This can make iron less available to competing microorganisms.
Conceptually:
Siderophore→Fe availability to pathogen↓→Pathogen growth↓Siderophore  Fe\
availability\ to\ pathogen  Pathogen\ growth ## 16. Antibiosis
Antibiosis occurs when a biological control organism produces substances that inhibit or kill another
microorganism.
Biocontrol organism→Antimicrobial compound→Pathogen inhibitionBiocontrol\ organism  Antimicrobial\ compound  Pathogen\ inhibition
These substances may include:
- Antibiotics
- Lipopeptides
- Volatile compounds
- Other antimicrobial metabolites

17. Example of Antibiosis
Certain Bacillus and Pseudomonas strains produce antimicrobial metabolites.
The general pathway is:
Bacterial antagonist→Antimicrobial metabolite→Pathogen growth↓→Disease↓Bacterial\ antagonist
 Antimicrobial\ metabolite  Pathogen\ growth  Disease ## 18. Mycoparasitism
Mycoparasitism is a particularly important mechanism associated with some biocontrol fungi such as
Trichoderma.
The basic sequence is:
Biocontrol fungus→Recognizes pathogen→Attaches→Coils/penetrates→Produces lytic enzymes→Pat
hogen hyphae damagedBiocontrol\ fungus  Recognizes\ pathogen  Attaches
 Coils/penetrates  Produces\ lytic\ enzymes  Pathogen\ hyphae\
damaged
Thus:
Mycoparasitism=Fungus attacks another fungus/fungus−like pathogen 

19. Lytic Enzymes
Biocontrol microorganisms can produce enzymes capable of degrading pathogen structures.
Examples include:
- Chitinases
- β-1,3-glucanases
- Proteases
- Cell-wall-degrading enzymes
For a fungal pathogen:
Enzyme→Cell wall degradation→Hyphal damage→Pathogen suppressionEnzyme  Cell\
wall\ degradation  Hyphal\ damage  Pathogen\ suppression

20. Induced Resistance
Some beneficial microorganisms do not simply kill pathogens directly.
Instead, they can stimulate the plant's own defense system.
Biocontrol organism→Plant recognition→Defense signaling→Plant resistance response↑Biocontrol\
- rganism  Plant\ recognition  Defense\ signaling  Plant\ resistance\
response This is called induced resistance.

21. Induced Systemic Resistance — ISR
Beneficial root-associated microorganisms can trigger induced systemic resistance (ISR).
Conceptually:
Root colonization→Defense signaling→Whole plant defense readiness↑Root\ colonization  Defense\ signaling  Whole\ plant\ defense\ readiness The plant becomes better prepared to respond to subsequent pathogen attack.

22. Systemic Acquired Resistance — SAR
Systemic acquired resistance (SAR) is a plant defense state commonly associated with activation
after infection or certain defense-inducing signals.
It is distinct from ISR in signaling details, although both can increase systemic disease resistance.
Simplified:
Pathogen recognition→Defense signaling→Systemic defensePathogen\ recognition  Defense\ signaling  Systemic\ defense

23. ISR vs SAR
Feature
ISR
SAR
Common trigger
Beneficial microbes
Pathogen infection/defense activation
Association
Rhizobacteria/endophytes
Often pathogen-triggered
Function
Enhances plant defense readiness Systemic acquired defense
Direct pathogen killing Usually indirect
Usually indirect
Role
Preventive/priming
Systemic defense
The exact molecular pathways can overlap and are more complex than this simplified comparison.

24. Plant-Growth Promotion
Some biological control organisms also promote plant growth.
They may improve:
- Nutrient availability
- Root development
- Hormonal signaling
- Stress tolerance
- Plant vigor
A healthier plant may better tolerate or resist disease.
Biocontrol organism→Plant growth/vigor↑→Disease impact↓Biocontrol\ organism  Plant\
growth/vigor  Disease\ impact But plant-growth promotion and disease suppression are distinct functions.

## 25. Colonization
A biological control agent must often successfully establish in the relevant environment.
Important locations include:

Rhizosphere
Soil surrounding roots.

Phyllosphere
Above-ground plant surfaces.

Endosphere
Internal plant tissues inhabited by endophytic microorganisms.
The pathway can be:
Application→Colonization→Persistence→Pathogen suppressionApplication  Colonization
 Persistence  Pathogen\ suppression

26. Rhizosphere Biological Control
The rhizosphere is an important target for soil-borne diseases.
Root↔Rhizosphere microorganismsRoot  Rhizosphere\ microorganisms

Beneficial microbes can:
- Occupy root surfaces
- Compete with pathogens
- Produce antimicrobial compounds
- Improve plant nutrition
- Trigger defense

27. Phyllosphere Biological Control
The phyllosphere is the above-ground plant surface.
Biocontrol microorganisms can colonize:
- Leaves
- Stems
- Flowers
- Fruits
and compete with pathogens on these surfaces.
Leaf surface→Beneficial microbes→Pathogen establishment↓Leaf\ surface  Beneficial\
microbes  Pathogen\ establishment 28. Endophytic Biological Control
Some beneficial microorganisms live inside plant tissues without causing disease.
They can:
- Occupy internal niches
- Produce antimicrobial compounds
- Stimulate defense
- Improve plant vigor
Endophyte→Internal colonization→Pathogen suppression/defense activationEndophyte  Internal\ colonization  Pathogen\ suppression/defense\ activation

29. Biological Control and Disease Cycle
Recall:

Survival→Inoculum→Dissemination→Inoculation→Infection→Colonization→ReproductionSurvival
 Inoculum  Dissemination  Inoculation  Infection
 Colonization  Reproduction
Biological control can interfere with several stages:
Survival↓+Germination↓+Penetration↓+Colonization↓+Reproduction↓ 
Therefore disease development can decrease.

30. Biological Control and Disease Spread
The disease-spread pathway was:
Infected source→Inoculum→Dissemination→New host→InfectionInfected\ source  Inoculum  Dissemination  New\ host  Infection
Biological control can reduce:
Inoculum production→Inoculum↓→Spread↓Inoculum\ production  Inoculum  Spread Thus:
Biological control→Pathogen multiplication↓→Inoculum↓→Disease spread↓ 

31. Biological Control vs Biological Control
Agent
These terms are related but different.

Biological control
The strategy/process of suppressing a pest or pathogen using biological agents.

Biological control agent (BCA)
The specific organism or biological product used for suppression.
Example:
Trichoderma=Biological Control AgentTrichoderma = Biological\ Control\ Agent
while:

Using Trichoderma→Biological ControlUsing\ Trichoderma  Biological\ Control

32. Biological Control vs Biofertilizer
These are not the same.

Biofertilizer
Primarily intended to improve nutrient availability or acquisition.

Biocontrol agent
Primarily intended to suppress pests/pathogens.
However, one microorganism can potentially perform both functions.
Microorganism→Nutrient benefit+Disease suppressionMicroorganism  Nutrient\ benefit +
Disease\ suppression

33. Biological Control vs Biopesticide
A biopesticide is a broader regulatory/product category that may include biological organisms or their
products used to control pests.
Biological control is the ecological/management concept.
Therefore:
Biocontrol agent⊆Some biopesticide productsBiocontrol\ agent  Some\ biopesticide\ products
but the terms should not always be treated as exact synonyms.

34. Natural Biological Control
Natural biological control occurs without deliberate human release.
For example:
Naturally occurring antagonist→Pathogen suppressionNaturally\ occurring\ antagonist  Pathogen\ suppression
Natural microbial communities can naturally regulate pathogen populations.

35. Conservation Biological Control
Conservation biological control means protecting and enhancing naturally occurring beneficial
- rganisms.

Practices may include:
- Avoiding unnecessary broad-spectrum pesticides
- Maintaining suitable soil organic matter
- Preserving beneficial microbial habitats
- Reducing unnecessary disturbance
- Supporting microbial diversity
Conceptually:
Protect beneficial organisms→Natural antagonists↑→Disease suppression↑Protect\ beneficial\
- rganisms  Natural\ antagonists  Disease\ suppression 36. Augmentative Biological Control
Here, beneficial organisms are deliberately produced and introduced/applied.
Mass production→Formulation→Application/release→Biocontrol agent↑→Disease suppressionMass\
production  Formulation  Application/release  Biocontrol\
agent  Disease\ suppression
Microbial products containing beneficial organisms are common examples.

37. Classical Biological Control
Classical biological control generally involves the introduction and establishment of a natural enemy or
biological agent against an introduced pest/pathogen, following appropriate scientific, ecological and
regulatory assessment.
The basic concept is:
Introduced pest/pathogen→Identify effective natural antagonist→Carefully introduce/
establish→Long−term suppressionIntroduced\ pest/pathogen  Identify\ effective\ natural\
antagonist  Carefully\ introduce/establish  Long-term\ suppression
It is subject to strict risk assessment because introduced organisms can have unintended ecological
effects.

38. Production of Biological Control Agents
A generalized production pathway is:
Selection→Culture→Mass multiplication→Formulation→Quality control→Packaging→Storage→Ap
plication 

39. Selection of a Biocontrol Agent
A useful strain should ideally possess:
- Strong antagonistic activity
- Ability to colonize the target environment
- Stability
- Compatibility with crop
- Ability to survive formulation/storage
- Consistent performance
- Safety for the crop
- Appropriate regulatory status

40. Mass Multiplication
The selected organism is multiplied under controlled conditions.
Small starter culture→Large−scale culture→High populationSmall\ starter\ culture  Largescale\ culture  High\ population
The production method depends on whether the agent is:
- Bacterial
- Fungal
- Yeast
- Other microorganism

## 41. Formulation
The biological agent must often be formulated into a product that allows practical application.
Possible formulations include:
- Powders
- Granules

- Wettable formulations
- Liquid formulations
- Seed-treatment formulations
- Soil-applied formulations
The formulation must maintain viability and effectiveness.

42. Quality Control
A biological product should be evaluated for:
- Identity
- Viable population
- Purity
- Contamination
- Stability
- Shelf life
- Moisture where relevant
- Functional activity
- Product consistency
Therefore:
Good biocontrol product=Correct organism+Viability+Purity+Effective formulation 

43. Application Methods
Biocontrol agents can be applied through:

Seed treatment
Seed→BCA→Root−zone establishmentSeed  BCA  Root-zone\ establishment

Soil application
BCA→Soil/rhizosphereBCA  Soil/rhizosphere

Root dipping
Used for certain nursery/transplant systems.

Foliar application
BCA→Leaf surfaceBCA  Leaf\ surface

Irrigation/fertigation systems
Where the product is specifically suitable and compatible with the system.

Nursery application
Useful for protecting young plants.

44. Seed Treatment With Biocontrol Agents
Beneficial microorganisms can be applied to seed before sowing.
Treated seed→Germination→Root colonization→Disease suppressionTreated\ seed  Germination  Root\ colonization  Disease\ suppression
Potential advantages:
- Early establishment
- Protection near the seed
- Root-zone colonization
- Lower initial pathogen pressure

45. Soil Application
For soil-borne diseases:
BCA→Soil→Rhizosphere→Pathogen suppressionBCA  Soil  Rhizosphere
 Pathogen\ suppression
The effectiveness depends strongly on:
- Soil moisture
- Temperature
- Organic matter
- pH
- Microbial competition
- BCA survival

46. Foliar Biological Control
For foliar diseases:
BCA→Leaf surface→Colonization→Pathogen suppressionBCA  Leaf\ surface  Colonization  Pathogen\ suppression
Factors affecting performance include:
- UV radiation
- Temperature
- Humidity
- Rainfall
- Leaf wetness
- Microbial survival

47. Factors Affecting Biological Control
Biological control is strongly environment-dependent.
Effectiveness=f(BCA+Pathogen+Host+Environment+Application)Effectiveness=
f(BCA+Pathogen+Host+Environment+Application)
Important factors include:

Temperature
Microbial growth and pathogen growth are temperature-dependent.

Moisture
Many microorganisms require suitable moisture to survive and colonize.

pH
Soil and surface pH can affect microbial survival.

Organic matter
Can influence microbial populations and nutrient availability.

UV radiation
Can reduce survival of microorganisms applied to exposed surfaces.

Rainfall
Can wash organisms from leaves.

Soil microbial community
Native microorganisms can compete with introduced BCAs.

48. Why Biological Control Sometimes Fails
A biological product can work well in one field but poorly in another.
Possible reasons:
- Incorrect pathogen identification
- Wrong application timing
- Poor product quality
- Low BCA viability
- Extreme temperature
- Low/high moisture
- UV exposure
- Incompatible chemicals
- Poor colonization
- High disease pressure
- Incorrect dose
- Poor storage
- Pathogen not susceptible to the mechanism
Therefore:
Biological control is biological, not automatic. 

49. Timing of Biological Control
Biological control is often most useful when applied before or early in disease development,
depending on the agent and product.
Why?
Because:

Early colonization→Pathogen establishment↓Early\ colonization  Pathogen\
establishment Whereas:
Advanced disease→Extensive pathogen colonization→More difficult suppressionAdvanced\ disease
 Extensive\ pathogen\ colonization  More\ difficult\ suppression
This is not universal; product-specific recommendations must be followed.

50. Compatibility With Chemical Pesticides
Chemical pesticides may:
- Kill the BCA
- Inhibit its growth
- Reduce colonization
- Have little effect
- Sometimes be compatible
Therefore:
BCA+ChemicalBCA+Chemical
should not automatically be assumed compatible.
Compatibility should be based on:
- Active ingredient
- Formulation
- Concentration
- Timing
- Application method
- BCA species/strain
- Product label/manufacturer information

51. Biological Control and Integrated Disease
Management
Biological control fits naturally into IDM:

IDM=Resistance+Sanitation+Cultural practices+Biological control+Chemical control+Monitoring ed  IDM= Resistance+ Sanitation+ Cultural\ practices+ Biological\ control+ Chemical\ control+
Monitoring  
A practical sequence is:
Diagnose→Prevent→Monitor→Apply biocontrol where appropriate→Evaluate→Use additional comp
atible methods if requiredDiagnose  Prevent  Monitor  Apply\
biocontrol\ where\ appropriate  Evaluate  Use\ additional\ compatible\ methods\
if\ required

52. Advantages of Biological Control
1. Reduced dependence on synthetic pesticides
Chemical dependence↓Chemical\ dependence 2. Potentially lower non-target impact
When properly selected and used.

3. Resistance-management value
Biological mechanisms can provide alternatives to repeated chemical modes of action.

4. Environmental benefits
Potentially less chemical contamination.

5. Compatibility with sustainable agriculture
It can fit into:
- Organic systems where approved
- Integrated systems
- Low-input systems
- Sustainable disease management

6. Multiple mechanisms
One BCA may act through:
Competition+Antibiosis+Defense inductionCompetition+Antibiosis+Defense\ induction

## 53. Limitations
1. Variable field performance
Environment changes→BCA performance changesEnvironment\ changes  BCA\
performance\ changes

2. Slower action
Some biological agents do not produce rapid knockdown.

3. Storage sensitivity
Some microorganisms require specific storage conditions.

4. Shelf-life limitations
Viability may decrease over time.

5. High pathogen pressure
Severe epidemics may require additional control methods.

6. Compatibility problems
Some pesticides can harm beneficial microorganisms.

7. Need for correct timing
Late application may reduce effectiveness.

54. Biological Control vs Chemical Control
Feature
Main agent
Main mechanisms
Speed
Environmental
dependence
Resistance pressure
Persistence
Natural ecosystem
integration

Biological control
Living organism/biological product
Competition, antibiosis, parasitism,
induced resistance
Often slower

Chemical control
Chemical active ingredient

Often high

Also environment-dependent

Generally different/less direct
Often variable

Can select chemical resistance
Product-dependent

Generally high when appropriate

Can affect non-target organisms

Consistency

Can vary

Role in IDM

Important component

Toxicity or physiological inhibition
Often faster

Often more predictable when
correctly used
Important component when needed

Neither method should automatically be considered a complete replacement for the other.

55. Biological Control vs Sanitation
These two previous topics work together.

Sanitation
Remove pathogen source→Inoculum↓Remove\ pathogen\ source  Inoculum Biological control
Beneficial organism→Pathogen suppressionBeneficial\ organism  Pathogen\ suppression
Together:
Sanitation+Biological control→Lower pathogen pressure 

56. Biological Control vs Resistant Varieties
Resistant variety
Acts primarily through the host.
Host resistance→Disease development↓Host\ resistance  Disease\ development Biological control
Acts primarily through beneficial organisms and/or plant defense stimulation.
BCA→Pathogen suppression/Defense↑BCA  Pathogen\ suppression/Defense Combined:
Resistant host+Beneficial microorganisms→Multiple barriers to disease 

57. Biological Control and Disease Epidemiology
For polycyclic disease:
I0→I1→I2→I3→...I 0 I 1 I 2 I 3 ...
If biological control reduces each generation's inoculum:
I0↓→I1↓→I2↓→I3↓I 0  I 1  I 2  I 3 Therefore:

Small reductions in pathogen reproduction can potentially reduce later epidemic development.
 

58. Important Example — Trichoderma
A simplified interaction:
Trichoderma→Root colonizationTrichoderma  Root\ colonization
Then:
Competition+Mycoparasitism+Enzymes+AntibiosisCompetition+Mycoparasitism+Enzymes+Antibiosi
s
leading to:
Pathogen growth↓Pathogen\ growth and potentially:
Plant defense↑Plant\ defense Therefore:
Trichoderma→Direct pathogen suppression+Indirect plant defense 

59. Important Example — Bacillus
Bacillus→Root/leaf colonizationBacillus  Root/leaf\ colonization
Possible mechanisms:
