Antimicrobial metabolites+Competition+Siderophores+Lytic enzymes+Induced resistanceAntimicrobia
l\ metabolites + Competition + Siderophores + Lytic\ enzymes + Induced\ resistance
leading to:
Disease development↓Disease\ development 60. Biological Control and Soil Microbiome
The soil is not biologically empty.
It contains:
- Bacteria
- Fungi

- Actinomycetes
- Protozoa
- Nematodes
- Other microorganisms
Introducing a BCA means it enters an existing microbial community.
Therefore:
BCA+Native microbiome→Competition/interactionBCA+Native\ microbiome  Competition/interaction
This helps explain why field performance can vary.

61. Biological Control and Microbial Consortia
Sometimes multiple compatible beneficial microorganisms are used together.
BCA1+BCA2+BCA3→Multiple mechanismsBCA 1+BCA 2+BCA 3  Multiple\
mechanisms
Potential benefits include:
- Complementary mechanisms
- Broader activity
- Better environmental adaptation
But compatibility must be scientifically demonstrated; combining microorganisms does not
automatically improve performance.

62. Safety Considerations
Biological does not automatically mean harmless.
Before using a biological product, consider:
- Correct organism identification
- Product registration/approval
- Crop safety
- Target specificity
- Non-target effects
- Worker safety

- Storage
- Application instructions
- Compatibility
Always follow the relevant product label and local regulatory requirements.

63. Common Mistakes
Mistake 1: "Biological control means zero chemicals."
Incorrect.
Biological control is one component of IDM.

Mistake 2: "All microorganisms are beneficial."
Incorrect.
Some microorganisms cause plant diseases.

Mistake 3: "Trichoderma kills every pathogen."
Incorrect.
Activity depends on species/strain, pathogen, crop and environment.

Mistake 4: "Biocontrol works instantly."
Not necessarily.
Many biological agents need time to establish and act.

Mistake 5: "Any pesticide can be mixed with a biocontrol agent."
Incorrect.
Chemical compatibility must be checked.

Mistake 6: "More biological agent always means better control."
Incorrect.
Dose, viability, colonization and environment matter.

64. Complete Biological Control Workflow
Identify disease→Identify pathogen→Select suitable BCA→Check quality→Choose application metho
d→Apply at appropriate time→Allow colonization/activity→Monitor→Evaluate 

65. Biological Control in IDM
A practical integrated system could look like:
Healthy planting material 
↓
Resistant variety 
↓
Sanitation 
↓
Crop rotation+cultural practices 
↓
Biological control 
↓
Monitoring 
↓
Chemical control only when necessary and appropriate 
This is much closer to Integrated Disease Management than relying on any single method.

66. Key Formulas / Conceptual Relationships
Pathogen suppression
BCA→Pathogen growth↓ 

Inoculum reduction
BCA→Reproduction↓→Inoculum↓ 

Disease suppression
BCA→Infection/colonization↓→Disease↓ 

Defense induction
BCA→Plant defense activation→Disease susceptibility↓ 

Epidemic suppression
Pathogen multiplication↓→Secondary inoculum↓→Disease spread↓ 

67. Exam-Ready Answer
Biological control of plant diseases is the suppression of plant pathogens and disease
development through the use or manipulation of beneficial living organisms or their
biological products. Important biological control agents include species and strains of
Trichoderma, Bacillus, Pseudomonas, Streptomyces and other beneficial
microorganisms. Major mechanisms include competition for nutrients and space,
antibiosis, siderophore production, mycoparasitism, production of lytic enzymes,
pathogen exclusion and induction of plant defense mechanisms such as induced
systemic resistance. Biological control agents may be applied through seed treatment,
soil application, root treatment, nursery application, foliar application or other
suitable methods. Their effectiveness depends on the biological control agent,
pathogen, crop, environmental conditions, formulation, application timing and
compatibility with other management practices. Biological control is therefore an
important component of integrated disease management and is generally most
effective when combined with resistant varieties, sanitation, crop rotation, monitoring
and other compatible practices.

68. Final Memory
One-line definition
BIOLOGICAL CONTROL = USE BENEFICIAL ORGANISMS TO SUPPRESS PLANT PATHOGE
NS AND DISEASE   
Five major mechanisms to remember:
COMPETE + ANTAGONIZE + PARASITIZE + DEGRADE + DEFEND   
Where:
- Compete → nutrients and space

- Antagonize → antimicrobial compounds
- Parasitize → attack pathogen
- Degrade → enzymes destroy pathogen structures
- Defend → stimulate plant immunity

Most important biological-control organisms:
Trichoderma+Bacillus+Pseudomonas+Streptomyces 

Complete concept:
BCA→Competition/Antibiosis/Mycoparasitism/Enzymes/
Defense→Pathogen↓→Inoculum↓→Disease spread↓→Crop loss↓ 
Core principle: biological control does not simply mean "killing the pathogen with another organism."
It can work by competition, direct antagonism, pathogen parasitism, nutrient deprivation,
enzymatic degradation, colonization, and activation of the plant's own defense system.

## Module 16 — Plant Disease
## Management
## 18. Fungicides / Bactericides Where
## Appropriate
## 1. Introduction
Fungicides and bactericides are chemical or biologically derived disease-management products used
to prevent, suppress, or control fungal, oomycete, and bacterial plant diseases, respectively, when
their use is justified.
They are an important component of Integrated Disease Management (IDM), but they should not
automatically be the first or only response to every disease.
The central principle is:
Correct Diagnosis→Correct Product→Correct Timing→Correct Application→Disease suppression xed  Correct\ Diagnosis  Correct\ Product  Correct\ Timing  Correct\
Application  Disease\ suppression  

2. Why "Where Appropriate" Matters
The phrase "where appropriate" is extremely important.
Chemical disease control should be considered only when:
- The causal organism has been correctly identified or strongly established.
- The selected product is effective against that pathogen.
- The crop is legally registered/approved for that use.
- Disease pressure or risk justifies intervention.
- Environmental conditions allow effective application.
- Other management methods alone are insufficient.
- The application can be made safely and economically.
Therefore:
Disease observed≠Automatically spray 
Instead:
Diagnose→Assess risk→Decide→Treat if justified 

## 3. Definitions
Fungicide
A fungicide is a substance or product used to prevent, inhibit, suppress or kill fungi or
fungus-like pathogens responsible for plant diseases.
Important clarification:
Some important plant pathogens traditionally grouped with "fungi" are actually oomycetes, such as
Phytophthora and Pythium. A fungicide is not necessarily effective against every fungus or oomycete.

Bactericide
A bactericide is a substance or product used to suppress or kill plant-pathogenic bacteria or
reduce bacterial disease development.
Bacterial diseases can be particularly difficult to control once bacteria have become established inside
plant tissues.
Therefore:

Prevention+Early management 
is often particularly important.

4. Fungicide vs Bactericide
Feature
Main target

Fungicide
Fungal/fungus-like pathogens
Fungi and some oomycetes
Target organisms
depending on product
Examples of
Rusts, mildews, leaf spots, rots,
diseases
some wilts
Timing
Often preventive or early
Contact or systemic depending
Movement
- n product
Resistance
Can develop
Diagnosis
Essential

Bactericide
Plant-pathogenic bacteria
Bacteria
Blights, bacterial spots, cankers, bacterial wilts
Often preventive/early
Mostly protective; some products may have
limited systemic/indirect effects
Can also develop
Essential

5. Chemical Disease Management in IDM
Chemical control should fit into:
IDM=Resistance+Sanitation+Cultural practices+Biological control+Monitoring+Chemical protection - xed  IDM= Resistance+ Sanitation+ Cultural\ practices+ Biological\ control+ Monitoring+ Chemical\
protection  
The objective is not:
Maximum pesticide useMaximum\ pesticide\ use
but:
Maximum disease control+Minimum unnecessary chemical use 

6. The First Step — Correct Diagnosis
Before selecting a fungicide or bactericide:
Crop+Symptoms+Signs+Field pattern+Environment+History→Diagnosis 
For example, yellowing may result from:
- Fungal disease

- Bacterial disease
- Virus
- Nematodes
- Nutrient deficiency
- Drought
- Waterlogging
- Salinity
- Herbicide injury
Therefore:
Symptom≠Disease diagnosis 

7. Fungicides Are Not Effective Against
Everything
A fungicide designed for a fungal pathogen will not necessarily control:
- Viruses
- Viroids
- Phytoplasmas
- Nematodes
- Insects
- Abiotic disorders
Similarly:
Fungicide≠Universal plant medicineFungicide Universal\ plant\ medicine

8. Bactericides Are Not Effective Against Viruses
This is another important distinction.
Virus disease↛BactericideVirus\ disease   Bactericide
If an insect vector is transmitting a virus:
Virus→Vector→Healthy plantVirus  Vector  Healthy\ plant
management may instead involve:

- Resistant varieties
- Healthy planting material
- Vector management
- Sanitation
- Removal of infected plants where appropriate
- Exclusion

9. Classification of Fungicides by Movement
One major classification is based on movement within/on the plant.

A. Contact fungicides
Remain primarily on the plant surface.
Fungicide→Plant surface→Pathogen contact/germination inhibitedFungicide  Plant\ surface
 Pathogen\ contact/germination\ inhibited
They generally provide protective rather than deep curative activity.

B. Systemic or locally systemic fungicides
Can be absorbed and move within plant tissues to varying degrees, depending on the active ingredient.
Application→Absorption→Plant tissue→Target siteApplication  Absorption  Plant\ tissue  Target\ site
The term "systemic" should not be interpreted as meaning that every product moves throughout the
entire plant.

10. Protective Fungicides
Protective fungicides are applied before infection or before substantial disease establishment.
Fungicide→Plant protection→Pathogen infection↓ 
They are especially useful when:
- Disease risk is predictable
- Weather favors disease
- Pathogen inoculum is present

- The crop is at a susceptible stage

11. Curative / Post-Infection Activity
Some fungicides can suppress pathogen development after infection has begun, depending on:
- Active ingredient
- Pathogen
- Disease stage
- Crop
- Application timing
But:
Post−infection activity≠Guaranteed cure 
Once pathogen colonization and tissue damage are extensive, chemical control may not restore already
damaged tissue.

12. Eradicant Activity
Some products may have activity against established pathogen structures on plant surfaces or in
specific disease systems.
However, "eradicant" should be interpreted according to the particular product and pathogen rather than
assuming complete elimination.

13. Major Fungicide Target Concepts
Fungicides can interfere with processes such as:
- Cell membrane formation
- Sterol biosynthesis
- Cell wall formation
- Respiration
- Mitosis/cell division
- Amino acid or protein synthesis
- Nucleic acid processes

- Energy production
- Spore germination
- Enzyme function
Simplified:
Fungicide→Target process→Pathogen growth/reproduction↓Fungicide  Target\ process
 Pathogen\ growth/reproduction 14. Mode of Action — MOA
Mode of action (MOA) describes the biological process or target affected by a pesticide.
For example:
Fungicide→Specific cellular target→Physiological disruption→Pathogen growth inhibitionFungicide
 Specific\ cellular\ target  Physiological\ disruption  Pathogen\
growth\ inhibition
MOA is extremely important for fungicide-resistance management.

15. Multi-Site Fungicides
Some fungicides affect multiple biochemical processes/sites.
Conceptually:
Multiple cellular targets→Pathogen growth disruptionMultiple\ cellular\ targets  Pathogen\
growth\ disruption
They often have lower resistance risk than highly specific single-site fungicides, although resistance
risk is never simply zero.

16. Single-Site Fungicides
Single-site or site-specific fungicides act primarily on a particular biochemical target.
Specific target→Specific inhibitionSpecific\ target  Specific\ inhibition
They can be highly effective but may carry greater resistance-selection risk.
Therefore:
High specificity→Resistance management becomes especially important 

17. Fungicide Resistance
Fungicide resistance occurs when a pathogen population becomes less sensitive to a fungicide or
fungicide group that previously controlled it.
The selection process can be represented as:
Fungicide→Susceptible pathogens die→Less sensitive individuals survive→Reproduce→Resistance fr
equency↑Fungicide  Susceptible\ pathogens\ die  Less\ sensitive\ individuals\
survive  Reproduce  Resistance\ frequency Eventually:
Control effectiveness↓Control\ effectiveness 18. How to Reduce Fungicide Resistance
Important principles include:

1. Do not spray unnecessarily
Unnecessary application→Selection pressure↑Unnecessary\ application  Selection\
pressure 2. Rotate effective MOAs
MOA1→MOA2→MOA3MOA 1  MOA 2  MOA 3
where appropriate and according to recommendations.

3. Avoid repeatedly using the same single-site MOA
Repeated exposure can increase selection pressure.

4. Use mixtures appropriately
A mixture should contain effective, compatible components with appropriate resistance-management
characteristics and be used according to the label.

5. Combine nonchemical methods
Resistance+Sanitation+Biocontrol+ChemicalResistance+Sanitation+Biocontrol+Chemical
is generally stronger than relying repeatedly on one chemical mode of action.

## 19. Cross-Resistance
Cross-resistance occurs when resistance to one active ingredient or MOA also reduces sensitivity to
another related fungicide because of a shared resistance mechanism.

Resistance to A→Reduced sensitivity to BResistance\ to\ A  Reduced\ sensitivity\ to\ B
if both depend on related mechanisms.
Therefore:
Changing brand name does not necessarily change MOA 

20. Chemical Group vs Brand Name
This is an important practical concept.
Two different commercial products may contain:
- The same active ingredient
- The same MOA
- Different active ingredients but the same MOA
Therefore:
Rotate MOA, not merely product brand. 
Always check the active ingredient and resistance-group information on the current product label.

## 21. Bactericides
Bactericides are used against bacterial plant pathogens.
Bacterial diseases include:
- Bacterial blights
- Bacterial spots
- Bacterial cankers
- Bacterial soft rots
- Some bacterial wilts
The basic pathway is:
Bactericide→Bacterial population/activity↓→Disease development↓Bactericide  Bacterial\
population/activity  Disease\ development 22. Why Bacterial Diseases Can Be Difficult to
Control
Bacteria can:
- Multiply rapidly under favorable conditions
- Spread through water
- Enter through wounds
- Enter natural openings
- Be carried by tools and workers
- Be transmitted through planting material
- Be transported by insects in some diseases
Once bacteria are established inside plant tissues:
External treatment↛Complete elimination of internal infectionExternal\ treatment   Complete\ elimination\ of\ internal\ infection
Therefore prevention and sanitation are very important.

23. Copper-Based Bactericides
Copper compounds are widely used in some bacterial disease-management programs.
Their general action involves:
Copper ions→Damage to microbial proteins/membranes and cellular processes→Bacterial growth supp
ressionCopper\ ions  Damage\ to\ microbial\ proteins/membranes\ and\ cellular\ processes
 Bacterial\ growth\ suppression
Copper products may also have activity against some fungal/oomycete pathogens depending on the
product and use.
However:
Copper is not universally effective 
and excessive copper use can cause:
- Phytotoxicity
- Soil copper accumulation
- Selection for reduced sensitivity in some bacterial populations

24. Antibiotic-Type Plant Disease Products
Some jurisdictions permit specific antimicrobial compounds for particular plant diseases.
Their use is highly regulated and pathogen-specific.
Important principles:
- Use only registered/approved products.
- Follow the label.
- Avoid unnecessary use.
- Resistance management is important.
- Availability and legality vary by country.
Therefore:
Antimicrobial product≠Automatically appropriate for every bacterial disease. 

25. Preventive Bactericide Application
Preventive application can be useful when disease risk is predictable.
For example:
High disease risk+Susceptible crop+Favorable weather→Preventive protectionHigh\ disease\ risk +
Susceptible\ crop + Favorable\ weather  Preventive\ protection
The exact timing must follow the crop/pathogen/product recommendation.

26. Fungicide Application Timing
Timing can be represented as:
Before infection>Very early infection>Advanced disease 
for many protective disease-management situations.
The exact relationship varies by active ingredient and disease.
The key principle is:
Treat according to the pathogen′s infection biology, not simply when symptoms become obvious.
 

27. Disease Forecasting and Fungicide Timing
Disease forecasting can combine:
- Temperature
- Relative humidity
- Rainfall
- Leaf wetness
- Crop stage
- Pathogen biology
- Disease history
Conceptually:
Weather+Crop stage+Pathogen→Disease risk→Treatment decisionWeather+Crop\ stage+Pathogen
 Disease\ risk  Treatment\ decision
This is often better than blindly following a fixed calendar.

28. Spray Coverage
For contact products:
Good coverage→More treated surface→Better protectionGood\ coverage  More\ treated\
surface  Better\ protection
Poor coverage can leave untreated infection sites.
Coverage depends on:
- Nozzle
- Spray volume
- Droplet characteristics
- Canopy structure
- Equipment calibration
- Application speed
- Wind
- Crop growth stage

29. Systemic Product Application
For systemic products:
Application→Absorption→Movement→Target tissueApplication  Absorption  Movement  Target\ tissue
But movement varies significantly among products.
Therefore:
"Systemic"does not mean "reaches every part of the plant." 

30. Seed Treatment
Some fungicides and biological products can be used as seed treatments where registered.
Purpose:
Seed treatment→Seed/soil−borne pathogen suppression→Healthy establishmentSeed\ treatment
 Seed/soil-borne\ pathogen\ suppression  Healthy\ establishment
Potential targets include pathogens causing:
- Seed rot
- Seedling damping-off
- Early root disease

31. Soil Application
Some disease-management products can be applied to soil.
Product→Soil/rhizosphere→Pathogen suppressionProduct  Soil/rhizosphere  Pathogen\ suppression
This may be relevant to certain soil-borne diseases.
Effectiveness depends on:
- Soil type
- Organic matter
- Moisture
- Pathogen location
- Product movement

- Application method

32. Foliar Application
Foliar application is common for many leaf diseases.
Spray→Leaf surface→Pathogen inhibitionSpray  Leaf\ surface  Pathogen\
inhibition
It is used for diseases such as some:
- Leaf spots
- Rusts
- Mildews
- Blights
But the correct product depends on the specific pathogen.

33. Fungicide vs Herbicide
Do not confuse them.
Fungicide
Herbicide
Controls/suppresses fungal/fungus-like pathogens Controls weeds
Targets pathogens
Targets unwanted plants
Used in disease management
Used in weed management
Fungicide≠HerbicideFungicide Herbicide

34. Fungicide vs Insecticide
Similarly:
Fungicide→Disease/pathogenFungicide  Disease/pathogen
Insecticide→Insect pestInsecticide  Insect\ pest
If an insect is transmitting a virus:
Virus→VectorVirus  Vector
controlling the vector may reduce transmission, but it does not mean the insecticide directly cures the
virus-infected plant.

35. Chemical Control and Sanitation
These methods complement each other.

Sanitation
Inoculum source↓Inoculum\ source Fungicide/bactericide
Pathogen infection/development↓Pathogen\ infection/development Together:
Sanitation+Chemical protection→Disease pressure↓ 

36. Chemical Control and Biological Control
These can complement each other, but compatibility must be checked.
Biocontrol agent+ChemicalBiocontrol\ agent + Chemical
may result in:
- Compatibility
- Reduced BCA viability
- Reduced BCA activity
- Sometimes acceptable integrated performance
Therefore:
Never assume that a chemical is compatible with a biological control agent. 
Follow product-specific compatibility guidance.

37. Chemical Control and Resistant Varieties
Combining host resistance with chemical protection can reduce disease pressure.
Resistant variety→Disease risk↓Resistant\ variety  Disease\ risk plus:
Appropriate fungicide/bactericide→Additional suppressionAppropriate\ fungicide/bactericide
 Additional\ suppression

This may be especially useful when resistance is partial.

38. Chemical Control and Crop Rotation
Crop rotation can reduce pathogen survival.
Rotation→Suitable host availability↓Rotation  Suitable\ host\ availability Then:
Chemical protection→Additional disease suppressionChemical\ protection  Additional\
disease\ suppression
This is more sustainable than relying on chemicals alone.

39. Disease Management Decision Tree
A practical decision process is:
OBSERVE 
↓
IDENTIFY 
↓
CONFIRM CAUSE 
↓
ASSESS DISEASE RISK 
↓
CAN CULTURAL/RESISTANT/BIOLOGICAL/SANITATION METHODS SUFFICE? 
↓
If not:
SELECT APPROPRIATE REGISTERED PRODUCT 
↓
CHECK LABEL+MOA+TIMING+RATE+SAFETY 
↓

APPLY CORRECTLY 
↓
MONITOR RESPONSE 

40. Four Important "Rights"
Chemical disease management should follow:
Right Product+Right Rate+Right Time+Right Place 
This can be expanded to include:
Right Diagnosis+Right Product+Right Rate+Right Time+Right Application 

41. Importance of the Label
The product label is the primary source for:
- Registered crop
- Target disease
- Application rate
- Application interval
- Maximum applications
- Mixing instructions
- PPE
- Pre-harvest interval
- Re-entry interval
- Crop restrictions
- Resistance-management instructions
- Environmental precautions
Therefore:
Label=Legal+Technical basis for product use 
Local registration requirements vary by jurisdiction.

## 42. Phytotoxicity
A fungicide or bactericide can sometimes injure the crop.
Possible symptoms:
- Leaf burn
- Chlorosis
- Necrosis
- Stunting
- Fruit injury
Therefore:
Chemical dose/condition unsuitable→Crop injuryChemical\ dose/condition\ unsuitable  Crop\ injury
Correct product selection and label compliance are essential.

43. Environmental Conditions
Application effectiveness depends on:
- Temperature
- Rainfall
- Wind
- Humidity
- Leaf wetness
- Crop stage
- Disease stage
For example:
Rainfall immediately after application→Product removal possibleRainfall\ immediately\ after\
application  Product\ removal\ possible
depending on product and label.
Strong wind can cause:
Drift→Non−target exposureDrift  Non-target\ exposure

44. Spray Drift
Spray drift is movement of pesticide droplets or vapors away from the intended application area.
Potential consequences:
- Crop injury
- Environmental contamination
- Exposure of workers/bystanders
- Damage to neighboring crops
- Effects on beneficial organisms
Risk depends on:
- Wind
- Droplet size
- Nozzle
- Spray pressure
- Boom height
- Temperature
- Formulation

45. Pre-Harvest Interval — PHI
PHI is the required interval between the last pesticide application and harvest, as specified by the
applicable product label.
Conceptually:
Last application→Required interval→HarvestLast\ application  Required\ interval
 Harvest
It is important for:
- Residue management
- Food safety
- Legal compliance

46. Re-Entry Interval — REI
REI is the period after pesticide application during which workers should not enter the treated area
except according to specified protective requirements.
Application→REI→Permitted re−entryApplication  REI  Permitted\ re-entry
Follow the product label.

47. Worker Safety
Chemical disease-control products should be handled according to the label and safety documentation.
Important principles:
- Wear specified PPE
- Avoid skin/eye contact
- Avoid inhalation
- Do not eat/drink/smoke while handling
- Use calibrated equipment
- Store products safely
- Keep away from food/feed
- Wash after handling
- Follow spill and disposal procedures

48. Storage and Disposal
Products should generally be:
- Kept in original labeled containers
- Stored securely
- Protected from inappropriate temperature/moisture
- Kept away from food, feed and seed
- Kept away from unauthorized users
Disposal must follow:
Product label+Local regulations 

Never dispose of pesticide residues by dumping them into drains, water bodies or unsuitable areas.

49. Fungicide Resistance vs Host Resistance
This is a very important distinction.

Host resistance
Plant genetics→Pathogen development↓Plant\ genetics  Pathogen\ development Fungicide resistance
Pathogen genetics→Fungicide sensitivity↓Pathogen\ genetics  Fungicide\
sensitivity Therefore:
Host resistance≠Fungicide resistance 

50. Why Repeated Spraying Can Be Dangerous
Repeated unnecessary spraying can cause:
Repeated application→Selection pressure→ResistanceRepeated\ application  Selection\
pressure  Resistance
and potentially:
- Residues
- Environmental contamination
- Non-target effects
- Phytotoxicity
- Higher production cost
Therefore:
More spraying≠Better disease management 

51. When Chemical Control Is Particularly
Useful
Chemical protection may be appropriate when:
- Disease is correctly diagnosed.

- The pathogen is susceptible to the product.
- Disease risk is economically important.
- The crop is at a susceptible stage.
- Environmental conditions favor disease.
- Resistant varieties are unavailable or only partially effective.
- Sanitation and cultural methods are insufficient.
- The product is registered/approved for the crop and disease.
- Application can be made safely.

52. When Chemical Control May Be
Inappropriate
Chemical control may be inappropriate when:
- The disease is viral and the product does not target the virus.
- The problem is abiotic.
- The pathogen is not susceptible.
- Disease is already too advanced for meaningful protection.
- Disease pressure is negligible.
- A more appropriate nonchemical method is sufficient.
- The product is not registered for the intended use.
- Environmental conditions make application unsafe or ineffective.

53. Fungicide/Bactericide Decision Framework
Diagnosis→Pathogen→Disease risk→Economic importance→Management options→Product selectio
n→Timing→Application→Monitoring 
This is the correct conceptual approach.

54. Common Mistakes
Mistake 1: Spraying before identifying the disease
Incorrect.
Wrong diagnosis→Wrong product→No controlWrong\ diagnosis  Wrong\ product
 No\ control

Mistake 2: Using fungicide for a virus
Incorrect.

Mistake 3: Using bactericide for a fungal disease
Usually inappropriate unless the product has a relevant registered activity against the actual causal
- rganism.

Mistake 4: Using a higher dose to get faster control
Higher dose can increase:
- Crop injury
- Residues
- Environmental risk
- Resistance selection
Always follow the label.

Mistake 5: Changing brand but not MOA
This may not reduce resistance selection.

Mistake 6: Treating advanced disease as if it were an early infection
Chemical protection cannot reliably restore tissue that is already dead or severely damaged.

Mistake 7: Ignoring sanitation
Chemical control is more effective when pathogen sources are also managed.

55. Complete IDM Integration
The best conceptual model is:
PREVENT→IDENTIFY→MONITOR→ASSESS→DECIDE→INTEGRATE→RE−MONITOR d  PREVENT  IDENTIFY  MONITOR  ASSESS  DECIDE  INTEGRATE  RE-MONITOR  

Chemical control is one possible component:
Resistant variety+Sanitation+Cultural practices+Biological control+Chemical protection 
The exact combination depends on the crop-pathogen system.

56. Complete Disease-Management Flow
Healthy Seed 
↓
Resistant Variety 
↓
Sanitation 
↓
Crop Rotation+Cultural Practices 
↓
Biological Control 
↓
Disease Monitoring 
↓
Fungicide/Bactericide When Justified 
↓
Evaluate+Re−monitor 
This is the essence of integrated disease management.

57. Exam-Ready Answer
Fungicides and bactericides are chemical or biological disease-management products
used to suppress fungal, oomycete or bacterial plant pathogens when their use is
justified. Fungicides may act as contact, locally systemic or systemic products and
may be protective or, depending on the active ingredient and disease, have postinfection activity. Bactericides are primarily used against bacterial diseases and are
- ften most useful preventively or during early disease development. Their effective use
requires correct disease diagnosis, selection of an appropriate registered product,

proper timing, correct application, adherence to label instructions and resistancemanagement practices. Repeated unnecessary use of the same fungicide mode of
action can select resistant pathogen populations. Fungicides and bactericides should
therefore be integrated with resistant varieties, sanitation, crop rotation, cultural
practices, biological control and regular disease monitoring rather than being used as
the sole disease-management strategy.

58. Final Memory
Core principle
DIAGNOSE → ASSESS → SELECT → TIME → APPLY → MONITOR   
Fungicide
Fungicide→Fungal/Fungus-like pathogen suppression like\ pathogen\ suppression  

Bactericide
Bactericide→Bacterial pathogen suppression 

Resistance management
Avoid unnecessary spraying+Rotate effective MOAs+Use nonchemical methods 

IDM relationship
Resistance+Sanitation+Biological Control+Cultural Management+Chemical Protection where appropri
ate 

Most important takeaway
A fungicide or bactericide should be a targeted disease-management tool, not a substitute for diagnosis
and integrated management.   

## Module 16 — Plant Disease
## Management
## 19. Seed Treatment
## 1. Introduction
Seed treatment is the process of treating seeds before sowing to protect them from seed-borne and
soil-borne pathogens, improve early seedling establishment, and in some cases improve
germination and early plant growth.
It is one of the most important preventive disease-management practices because it protects the crop
at the earliest stage.
The basic concept is:
Seed treatment→Pathogen load↓→Seed/seedling infection↓→Healthy establishment↑ 
Seed treatment can involve:
- Chemical fungicides
- Bactericidal products where appropriate
- Biological control agents
- Insecticidal treatments where appropriate
- Physical treatments
- Combination treatments

## 2. Definition
Simple definition
Seed treatment is the application of a chemical, biological or physical treatment to
seed before sowing to protect the seed and developing seedling from pathogens and
- ther harmful organisms.

Exam-ready definition
Seed treatment is a pre-sowing practice in which seeds are treated with suitable
chemical, biological or physical agents to control seed-borne and soil-borne pathogens,

protect germinating seeds and seedlings, improve establishment and reduce earlyseason disease incidence.

3. Why Seed Treatment Is Important
Seeds can carry pathogens:
Seed→Pathogen→Seedling→Crop 
A contaminated seed lot can introduce disease into a field even when the field was previously relatively
clean.
Seed treatment attempts to break this pathway:
Treated seed→Pathogen suppression→Healthy germination→Healthy establishment 

4. Objectives of Seed Treatment
The major objectives are:
1. Control seed-borne pathogens
2. Reduce seed infection
3. Reduce soil-borne infection
4. Protect germinating seeds
5. Protect young seedlings
6. Reduce damping-off
7. Improve plant establishment
8. Reduce early disease spread
9. Improve seedling vigor in some cases
10.Deliver beneficial microorganisms
11.Sometimes protect against early insect pests

5. Seed-Borne Pathogens
A pathogen may be associated with seed:

Externally
Present on the seed surface.
Pathogen→Seed surfacePathogen Seed\ surface

Internally
Present inside seed tissues.
Pathogen→Internal seed tissuesPathogen Internal\ seed\ tissues

With seed material
Associated with seed lots or contaminating material.
Therefore:
Seed treatment effectivenessdepends partly on where the pathogen is located. 

6. Seed-Borne vs Soil-Borne Pathogens
Seed-borne pathogen
Travels with the seed.
Infected seed→Sowing→Seedling→DiseaseInfected\ seed  Sowing  Seedling
 Disease

Soil-borne pathogen
Survives in soil and infects the seed/seedling.
Pathogen in soil→Germinating seed→InfectionPathogen\ in\ soil  Germinating\ seed
 Infection
Seed treatment can help address both situations depending on the product and pathogen.

7. Major Types of Seed Treatment
Seed treatments can be classified as:

1. Chemical seed treatment
Uses registered chemical products.

2. Biological seed treatment
Uses beneficial microorganisms or biological products.

3. Physical seed treatment
Uses physical methods such as:
- Hot-water treatment
- Heat treatment
- Other approved physical methods

4. Insecticidal seed treatment
Uses appropriate insect-control products where registered.

5. Combination treatment
Uses more than one compatible treatment.

8. Chemical Seed Treatment
Chemical seed treatment commonly uses registered fungicidal or other pesticide products to protect
seed and seedlings.
General pathway:
Chemical→Seed→Pathogen suppression→Disease risk↓Chemical  Seed  Pathogen\ suppression  Disease\ risk The exact active ingredient must be selected according to:
- Crop
- Pathogen
- Seed type
- Disease
- Product registration
- Label instructions

9. Fungicidal Seed Treatment
Fungicidal seed treatment is primarily used against fungal or fungus-like pathogens associated with
seed or soil.
It can protect against diseases such as:
- Seed rots

- Seedling rots
- Damping-off
- Some smuts
- Some bunts
- Certain seed-borne leaf/seedling diseases
The exact target depends on crop and pathogen.

10. Bactericidal Seed Treatment
Where an appropriate, registered treatment exists, seed treatment may also be used to manage certain
bacterial pathogens.
However:
Not every bacterial disease can be controlled by seed treatment. 
Effectiveness depends strongly on whether the pathogen is:
- On the seed surface
- Inside the seed
- Associated with planting material
- Primarily soil-borne
- Able to survive the treatment

11. Biological Seed Treatment
Beneficial microorganisms can be applied to seed.
Common biological agents include:
- Trichoderma
- Bacillus
- Pseudomonas
- Other approved beneficial microorganisms
The concept is:

Treated seed→Beneficial microorganism→Root/seed colonization→Pathogen suppressionTreated\
seed  Beneficial\ microorganism  Root/seed\ colonization  Pathogen\
suppression

12. Trichoderma Seed Treatment
Trichoderma can colonize the seed/rhizosphere and suppress certain pathogens through:
- Competition
- Mycoparasitism
- Antibiosis
- Enzyme production
- Plant-defense stimulation
Conceptually:
Trichoderma→Root−zone establishment→Pathogen suppressionTrichoderma  Root-zone\
establishment  Pathogen\ suppression

13. Bacillus and Pseudomonas Seed Treatment
Some strains of Bacillus and Pseudomonas can be used as biological seed treatments.
Potential mechanisms include:
Competition+Antibiosis+Siderophores+Plant defenseCompetition + Antibiosis + Siderophores + Plant\
defense
leading to:
Disease pressure↓Disease\ pressure 14. Physical Seed Treatment
Physical methods can be used for certain seed-borne pathogens.
The classic example is:

Hot-water treatment
Controlled heat→Pathogen inactivationControlled\ heat  Pathogen\ inactivation
The treatment must use the correct temperature and duration for the specific crop and pathogen.
Too little treatment:

Pathogen survivesPathogen\ survives
Too much treatment:
Seed injury→Germination↓Seed\ injury  Germination Therefore:
Temperature×Time 
must be carefully controlled.

15. Seed Treatment and Germination
A properly selected seed treatment can protect the germinating seed.
Treated seed→Pathogen pressure↓→Germination/establishment protectedTreated\ seed  Pathogen\ pressure  Germination/establishment\ protected
However:
Seed treatment does not automatically increase germination of poor−quality seed. 
Seed quality remains fundamental.

16. Seed Treatment and Seedling Diseases
The most vulnerable stage is often the period around germination and early seedling development.
Seed→Germination→Radicle/plumule→SeedlingSeed  Germination  Radicle/plumule  Seedling
Pathogen attack during this period can cause:
- Seed rot
- Pre-emergence damping-off
- Post-emergence damping-off
- Root infection
- Seedling death
Seed treatment provides a protective barrier during this critical stage.

## 17. Damping-Off
Damping-off is a disease syndrome involving death of seedlings before or after emergence, commonly
associated with soil- and seed-associated pathogens.
Simplified:
Seed→Germination→Pathogen infection→Seedling deathSeed  Germination  Pathogen\ infection  Seedling\ death
Seed treatment can reduce risk for susceptible seed/seedling diseases when the causal pathogen is
controlled by the selected treatment.

18. Pre-Emergence Protection
If the pathogen kills the seedling before it emerges:
Seed→Germination→Infection→DeathSeed  Germination  Infection  Death
This is called pre-emergence damping-off in relevant disease systems.
Seed treatment can help interrupt:
Pathogen→Germinating seedPathogen Germinating\ seed

19. Post-Emergence Protection
If the seedling emerges but is subsequently attacked:
Seedling→Pathogen infection→Stem/root damage→Seedling deathSeedling  Pathogen\
infection  Stem/root\ damage  Seedling\ death
Appropriate seed treatment can provide early protection depending on the pathogen and product.

20. Seed Treatment and Disease Cycle
Recall:
Survival→Inoculum→Dissemination→Inoculation→Infection→DiseaseSurvival  Inoculum
 Dissemination  Inoculation  Infection  Disease
Seed treatment can interrupt the cycle at the seed/initial infection stage.
Seed treatment→Initial inoculation/infection↓ 

21. Seed Treatment and Disease Spread
The relationship is:
Treated seed→Initial infection↓→Diseased seedlings↓→Secondary inoculum↓→Disease spread↓Treate
d\ seed  Initial\ infection  Diseased\ seedlings  Secondary\ inoculum  Disease\ spread Therefore:
Seed treatment→Early disease suppression→Potentially lower later disease pressure 
This is especially relevant to pathogens that produce secondary inoculum.

22. Seed Treatment and Sanitation
These two practices complement each other.

Sanitation
Remove pathogen sourcesRemove\ pathogen\ sources

Seed treatment
Protect seed/seedlingProtect\ seed/seedling
Together:
Healthy seed+Clean field→Lower initial disease pressure 

23. Seed Treatment and Resistant Varieties
These are different but complementary.

Resistant variety
Host genetics→Disease development↓Host\ genetics  Disease\ development Seed treatment
Seed protection→Early infection↓Seed\ protection  Early\ infection Together:
Resistant variety+Treated healthy seed→Multiple layers of protection 

24. Seed Treatment and Biological Control
A biological seed treatment can establish beneficial microorganisms around the seed and roots.
BCA→Seed→Rhizosphere→Colonization→Pathogen suppressionBCA  Seed  Rhizosphere  Colonization  Pathogen\ suppression
This can combine:
Competition+Antibiosis+Defense inductionCompetition+Antibiosis+Defense\ induction

25. Seed Treatment and Biofertilizers
A seed can receive both:
- Disease-control biological agent
- Nutrient-related inoculant
But compatibility is critical.
For example:
Seed→Biofertilizer+Biocontrol organismSeed  Biofertilizer + Biocontrol\ organism
may work only if both organisms remain viable and compatible.
Chemical seed treatments may also affect beneficial inoculants.
Therefore:
Check compatibility before combining seed treatments. 

26. Seed Treatment Methods
Common practical methods include:

Seed dressing
A small quantity of treatment is uniformly applied to the seed surface.

Seed coating
The treatment is incorporated into a coating around the seed.

Seed pelleting
Seed is enclosed in a coating that may contain:

- Protectants
- Biological agents
- Nutrients
- Other approved materials

Seed soaking
Seeds are exposed to a treatment solution.

Hot-water treatment
Controlled temperature/time treatment.

Biological inoculation
Beneficial microorganisms are applied to seed.

27. Seed Dressing
Seed dressing is one of the simplest methods.
Seed+Treatment→Uniform coatingSeed+Treatment  Uniform\ coating
Important factors:
- Uniformity
- Correct dosage
- Seed viability
- Proper mixing
- Drying where required
- Worker safety

28. Seed Coating
Seed coating places a material around the seed.
Potential functions:
- Pathogen protection
- Nutrient delivery
- Biological inoculation
- Improved handling

- Improved planting precision
Conceptually:
Seed→Protective coating→Controlled protectionSeed  Protective\ coating  Controlled\ protection

29. Seed Pelleting
Seed pelleting increases seed size and creates a more uniform shape.
It can be useful for precision sowing of small seeds.
Pellets may contain:
- Inert materials
- Nutrients
- Biological agents
- Protectants
But the pellet formulation must not reduce germination.

30. Seed Treatment Rate
A generic relationship is:
Treatment required=Recommended dose×Seed quantity 
The actual unit may be:
- g/kg seed
- mL/kg seed
- g/L
- product/acre
- product/hectare
Always use the registered product label, because formulations and concentrations differ.

31. Example Calculation
Suppose a product is recommended at:

2 g/kg seed2\ g/kg\ seed
and the farmer has:
100 kg seed100\ kg\ seed
Then:
Treatment=2×100Treatment=2 100 Treatment=200 g 
This is only a mathematical illustration; actual treatment rates must come from the relevant product
label.

32. Seed Treatment and Seed Rate
Seed treatment does not change the basic seed-rate calculation.
Seed rate depends on factors such as:
Seed rate=f(Target population,Germination,Purity,Establishment,Seed size)Seed\ rate = f(Target\
population,Germination,Purity,Establishment,Seed\ size)
Treatment is an additional protection step.

33. Seed Quality Before Treatment
Good seed treatment starts with good seed.
Important quality parameters include:
- Genetic purity
- Physical purity
- Germination
- Vigor
- Moisture
- Seed health
- Absence of serious contamination
Recall:
Seed Quality=Genetic+Physical+Physiological+Health 

34. Seed Treatment and Seed Vigor
Healthy, vigorous seeds generally establish faster.
High vigor→Rapid emergence→Shorter vulnerable periodHigh\ vigor  Rapid\ emergence
 Shorter\ vulnerable\ period
Seed treatment can provide additional protection, but it cannot compensate indefinitely for poor seed
quality.

35. Seed Treatment and Storage
After treatment, storage requirements depend on the type of treatment.
Important considerations:
- Moisture
- Temperature
- Storage duration
- Product stability
- Seed viability
- Packaging
Some treatments may reduce storage life if improperly used.
Therefore:
Treatment compatibility+Seed storage+Product storage 
must all be considered.

36. Seed Treatment and Insect Pests
Some seed treatments target early-season insects in addition to diseases.
For example, an insecticidal seed treatment may protect emerging seedlings from certain pests.
But:
Fungicidal seed treatment≠Insecticidal seed treatmentFungicidal\ seed\ treatment Insecticidal\
seed\ treatment
The active ingredient must match the target organism.

37. Seed Treatment and Viruses
Seed treatment generally cannot be assumed to eliminate viruses.
Some viruses can be seed-transmitted, but management depends on:
- Virus
- Crop
- Seed transmission mechanism
- Seed health
- Vector management
- Planting material
Therefore:
Seed treatment is not a universal solution for seed−transmitted viruses. 

38. Seed Treatment and Nematodes
Some seed treatments may provide limited protection against certain nematodes, depending on the
product and crop.
However, many nematode problems require broader management:
Crop rotation+Resistant varieties+Healthy planting material+Soil management+Approved nematicidal
approachesCrop\ rotation + Resistant\ varieties + Healthy\ planting\ material + Soil\ management +
Approved\ nematicidal\ approaches
Do not assume a standard fungicidal seed treatment controls nematodes.

39. Seed Treatment and Soil Conditions
Effectiveness can depend on:
- Soil moisture
- Soil temperature
- Soil texture
- Organic matter
- Pathogen density
- Planting depth

- Seed vigor
For example:
Severe pathogen pressure+Poor soil conditions→Seedling disease risk↑Severe\ pathogen\ pressure +
Poor\ soil\ conditions  Seedling\ disease\ risk Seed treatment is only one protective layer.

40. Importance of Uniform Treatment
Uneven treatment can create:
Untreated seed→Disease riskUntreated\ seed  Disease\ risk
while:
Overtreated seed→Potential phytotoxicityOvertreated\ seed  Potential\ phytotoxicity
Therefore:
Uniformity+Correct dose 
are essential.

41. Seed Treatment and Phytotoxicity
An inappropriate treatment can damage seed.
Possible effects:
- Reduced germination
- Delayed emergence
- Abnormal seedlings
- Root injury
Therefore:
Wrong treatment/rate→Seed injuryWrong\ treatment/rate  Seed\ injury
Always follow the approved use instructions.

42. Chemical Compatibility
If multiple products are used on the same seed:
Treatment1+Treatment2+Treatment3Treatment 1+Treatment 2+Treatment 3

they must be compatible.
Problems may include:
- Reduced microbial viability
- Reduced chemical stability
- Phytotoxicity
- Poor coating
- Uneven distribution
Therefore:
Compatibility must be verified, not assumed. 

43. Seed Treatment and Fungicide Resistance
Repeated use of the same fungicide mode of action can contribute to resistance selection.
Repeated seed treatment→Selection pressure→Less sensitive pathogen populationRepeated\ seed\
treatment  Selection\ pressure  Less\ sensitive\ pathogen\ population
Therefore resistance-management principles can also apply to seed treatments.

44. Seed Treatment and Integrated Disease
Management
A complete system is:
Healthy seed+Seed treatment+Resistant variety+Sanitation+Crop rotation+Biological control+Proper ir
rigation/drainage+Monitoring 
Chemical treatment is one component, not the whole system.

45. Advantages of Seed Treatment
1. Protects the crop at the earliest stage
Early protectionEarly\ protection

2. Requires relatively small quantities of product
Compared with treating the entire crop canopy.

3. Targets the seed/seedling zone
Seed→Root zoneSeed Root\ zone

4. Can reduce early disease incidence
5. Can improve stand establishment
6. Can be combined with biological agents
Where compatible.

7. Can reduce early pathogen spread
8. Can protect against multiple early-season threats
When a properly designed and approved combination is used.

46. Limitations of Seed Treatment
Seed treatment does not solve every disease problem.
Limitations include:
- Limited protection duration
- Pathogen may be outside treatment zone
- Internal infections may be difficult to eliminate
- High pathogen pressure can overwhelm protection
- Poor application can reduce effectiveness
- Some treatments can reduce seed viability
- Resistance may develop
- Biological treatments can be environment-sensitive
- Not effective against all pathogens
Therefore:
Seed treatment≠Complete disease control 

47. Seed Treatment vs Foliar Spray
Seed treatment
Foliar spray
Applied before sowing
Applied after crop emergence
Protects seed/young seedling Protects above-ground plant tissues
Targets early disease
Often targets established canopy disease
Small treatment volume
Larger field application
Preventive role is important Preventive/early curative depending on product
Applied directly to seed
Applied to leaves/stems

48. Seed Treatment vs Soil Treatment
Seed treatment

Soil treatment
Treats seed
Treats soil/root zone
Lower quantity of material may be needed Larger soil volume may be treated
Targets seed/early seedling
Targets soil pathogen population
Convenient for sowing operations
Can require additional application equipment
Limited spatial coverage
Larger soil-zone coverage

49. Seed Treatment vs Seed Priming
These are different.

Seed treatment
Primarily protects seed/seedling or delivers a specific treatment.

Seed priming
A controlled hydration process intended to initiate physiological processes before sowing and improve
emergence.
Priming≠Seed treatmentPriming Seed\ treatment
Although the two may sometimes be combined.

50. Seed Treatment vs Seed Inoculation
Seed inoculation
Usually refers to applying beneficial microorganisms such as:
- Rhizobia

- Mycorrhizal organisms
- Other microbial inoculants

Seed treatment
Broader term covering:
- Chemical
- Biological
- Physical
- Other appropriate treatments
Thus:
Seed inoculation⊆Broad seed−treatment practicesSeed\ inoculation  Broad\ seed-treatment\
practices
depending on terminology.

51. Seed Treatment Workflow
A practical workflow is:
1. Identify target problem 
↓
2. Select healthy, high−quality seed 
↓
3. Identify appropriate registered treatment 
↓
4. Check compatibility 
↓
5. Calculate required treatment 
↓
6. Treat uniformly 
↓
7. Dry/store according to instructions 
↓

8. Sow properly 
↓
9. Monitor emergence and disease 

52. Seed Treatment Decision Framework
Before treatment, ask:

Question 1
What pathogen am I targeting?

Question 2
Is the pathogen seed-borne, soil-borne or both?

Question 3
Is the selected treatment effective against this pathogen?

Question 4
Is the treatment registered/approved for this crop and use?

Question 5
Is the seed compatible with the treatment?

Question 6
Are multiple treatments compatible?

Question 7
What is the correct rate and method?

Question 8
What safety measures are required?

Question 9
How should treated seed be stored and handled?

53. Safety of Treated Seed
Treated seed should be clearly identified and handled as a treated agricultural input.
Important practices:

- Follow product label.
- Use appropriate PPE.
- Avoid eating/drinking/smoking while treating seed.
- Avoid generating dust.
- Prevent treated seed from entering food/feed channels.
- Store separately and securely.
- Keep away from children and animals.
- Use appropriate containers/labels.
- Follow disposal regulations.

54. Treated Seed Must Not Be Used as Food or
Feed
This is an extremely important safety principle.
Treated seed→Planting only 
It must not be diverted into:
- Human food
- Livestock feed
- Household use
when treated with products that make it unsuitable for consumption.

55. Seed Treatment and Storage Labels
After treatment, maintain clear identification of:
- Crop
- Variety
- Treatment used
- Date
- Batch/lot information where applicable
- Intended planting use

This reduces accidental misuse.

56. Seed Treatment and Disease Forecasting
Seed treatment is primarily a preventive practice.
If disease risk is predictable:
Risk assessment→Seed treatment→Early protectionRisk\ assessment  Seed\ treatment
 Early\ protection
Later crop management may still require monitoring and additional interventions.

57. Common Mistakes
Mistake 1: Treating all seed with the same product
Incorrect.
Different pathogens require different treatments.

Mistake 2: Using a fungicide for every seed problem
Incorrect.
The problem may be bacterial, viral, nematode-related, insect-related or abiotic.

Mistake 3: Overdosing
Excess treatment→PhytotoxicityExcess\ treatment  Phytotoxicity

Mistake 4: Uneven coating
Creates untreated seed.

Mistake 5: Mixing incompatible products
Can reduce effectiveness or harm seed.

Mistake 6: Using poor-quality seed
Treatment cannot compensate for every problem associated with poor seed.

Mistake 7: Ignoring storage
Improper storage can reduce seed viability and biological product effectiveness.

Mistake 8: Assuming treated seed means disease-free crop
Incorrect.

Treated seed≠Disease-free crop free\ crop  

58. Important Concept — Seed Treatment Is
Preventive
The strongest conceptual understanding is:
Seed treatment acts before or around the earliest infection stage. 
It is therefore fundamentally different from trying to rescue a crop after severe disease development.
Prevention>Early intervention>Late interventionPrevention > Early\ intervention > Late\ intervention
for many seedling disease situations.

59. Seed Treatment in Integrated Disease
Management
The complete approach is:
Healthy seed→Seed treatment→Resistant variety→Sanitation→Crop rotation→Biological control→M
- nitoring→Additional disease control if needed 
This creates multiple barriers against disease.

60. Complete Conceptual Flow
INFECTED SEED→PATHOGEN→GERMINATION→SEEDLING INFECTION→DISEASE 
Seed treatment attempts to interrupt it:
## Treated Healthy Seed→Pathogen Suppression→Healthy Germination→Heal
THY SEEDLING→LOWER EARLY DISEASE 

61. Exam-Ready Answer
Seed treatment is a pre-sowing disease-management practice in which seeds are
treated with suitable chemical, biological or physical agents to control seed-borne and
soil-borne pathogens and protect germinating seeds and young seedlings. Seed
treatment may involve fungicides, appropriate bactericidal products, biological agents
such as Trichoderma, Bacillus and Pseudomonas, physical treatments such as
properly controlled hot-water treatment, or suitable combinations of treatments. It
can reduce seed rot, damping-off and other early-season diseases and can improve
crop establishment when the seed and treatment are appropriate. Effective seed
treatment requires correct identification of the target pathogen, selection of a suitable
registered treatment, correct dosage, uniform application, compatibility among
products, proper handling and storage, and monitoring after sowing. Seed treatment
is a preventive component of integrated disease management and should be combined
with healthy seed, resistant varieties, sanitation, crop rotation, biological control and
- ther appropriate practices.

62. Final Memory
One-line definition:
SEED TREATMENT = PROTECT THE SEED AND EARLY SEEDLING FROM PATHOGENS BEF
ORE DISEASE BECOMES ESTABLISHED   
Main pathway:
TREATED SEED→PATHOGEN↓→SEED/SEEDLING INFECTION↓→ESTABLISHMENT↑ 

Major types:
Chemical+Biological+Physical+Other appropriate treatments 

Major biological agents:
Trichoderma+Bacillus+Pseudomonas 

Major disease-management principle:
PREVENT EARLY → PROTECT SEED → PROTECT SEEDLING → REDUCE INITIAL INFECTI
ON   
And the most important distinction:
Seed treatment is an early preventive barrier, not a guarantee of complete disease control.
   

## Module 16 — Plant Disease
## Management
## 20. Integrated Disease Management
(IDM)
## 1. Introduction
Integrated Disease Management (IDM) is a comprehensive, preventive and decision-based approach
in which multiple compatible methods are combined to prevent, suppress, or manage plant
diseases economically and sustainably.
Instead of depending entirely on fungicides or bactericides, IDM combines methods such as:
- Resistant varieties
- Healthy seed and planting material
- Seed treatment
- Sanitation
- Cultural practices
- Crop rotation
- Biological control
- Physical/mechanical methods
- Chemical control
- Vector management
- Quarantine/exclusion
- Disease monitoring and forecasting

The central idea is:
Prevention + Monitoring + Multiple Compatible Methods→Disease Suppression    

## 2. Definition
Simple definition
Integrated Disease Management is the coordinated use of multiple compatible diseasemanagement methods to keep plant diseases below economically damaging levels
while minimizing unnecessary environmental, health and economic costs.

Exam-ready definition
Integrated Disease Management (IDM) is an ecosystem-based and decision-oriented
approach that integrates compatible cultural, biological, genetic, physical, chemical,
regulatory and other disease-management practices to prevent or suppress plant
diseases economically while minimizing risks to humans, beneficial organisms and the
environment.

3. Basic Principle of IDM
The fundamental principle is:
Do not depend on one method 
Instead:
Multiple compatible methods→Multiple points of disease suppression 
For example:
Resistant variety+Healthy seed+Seed treatment+Sanitation+Crop rotation+Biological control+Chemica
l protectionResistant\ variety + Healthy\ seed + Seed\ treatment + Sanitation + Crop\ rotation +
Biological\ control + Chemical\ protection
can provide greater overall disease management than repeatedly using one fungicide alone.

4. Why Integrated Disease Management Is
Needed
Plant diseases can be difficult to control because pathogens have:

- Different survival mechanisms
- Different infection routes
- Different dispersal mechanisms
- Different host ranges
- Different environmental requirements
- Different reproductive cycles
For example:
Fungal pathogen≠Bacterial pathogen≠Virus≠NematodeFungal\ pathogen  Bacterial\ pathogen  Virus  Nematode
Therefore, a single management method cannot effectively control all diseases.

5. IDM and the Disease Triangle
Disease development depends on:
Host+Pathogen+Environment→Disease 
IDM attempts to disturb one or more components.

Host management
Resistant variety→Host susceptibility↓Resistant\ variety  Host\ susceptibility Pathogen management
Sanitation+Seed treatment→Inoculum↓Sanitation+Seed\ treatment  Inoculum Environment management
Drainage+Spacing+Irrigation management→Favorable conditions↓Drainage+Spacing+Irrigation\
management  Favorable\ conditions Thus:
IDM=Attack the disease system from multiple directions 

6. Disease Tetrahedron
Disease development can also be represented as:
Host+Pathogen+Environment+Time→Disease 

Time is important because disease may increase through repeated infection cycles.
Therefore IDM also focuses on timing.

7. Main Objectives of IDM
The major objectives are:
1. Prevent disease establishment.
2. Reduce initial inoculum.
3. Reduce secondary spread.
4. Protect crop yield and quality.
5. Maintain healthy plants.
6. Reduce unnecessary pesticide use.
7. Delay resistance development.
8. Protect beneficial organisms.
9. Reduce production costs where possible.
10.Reduce environmental contamination.
11.Maintain long-term disease-management effectiveness.
12.Produce safe agricultural products.

8. Core IDM Strategy
A useful framework is:
PREVENT→IDENTIFY→MONITOR→ASSESS→DECIDE→MANAGE→RE−MONITOR 
Each step is important.

9. Step 1 — Prevention
The best disease is often the disease that never becomes established.
Prevention includes:
- Healthy seed

- Certified planting material
- Resistant varieties
- Crop rotation
- Sanitation
- Proper drainage
- Appropriate irrigation
- Proper spacing
- Weed/alternate-host management
- Quarantine
- Clean equipment
- Appropriate planting time
Conceptually:
Prevention→Initial inoculum↓→Disease risk↓ 

10. Step 2 — Correct Disease Identification
Before management:
Identify the disease 
A practical diagnostic sequence is:
Crop→Symptoms→Signs→Plant part→Pattern→Environment→History→ConfirmationCrop
 Symptoms  Signs  Plant\ part  Pattern  Environment  History  Confirmation
Wrong diagnosis can lead to:
Wrong pathogen→Wrong treatment→Disease continuesWrong\ pathogen  Wrong\
treatment  Disease\ continues

11. Step 3 — Disease Monitoring
Monitoring means regularly observing:
- Disease incidence
- Disease severity
- New symptoms

- Pathogen signs
- Disease distribution
- Crop growth stage
- Weather
- Soil conditions
- Vector populations
- Natural enemies
- Disease progression

Incidence
Disease incidence(%)=Diseased plantsTotal plants×100  Total\ plants  100  

Severity
Disease severity(%)=Diseased tissue areaTotal tissue area×100  Total\ tissue\ area  100  
Memory:
Incidence=How many?  Severity=How much?
 

12. Step 4 — Disease Risk Assessment
Not every detected pathogen will produce serious disease.
Risk depends on:
Risk=f(Host+Pathogen+Environment+Time) 
Important factors:
- Host susceptibility
- Pathogen inoculum
- Weather
- Temperature
- Humidity
- Leaf wetness
- Rainfall

- Soil moisture
- Crop stage
- Disease history
- Vector population

13. Step 5 — Decision Making
After monitoring:
Disease level+Disease risk+Crop stage+Weather+Economics→Management decisionDisease\ level +
Disease\ risk + Crop\ stage + Weather + Economics  Management\ decision
Possible decisions include:
- No action
- Continue monitoring
- Improve sanitation
- Modify irrigation
- Remove infected plants
- Apply biological control
- Apply an approved chemical treatment
- Combine multiple measures
Important:
Disease detected≠Automatic pesticide application 

14. Step 6 — Integrated Management
After deciding that action is needed, select the most appropriate combination of compatible methods.
Cultural+Genetic+Biological+Physical+Chemical+Regulatory 
Not every component is required in every crop.

15. Major Components of IDM
1. Genetic control
Use resistant or tolerant varieties.
Resistant host→Infection/colonization/disease development↓Resistant\ host  Infection/colonization/disease\ development Advantages:
- Often economical
- Can provide season-long protection
- Reduces dependence on pesticides
Limitations:
- Resistance may be incomplete
- Pathogen populations can sometimes overcome resistance
- Resistance may be disease- or strain-specific

16. Healthy Seed and Planting Material
Use:
- Disease-free seed
- Certified seed
- Healthy seedlings
- Disease-free cuttings
- Healthy tubers
- Healthy bulbs
- Healthy grafting material
Basic pathway:
Healthy planting material→Initial inoculum↓→Disease risk↓Healthy\ planting\ material  Initial\ inoculum  Disease\ risk 17. Seed Treatment
Seed treatment protects the crop at the earliest stage.

Seed treatment→Seed/seedling pathogen suppressionSeed\ treatment  Seed/seedling\
pathogen\ suppression
It can involve:
- Fungicides
- Appropriate bactericidal products
- Biological agents
- Physical treatment
- Other registered treatments
Seed treatment is particularly useful for certain seed-borne and early soil-borne diseases.

## 18. Sanitation
Sanitation means removing or preventing pathogen sources and contaminated material.
Methods include:
- Removing diseased plants where appropriate
- Removing infected crop residues
- Cleaning tools
- Cleaning machinery
- Removing volunteer plants
- Managing weeds/alternate hosts
- Using clean planting material
- Disposing of infected material appropriately
Core relationship:
Sanitation→Inoculum source↓→Disease spread↓ 

19. Cultural Control
Cultural practices modify the crop environment or production system to make disease development less
favorable.
Examples:
- Crop rotation

- Proper planting date
- Proper spacing
- Proper plant density
- Balanced nutrition
- Irrigation management
- Drainage
- Canopy management
- Weed management
- Residue management
- Appropriate planting depth
Principle:
Cultural management→Host suitability↓+Pathogen survival/infection↓ 

20. Crop Rotation
Crop rotation involves growing different crops sequentially on the same land.
For host-specific pathogens:
Host crop→Non−host crop→Host cropHost\ crop  Non-host\ crop  Host\ crop
This can reduce pathogen populations that depend on the host crop.
Conceptually:
Non−host period→Pathogen reproduction↓→Inoculum↓Non-host\ period  Pathogen\
reproduction  Inoculum However, rotation is not equally effective against pathogens with:
- Very long survival
- Broad host range
- Soil persistence
- Alternative hosts

21. Water and Drainage Management
Excess moisture can favor some diseases.

Therefore:
Waterlogging→Root stress+Potential pathogen favorabilityWaterlogging  Root\ stress +
Potential\ pathogen\ favorability
Proper drainage can reduce risk in suitable disease systems.
Similarly, avoiding unnecessary leaf wetness can reduce some foliar disease risks.

22. Plant Spacing
Dense planting can create:
- High humidity
- Poor air circulation
- Longer leaf-wetness periods
Conceptually:
High density→Microclimate humidity↑→Disease risk may↑High\ density  Microclimate\
humidity  Disease\ risk\ may Appropriate spacing can improve:
Air movement→Canopy drying→Disease risk↓Air\ movement  Canopy\ drying  Disease\ risk The exact relationship is disease-specific.

23. Biological Control
Biological control uses beneficial organisms or their products to suppress pathogens.
Examples include:
- Trichoderma
- Bacillus
- Pseudomonas
Mechanisms can include:
Competition+Antibiosis+Mycoparasitism+Induced plant defensesCompetition + Antibiosis +
Mycoparasitism + Induced\ plant\ defenses
Therefore:

Biological agent→Pathogen suppression→Disease pressure↓Biological\ agent  Pathogen\
suppression  Disease\ pressure 24. Physical and Mechanical Control
Examples include:
- Removal of infected plant parts
- Rogueing infected plants
- Pruning
- Heat treatment
- Hot-water treatment for appropriate seed systems
- Barriers/exclusion
- Soil solarization where appropriate
- Cleaning equipment
Conceptually:
Physical intervention→Inoculum/pathogen reductionPhysical\ intervention  Inoculum/pathogen\ reduction

25. Chemical Control
Chemical control includes appropriate:
- Fungicides
- Bactericides
- Other registered disease-management products
The process should be:
Correct diagnosis→Correct product→Correct timing→Correct applicationCorrect\ diagnosis
 Correct\ product  Correct\ timing  Correct\ application
Chemical control should be integrated rather than used automatically.

26. Fungicides in IDM
Fungicides can be used against appropriate fungal/fungus-like pathogens.
They may be:

- Contact
- Systemic/local systemic
- Preventive
- Post-infection active, depending on product
Their effectiveness depends on:
- Pathogen
- Disease stage
- Active ingredient
- Application timing
- Coverage
- Weather
- Resistance status

27. Bactericides in IDM
Bactericides can be used against suitable bacterial diseases.
They are often particularly valuable as preventive or early interventions because established bacterial
infections can be difficult to eliminate.
Prevention+Sanitation+Early protectionPrevention + Sanitation + Early\ protection
is often more useful than attempting to cure severely infected tissue.

28. Chemical Resistance Management
Repeated use of the same fungicide mode of action can select resistant pathogen populations.
Repeated MOA→Selection pressure→Less sensitive pathogens→ResistanceRepeated\ MOA
 Selection\ pressure  Less\ sensitive\ pathogens  Resistance
Therefore:
Rotate effective MOAs 
and combine chemical control with nonchemical methods.

29. Quarantine and Exclusion
Exclusion attempts to prevent a pathogen from entering a field, farm, region or country.
Methods include:
- Certified planting material
- Inspection
- Quarantine
- Sanitary equipment
- Restriction of contaminated material
- Border/plant-health regulations
Basic principle:
Prevent entry→Prevent establishment 

30. Vector Management
Some pathogens are transmitted by insects.
For example:
Infected plant→Vector→Healthy plantInfected\ plant  Vector  Healthy\ plant
Vectors may include:
- Aphids
- Whiteflies
- Leafhoppers
- Planthoppers
- Thrips
- Beetles
Vector management can therefore be part of IDM for vector-transmitted diseases.
But:
Vector control≠Direct cure of an already infected plant 
Its major role is reducing further transmission.

31. Weed and Alternate-Host Management
Weeds can sometimes serve as:
- Pathogen reservoirs
- Alternate hosts
- Vector habitats
Therefore:
Weed/alternate host→Pathogen survival→Crop infectionWeed/alternate\ host  Pathogen\
survival  Crop\ infection
Removing or managing important alternate hosts can interrupt the disease cycle.

32. Disease Cycle and IDM
IDM becomes easier to understand by looking at the disease cycle:
Survival→Dissemination→Inoculation→Penetration→Infection→Colonization→Reproduction→Spre
ad 
Different IDM practices target different stages.
Disease-cycle stage
Possible IDM intervention
Survival
Rotation, sanitation, residue management
Inoculum production Sanitation, resistant varieties
Dissemination
Vector control, sanitation, barriers
Inoculation
Protective treatments
Penetration
Protective chemicals, resistant host
Infection
Host resistance, biological/chemical protection
Colonization
Genetic/chemical/biological management
Reproduction
Chemical/biological control
Secondary spread
Monitoring, sanitation, vector management

33. Monocyclic Diseases and IDM
For a largely monocyclic disease:
Initial inoculum→Main infection→DiseaseInitial\ inoculum  Main\ infection  Disease
Management focuses strongly on reducing:

Initial inoculum 
Methods may include:
- Healthy seed
- Seed treatment
- Resistant varieties
- Crop rotation
- Sanitation
- Removal of infected residues

34. Polycyclic Diseases and IDM
For polycyclic diseases:
I0→I1→I2→I3→...I 0  I 1  I 2  I 3  ...
where each cycle generates new inoculum.
Therefore IDM must reduce both:
Primary inoculumPrimary\ inoculum
and:
Secondary spreadSecondary\ spread
Monitoring and timely intervention become particularly important.

35. IDM and Weather
Weather strongly influences many diseases.
Important factors:
- Temperature
- Relative humidity
- Rainfall
- Leaf wetness
- Wind
- Soil moisture
Conceptually:

Weather+Host susceptibility+Pathogen inoculum→Disease riskWeather + Host\ susceptibility +
Pathogen\ inoculum  Disease\ risk
Disease forecasting can therefore help determine when intervention is justified.

36. Disease Forecasting
Disease forecasting uses environmental and biological information to estimate disease risk.
Weather+Crop stage+Pathogen biology+Disease history→Risk forecastWeather + Crop\ stage +
Pathogen\ biology + Disease\ history  Risk\ forecast
This can help:
- Avoid unnecessary sprays
- Improve timing
- Detect high-risk periods
- Improve economic efficiency

37. IDM and Economic Considerations
Disease management must be economically meaningful.
A treatment may be technically effective but economically unjustified if:
Cost of management>Value of prevented lossCost\ of\ management > Value\ of\ prevented\ loss
Therefore:
Technical effectiveness+Economic justification 
are both important.

38. IDM vs Calendar-Based Spraying
Calendar spraying
Every X days→SprayEvery\ X\ days  Spray
regardless of actual disease risk.

IDM
Monitor→Assess risk→Decide→Treat if justifiedMonitor  Assess\ risk  Decide
 Treat\ if\ justified

Therefore:
Calendar spraying≠IDM 

39. IDM and Pesticide Resistance
IDM helps reduce resistance pressure because it avoids relying exclusively on chemical control.
Nonchemical methods→Chemical dependence↓Nonchemical\ methods  Chemical\
dependence and:
MOA diversification→Resistance selection pressure↓MOA\ diversification  Resistance\
selection\ pressure This principle is particularly important for fungicides.

40. IDM and Beneficial Organisms
Unnecessary pesticide use can harm:
- Beneficial microorganisms
- Biological-control organisms
- Pollinators
- Natural enemies
Therefore IDM aims for:
Disease suppression+Minimum unnecessary harm to beneficial organisms 

41. IDM and Sustainability
A sustainable disease-management program attempts to balance:
Crop productivity+Economic viability+Environmental protection+Long-term disease management ed  Crop\ productivity + Economic\ viability + Environmental\ protection + Long term\ disease\
management  

42. Example — Integrated Management of a
Fungal Foliar Disease
Suppose a crop is susceptible to a foliar fungal disease.
A possible IDM program could involve:

Before planting
Resistant variety+Healthy seed+Crop rotationResistant\ variety + Healthy\ seed + Crop\ rotation

At planting
Seed treatmentSeed\ treatment

During crop growth
Proper spacing+Balanced nutrition+Appropriate irrigationProper\ spacing + Balanced\ nutrition +
Appropriate\ irrigation

Monitoring
Field scouting+Weather monitoring+Disease incidence/severityField\ scouting + Weather\ monitoring
+ Disease\ incidence/severity

If risk becomes significant
Biological controlBiological\ control
and/or an appropriate registered fungicide.

After harvest
Sanitation+Residue managementSanitation + Residue\ management
Thus:
Multiple interventions→Multiple disease−cycle points disrupted 

43. Example — Integrated Management of a
Bacterial Disease
A possible strategy may include:
Healthy planting materialHealthy\ planting\ material
•

Resistant/tolerant varietyResistant/tolerant\ variety
•
SanitationSanitation
•
Avoiding unnecessary overhead irrigationAvoiding\ unnecessary\ overhead\ irrigation
where appropriate
•
Vector/alternate−host managementVector/alternate-host\ management
•
Biological methodsBiological\ methods
•
Appropriate registered bactericidal protectionAppropriate\ registered\ bactericidal\ protection
when justified.

44. Example — Integrated Management of a
Virus
For a viral disease:
Virus→Vector→Healthy plantVirus Vector Healthy\ plant
An IDM strategy may emphasize:
- Virus-free planting material
- Resistant varieties
- Vector management
- Rogueing/removal where appropriate
- Weed/alternate-host management
- Sanitation
- Quarantine
- Monitoring
A fungicide will generally not cure the virus.
Therefore:

Correct diagnosis→Correct management 

45. Example — Integrated Management of a
Soil-Borne Disease
Possible measures:
Healthy seed+Resistant variety+Seed treatment+Crop rotation+Drainage+Soil health management+Bio
logical controlHealthy\ seed + Resistant\ variety + Seed\ treatment + Crop\ rotation + Drainage + Soil\
health\ management + Biological\ control
Depending on pathogen:
Soil pathogen population↓→Root infection↓Soil\ pathogen\ population  Root\
infection 46. Integrated Disease Management Pyramid
A useful conceptual hierarchy is:
Prevention 
↓
Cultural+Genetic 
↓
Physical+Biological 
↓
Chemical protection when justified 
↓
Monitoring+Evaluation 
This is a conceptual framework, not a rigid rule that every crop must follow exactly.

47. Advantages of IDM
1. Reduces dependence on pesticides
2. Can delay resistance
3. Protects beneficial organisms
4. Can reduce disease losses
5. Can improve long-term sustainability
6. Uses prevention rather than relying only on cure
7. Can improve economic efficiency
8. Addresses different stages of the disease cycle
9. Can reduce environmental risks
10. Encourages better crop monitoring

48. Limitations of IDM
IDM is not effortless.
Challenges include:
- Requires accurate diagnosis
- Requires regular monitoring
- Requires knowledge of pathogen biology
- Thresholds/risk levels may vary
- Biological control can be environment-dependent
- Resistant varieties may not be available
- Some diseases develop rapidly
- Weather can change disease risk quickly
- Multiple practices require coordination
- Farmers may need additional technical knowledge
Therefore:
IDM=More informed management, not simply more management 

49. Common Mistakes in IDM
Mistake 1 — Thinking IDM means zero pesticides
Incorrect.
IDM can include pesticides when justified. 

Mistake 2 — Thinking IDM means using every method
Incorrect.
IDM≠Use everythingIDM Use\ everything
Instead:
Use compatible and appropriate methods. 

Mistake 3 — Spraying without diagnosis
Incorrect.

Mistake 4 — Ignoring sanitation
Incorrect.

Mistake 5 — Ignoring resistant varieties
Incorrect.

Mistake 6 — Repeatedly using one fungicide
Can increase resistance risk.

Mistake 7 — Treating symptoms without understanding the disease cycle
This often produces poor long-term control.

50. IDM vs IPM
These concepts are closely related.
IDM
IPM
Focuses specifically on plant diseases Covers pests broadly
Fungi, bacteria, viruses, nematodes, etc. Insects, mites, weeds, diseases and other pests
Uses disease triangle/cycle
Uses pest biology, thresholds and ecosystem principles
Disease incidence/severity important
Pest density/damage/thresholds important
Disease forecasting important
Pest monitoring/ETL/EIL important
Conceptually:

IDM⊆Broad Integrated Pest Management Framework 
in systems where disease is one component of pest management.

51. IDM and the Disease Triangle — Complete
Integration
Host
Resistant variety→Susceptibility↓Resistant\ variety  Susceptibility Pathogen
Sanitation+Seed treatment+Rotation→Inoculum↓Sanitation+Seed\ treatment+Rotation  Inoculum Environment
Spacing+Irrigation+Drainage→Disease−favorable conditions↓Spacing+Irrigation+Drainage
 Disease-favorable\ conditions Time
Monitoring+Forecasting+Timely action→Disease progression↓Monitoring+Forecasting+Timely\
action  Disease\ progression Therefore:
IDM attacks Host+Pathogen+Environment+Time 

52. Complete IDM Workflow
1. PREVENT 
↓
2. IDENTIFY 
↓
3. MONITOR 
↓
4. ASSESS RISK 
↓

5. DECIDE 
↓
6. INTEGRATE CONTROL METHODS 
↓
7. EVALUATE 
↓
8. RE−MONITOR 
This cycle continues throughout the crop season.

53. Master IDM Formula
A useful conceptual representation is:
IDM=Genetic+Cultural+Sanitation+Biological+Physical+Chemical+Regulatory+Monitoring 
with the exact combination depending on the crop-pathogen system.

54. The Disease-Cycle View of IDM
Pathogen stage
IDM intervention
Survival
Rotation, sanitation, residue management
Inoculum formation Resistant varieties, sanitation
Dissemination
Vector control, sanitation, barriers
Inoculation
Protective treatment
Penetration
Host resistance, protection
Infection
Biological/chemical/genetic control
Colonization
Host resistance and appropriate treatment
Reproduction
Chemical/biological suppression
Secondary spread Monitoring, sanitation, vector management
This is one of the most useful ways to understand IDM.

55. The Golden Rule of IDM
Prevent the pathogen from entering, survive less, spread less, infect less, reproduce less, and cause less
damage.   

Every IDM method should contribute to one or more of these objectives.

56. Exam-Ready Short Answer
Integrated Disease Management (IDM) is an ecosystem-based and decision-oriented
approach that combines compatible genetic, cultural, biological, physical, chemical,
regulatory and other methods to prevent or suppress plant diseases economically and
sustainably. It begins with prevention and correct diagnosis, followed by monitoring
and disease-risk assessment. Management may include resistant varieties, healthy
planting material, seed treatment, sanitation, crop rotation, irrigation and drainage
management, biological control, vector management, quarantine and appropriate
fungicides or bactericides. Chemical control is used when justified and should be
integrated with nonchemical methods to reduce unnecessary pesticide use and delay
resistance. IDM therefore focuses on disrupting the disease triangle and disease cycle
at multiple stages rather than depending on a single control method.

57. Final One-Read Summary
What is IDM?
IDM=Integrated use of multiple compatible methods to keep plant diseases economically manageable.
   

Core sequence
PREVENT→IDENTIFY→MONITOR→ASSESS→DECIDE→MANAGE→RE−MONITOR 

Main tools
Resistant Varieties+Healthy Seed+Seed Treatment+Sanitation+Cultural Control+Crop Rotation+Biolog
ical Control+Physical Control+Chemical Control+Quarantine+Vector Management 

Disease triangle
Host+Pathogen+Environment→Disease 

Disease cycle
Survival→Dissemination→Inoculation→Penetration→Infection→Colonization→Reproduction→Spre
ad 

Most important concept
IDM does not mean using every control method. It means selecting and integrating the most appropriat
e compatible methods at the right time.   

Final memory line
PREVENT + DIAGNOSE + MONITOR + INTEGRATE + EVALUATE = IDM
